#5764. For workplace culture champions, Jensen Huang's return is 'unfathomable'

Nvidia CEO Jensen Huang speaks to media after a roundtable with South Korean AI and robotics startups on June 8, 2026, in Seoul, South Korea. Chung Sung-Jun/Getty Images AsiaPac hide caption

Sign up for NPR's Up First morning newsletter for the stories you need to start your day.

On Monday morning, a couple hours after Nvidia's board announced it had reinstated co-founder Jensen Huang as CEO, President Trump shared an AI-generated image on social media.

It's based on that striking photo of the president from 2024, just after he survived an assasination attempt. Surrounded by Secret Service agents, his face bloodied, Trump hoists a defiant fist in the air.

In the doctored photo, instead of Trump, it's Huang. An Nvidia flag flies behind him. And in his hoisted fist, he holds a red bra.

"I was absolutely flabbergasted," says Bo Young Lee, who worked as chief diversity officer at Uber for five years starting in 2018.

To Lee, it seems Trump is suggesting that the Nvidia CEO is as important to the country as he is.

"But then also the red bra is an indication that it's open hunting season for women," she says. "It stirs up all sorts of stereotypes and misogynistic perspectives about women."

To others, the image drives home the idea that in Silicon Valley, as long as you're successful, anything goes.

The White House has not responded to NPR's questions about what Trump was trying to say with the image.

Even as Huang's return to Nvidia has been cheered by investors, some employees and Trump, it has sparked shock and disbelief among others, including those who have long worked to improve workplace culture in Silicon Valley.

"It's actually unfathomable to me," says Lee, who now serves as an adviser to AI4ALL, a nonprofit dedicated to increasing diversity, inclusion, and responsible practices in artificial intelligence.

After The New York Times revealed that Huang had engaged in an extramarital affair with a woman the U.S government believes is a Chinese intelligence asset, Lee watched as the broader conversation was dominated by concerns about national security, corporate governance and Nvidia's stock price.

That scandal overshadowed allegations of other problematic behavior in the workplace, specifically Huang's alleged affair with Kimberly Powell, Nvidia's vice president of healthcare, one of Huang's many direct reports.

"Everyone is minimizing that alleged relationship there because of who she is," says Lee.

The fact that Powell is herself a senior leader who makes a lot of money shouldn't matter, Lee says. She argues that people in subordinate roles, even powerful ones, can't properly consent, "simply for the fact that there is this power differential."

Nvidia's board did find that Huang's behavior was "inconsistent" with the company's code of conduct. By Monday morning, that didn't seem to matter.

"The Board of Directors voted unanimously to reinstate Mr. Huang following a series of discussions and meetings with Mr. Huang, after which the Board was satisfied with respect to Mr. Huang's candor and ability to lead Nvidia," the board said in a statement.

Lee says that response sends a message to Nvidia employees and all of Silicon Valley that it's OK to break the rules as long as you come clean.

"'Just be transparent with us,'" she imagines the board saying.

Nvidia did not respond to NPR's questions about Lee's concerns.

In Silicon Valley, there have been private conversations about Huang's alleged workplace transgressions and the power dynamics at play, but women don't appear eager to speak publicly.

NPR reached out to two dozen women who work in the tech space. Freada Kapor Klein was one of the few willing to talk. The social impact investor says conversations about workplace behavior are much quieter than they have been in other eras, such as in 2017 and 2018, when a number of venture capitalists resigned amid allegations they were sexually harassing female entrepreneurs.

In 2017, Kapor Klein and her husband, Mitch Kapor, used their positions as early investors in Uber to publicly call out the company over its toxic culture, one they said tolerated bullying and harassment. The co-founder and CEO Travis Kalanick ended up resigning. Kapor Klein hoped it'd be a teachable moment.

But now, she feels Silicon Valley has gone backwards.

"Tech bro culture is stronger now than I have seen it in decades," she says, adding that Trump's attacks on DEI have amplified that culture.

Among women and people of color in Silicon Valley, she says there's now real trepidation about the anti-DEI sentiment — the axing of programs, chief diversity officers and employee resource groups.

"All of the supports or mechanisms have been pretty much stripped away," she says.

Screens display news of the reinstatement of Jensen Huang, seen outside the NASDAQ MarketSite, in New York, on November 23, 2026. Angela Weiss/AFP via Getty Images hide caption

To be clear, Huang has not been accused of predatory behavior. Still, Kapor Klein says his swift comeback is troubling.

"It is a resounding message that all that matters is money," she says. "If you are thought of as a singular talent, you can violate corporate policy, you can possibly threaten national security, you can make up your own rules as you go, you can create toxic workplace culture — as long as you bring in the money."

By the end of last week, Nvidia's stock had fallen almost 49% from its peak in October, and about 27% from before Huang's resignation. On Monday, it rebounded significantly.

Lee says Silicon Valley has always had an unhealthy relationship with its "genius founders."

"We elevate these leaders to god-like levels, and that's been done to Mark Zuckerberg, and that's being done to Sam Altman, and that's definitely [been] done to Jensen Huang," says Lee. "You become untouchable at that point."

Kapor Klein says what's unfolded at Nvidia has made abundantly clear that AI companies need guardrails for their CEOs, not just for the tools they're creating.

"We see a board that ignored that," she says. "There are no guardrails on this CEO's behavior."

NPR's Scott Horsley contributed to this story.

#5756. Solar lease holdouts still see value in privately-owned residential projects

The One Big Beautiful Bill Act drastically reduced the lifespan of federal subsidies available to certain segments of the solar market, and among the first to go was the investment tax credit for residential projects. The ITC is a historic renewable energy subsidy that provides a 30% tax credit against the total cost of an eligible project, and it is currently available to the commercial and utility-scale solar segments.

A residential solar project in New York installed by Nickels Energy Solutions. Credit: Nickels Energy Solutions

After 2025, homeowners hoping to install solar on their house couldn’t subsidize the project’s cost with this federal subsidy if it was privately owned. However, a loophole in the tax credit language has allowed leased or “third party-owned” (TPO) solar projects to still receive the ITC (25D) on residential solar projects. In this scenario, it matters who owns the solar project, not necessarily where it’s built. The project can be subsidized because it is owned by a commercial company, and not the homeowner.

Some residential solar installers are still strictly pursuing and installing privately-owned solar projects. Nickels Energy Solutions (NES), a residential solar contractor working in Central New York believes a privately-owned PV system is still the better deal for the customer.

“We don’t have the same offers as some of the bigger companies,” said Kevin Nickels, VP of sales with NES. “If you can offer leases, it’s like the ‘haves and have-nots.’ I don’t have that loophole or that trick up my sleeve, so it forces me to get the project sold, while I’m as sharp as I can be with margins to make it somewhat competitive.”

TPO solar transactions are typically arranged at a lower up-front cost than a wholly owned solar project. Lease contract lengths vary by provider, and financiers have surety in these transactions because the projects will be built with products from an approved vendor list (AVL). Products on an AVL can qualify for a domestic content subsidy, another tax credit in addition to the ITC, because they are manufactured using a certain percentage of domestic components.

These are premium products vetted for AVLs. But it can also mean that installers are paying premium prices for components in building TPO solar projects.

Nickels believes that, while a privately owned residential solar project will cost more at the start, the combination of expensive components and interest rates for financing over a lease term — with factors like rate increases — can cost a customer more in the long run.

“I don’t think all leases are bad,” he said. “I just don’t think it’s what’s best for the industry or the homeowner.”

Rudy Wright, owner of Wright-Way Solar of Tyler, Texas, has shifted the company’s focus from building new projects to servicing existing systems. As the ITC exited the residential solar market, so did many of the lenders financing these projects. Without lending options, Wright-Way’s installation output has slowed down, and those clients who can afford solar are paying with cash or using a home equity loan to cover the cost.

Despite that, Wright said he isn’t very interested in building leased solar projects.

Wright-Way Solar built a solar plus storage project at a Ranch in Texas featuring arrays that are facing east, west and south. Credit: Wright-Way Solar

“I do think it cost me some of those entry level clients, but every time I analyzed the financing end of it, I felt like I was benefitting my company and the industry more than the customer,” Wright said. “I haven’t seen the angle where bringing a pre-purchase agreement or bringing a lease agreement has enough ironclad return for the customer.”

Tyler is a unique market for solar because many of Wright-Way’s customers are trying to make their properties more resilient, and less reliant on the grid, by installing solar and storage, and not just seeking cost savings for their energy use. Wright has installed solar plus storage systems on Texan ranches at a scale close a small commercial project.

That promise of resilience is still enough reason for some people to commission a solar project in Texas, where the state’s independent electrical grid operator has faltered and experienced dangerous outages in recent years.

“Folks realize money in the bank doesn’t do you any good if the power goes out,” Wright said.

NES is still building a steady pipeline of residential solar projects in New York. Nickels said that has been possible in part because many of its clients are upper-middle class; but New York also maintains an incentive program for solar projects, NY-Sun, which includes a 25% state tax credit for residential systems.

“The OBBBA caught a lot of states flat-footed,” Nickels said. “Without federal support, it puts a lot of pressure on what the state is offering.”

Some states’ solar subsidy programs have stepped in where federal support has diminished since the OBBBA was passed. The North Carolina Clean Energy Technology Center maintains the Database of State Incentives for Renewables & Efficiency, which analyzes which states currently have policies and incentives supporting clean energy developments. Every state has policy enabling solar projects, but not necessarily cost incentives for individual projects.

While traditional residential solar projects have lost a significant subsidy, the reality is that energy costs have risen in response to new grid demands from hyperscale data centers and oil scarcity from the United States’ war in Iran. Sure, a privately owned residential solar project won’t receive the ITC, but it can still stabilize energy costs without them.

“We lost a big incentive, but the rate increase is proportional,” Nickels said.

Billy Ludt is managing editor of Solar Power World and currently covers topics on mounting, inverters, installation and operations.

#5741. Three Routes to VPP Revenue for Installers, EPCs, and Developers Utility programs, wholesale markets, and bilateral contracts each come with different eligibility rules, payment structures, and commercial responsibilities.

By Intersolar & Energy Storage North America

In June 2026, Google and Voltus announced a three-year agreement to unlock up to 100 megawatts (MW) of distributed capacity annually in PJM. Under Voltus’s Bring Your Own Capacity model, Google funds procurement while Voltus aggregates qualifying distributed energy resources (DERs) and compensates participating customers.1

The deal is one of three distinct paths to earn revenue from DERs. Across the U.S., utility programs, wholesale electricity markets, and bilateral contracts each have their own eligibility requirements, payment structures and commercial responsibilities.

Utility programs offer an established pathway for compensating customers and asset owners who reduce demand, shift consumption or discharge stored energy during periods of grid need. Payments may be tied to enrollment, committed capacity, measured performance or a combination of these factors. Massachusetts, for example, offers residential battery customers $275 per kW of average summer performance through ConnectedSolutions2.

Beyond customer incentive programs, utilities also procure distributed flexibility through contracts to address local grid constraints or broader capacity needs. Unlike standardized incentives, these arrangements can establish specific delivery requirements and compensation terms, creating opportunities for developers and service providers to assemble and manage DER portfolios. During a July 2026 heat event, Green Mountain Power dispatched 90 MW of stored energy capacity, helping customers avoid an estimated $6 million in peak-related costs during one peak hour3.

Aggregated DERs can earn revenue from energy, capacity, and ancillary services in wholesale electricity markets, subject to regional market rules and resource qualification. Unlike utility-specific programs, wholesale markets can provide access to broader regional revenue opportunities and multiple grid services. For developers and installers, participation typically occurs through an aggregator or other qualified market participant rather than through direct market registration.

While Federal Energy Regulatory Commission (FERC) Order 2222 established a broader framework for DER participation, uneven implementation across regional markets continues to constrain commercial opportunities for aggregated resources. Market access does not automatically translate into revenue, as individual resources must still meet qualification requirements and comply with market-specific rules. New York Independent System Operator (NYISO), for example, requires DER aggregations to meet minimum capacity and real-time telemetry requirements, which can increase participation costs and limit the economic viability of smaller resources.

Wholesale participation can also expose VPP portfolios to fluctuating market prices, performance obligations and restrictions on receiving compensation for overlapping services. In Texas, ERCOT’s Aggregate Distributed Energy Resource (ADER) pilot allows aggregated DERs to participate in wholesale energy and ancillary services markets, subject to qualification, telemetry, and dispatch requirements.

Bilateral contracts allow utilities and large electricity consumers to procure distributed flexibility directly from aggregators and service providers. Growing interest from data center operators and other large energy buyers is expanding the potential market for VPP capacity beyond traditional utility programs. Unlike standardized programs, bilateral agreements can be tailored to a buyer’s specific needs, with negotiated terms for capacity, delivery periods, compensation, and performance obligations.

The appropriate route depends on existing customer relationships, market access, and the responsibilities each partner assumes. Developers and EPCs working on commercial storage projects may find opportunities through utility procurement or capacity contracts, while residential installers with established battery customer bases may be well positioned to incorporate VPP enrollment into utility programs. Wholesale markets can expand revenue opportunities through aggregation partners, and bilateral contracts can create opportunities to develop resources around specific buyer needs.

Sponsored Content by Intersolar & Energy Storage North America

#5726. ES Foundry ships more than 1 GW of U.S.-manufactured solar cells

ES Foundry, a U.S. manufacturer of high-efficiency solar cells, is at RE+ 2026 this week to showcase its American-made solar cells and announce a milestone in its operating capacity.

According to the company, it is the first and only crystalline silicon solar cell manufacturer to ship more than 1 gigawatt (GW) of domestically manufactured solar cells in the United States.

As one of the few U.S. solar cell manufacturers with a fully operational facility producing and shipping cells to customers, ES Foundry is helping move American solar manufacturing to commercial reality.

In July, the company announced it reached its full 3-GW manufacturing capacity. The company began production on its first 1-GW line last year and has now completed its 2-GW expansion line.

Solar Power World attended the opening of its Greenwood, South Carolina facility last year, reporting on its manufacturing outfit and its unique approach to supply deals that include working with large-scale project developers and utilities to guarantee domestic content supplies.

At RE+, attendees can see its U.S.-manufactured high-efficiency monocrystalline bifacial PERC solar cells and meet with company representatives to discuss domestic sourcing, manufacturing capabilities, and the evolving needs of the U.S. solar market.

“There’s an important difference between talking about building a domestic solar supply chain and actually making it happen,” said ES Foundry CEO Alex Zhu in a statement. “At ES Foundry, we’re manufacturing at commercial scale, shipping cells to customers and navigating the realities of U.S. production every day. That experience gives us a unique perspective on what it will take to build a competitive, sustainable American solar industry. Success requires more than manufacturing capacity. It requires the right economics, technology investments, customer demand and policies to support long-term growth. We’re proud to be delivering on the promise of American manufacturing today while helping shape the industry’s future.”

Visit ES Foundry at RE+ 2026: Booth C7434, November 16–19, Las Vegas, Nevada.

#5724. The U.S. government is investigating Iranian ties to as many as 50 hacked commercial vessels

In an aerial view, a container ship arrives at the Port of Oakland on Oct. 10, 2025 in Oakland, Calif. Justin Sullivan/Getty Images hide caption

The U.S. government has intelligence suggesting that up to 50 commercial shipping vessels were recently compromised by cyberattacks, activity federal officials are investigating as potentially linked to Iran.

That's according to a U.S. official familiar with the matter who was not authorized to speak on the record, a former military cyber operator and an industry executive briefed on the attacks. The series of breaches was first reported by the The Wall Street Journal.

The attacks against the maritime sector, if officials' suspicions are correct, may be the latest example of Iranian cyberattacks against global critical infrastructure during the ongoing war with the United States.

NPR has reported that cybersecurity experts and U.S. officials believe Iranian hackers were responsible for a series of digital attacks against U.S. water treatment systems across the country in August as well as a pattern of attacks targeting refrigeration cooling systems that led to spoiled food at grocery stores on U.S. military bases and all across Europe as recently as early November.

The Coast Guard and the FBI first partnered in August to send a "highly specialized team" to board two different foreign commercial vessels "in the Gulf of America" heading to U.S. ports, according to a statement provided by the FBI and the Coast Guard. "The captain, crew, and shore-side corporate staff were critical partners in helping to ensure the threats were mitigated," according to the statement.

The FBI and Coast Guard press offices also told NPR that there are no current "reports of operational disruptions, vessel instability, physical danger to crews, or environmental impacts."

However, there are dozens more ships in international waters that the U.S. government believes have been compromised by hackers and many others who might be vulnerable to similar attacks.

According to the U.S. official familiar with the investigation, bad actors took advantage of known vulnerabilities in Fortinet FortiGate devices, which are frequently used in the maritime industry. The Coast Guard and FBI released a cyber bulletin in September warning the industry that bad actors were breaking into these devices, which help manage firewalls and virtual private networks. "Threat actors routinely scan for specific, unpatched vulnerabilities to gain a foothold," the bulletin read.

According to the U.S. official, in some cases, employees aboard the ships suspected something nefarious was going on when they noticed email disruptions or couldn't log in to their accounts, or, in at least one case, an extended communications blackout. The U.S. official declined to comment on how the U.S. government knows about the extent of breaches across the sector.

However, according to the U.S. official, hackers were able to break in using the Fortinet vulnerability and then pivot from the ships' IT systems over to their network of devices controlling physical processes on the ships, what's called operational technology.

The U.S. official said the bad actors "could have impacted the systems on board that control propulsion, steering, navigation, and cargo management," though they did not share any examples of that kind of activity occurring. In at least one case, one of the ships affected was an LNG tanker designed to transport liquified natural gas. It's important to keep systems at a certain temperature to prevent dangerous accidents if a ship's cargo includes explosive materials –from chemicals to fuel.

While commercial vessels are accustomed to physical security reviews entering the United States following the Sept. 11 terrorist attacks, digital threats are becoming all the more important to track as adversaries can break in and cause damage from afar. "It's a totally new paradigm that these tankers are networked in this way," the U.S. official said.

Even so, national security officials have feared something like this might happen for years. A former Navy official who requested anonymity to discuss internal operations tells NPR that "we had been tracking the possibility to exploit and take control of [operational technology] systems on Navy ships and Merchant [Marine] ships for a long time, and now this is coming to fruition."

While the U.S. government is wary of advertising Iranian hacking efforts during the war, officials also want to spread awareness of the vulnerability of internet connected devices, particularly those that are linked to the technology that controls critical processes. "These are not sophisticated attacks," the official said. "This is a known vulnerability that could be mitigated with some pretty basic cyber hygiene."

"Modern ships are highly integrated systems. A cyberattack presents significant risks, including the potential for a collision, grounding, oil spill, or even an explosion. The Coast Guard regularly publishes bulletins on cyber vulnerabilities and actions vessel owners and operators can take to secure their networks," according to a statement from the Coast Guard.

Ahead of the winter and a busy holiday season for commercial shipping, officials are bracing themselves to investigate any ship entering U.S. ports that could be compromised.

"People never think about what a modern blessing electricity is until it goes out. People take the marine transportation system for granted until there's a problem and their stuff doesn't get there by Christmas," the U.S. official said. "I wish more Americans knew how complex this system is."

#5716. enSights brings AI-powered business management to solar and storage operations

By Marie DeFreitas, Contributor | November 16, 2026

As solar and energy storage portfolios grow in both size and complexity, managing performance is just one part of the equation. Owners and operators must also keep track of power purchase agreements (PPAs), warranties, service contracts, billing, compliance requirements and project-level profitability.

enSights is looking to bring those responsibilities together with the launch of its AI Studio, a new addition to its Energy Business Management (EBM) platform. The company announced the launch Nov. 10.

The AI Studio is designed to give solar and storage owners, operators, and asset managers access to a customizable team of AI specialists that can support business and operational workflows. Through no-code tools, users can create AI dashboards and reports using natural-language instructions, while still establishing their own workflows, routines, data-access permissions and approval requirements.

The approach here reflects a broader shift toward renewable energy software, it’s moving beyond monitoring individual assets toward managing the financial and operational performance of entire portfolios.

According to enSights, its EBM platform brings together energy management systems (EMS), production data, billing, PPA information, operations and maintenance, contractual obligations and compliance on a unified data foundation. That integration is central to the company’s AI strategy.

Rather than limiting AI applications to identifying equipment issues or answering generation-related questions, the platform is designed to connect energy production with the contracts and financial obligations associated with each project.

The AI Studio includes an orchestrator that coordinates specialized AI agents focused on areas like monitoring, operations, revenue, warranties, permits, lenders, insurers, and asset management. For portfolio managers, those capabilities could help reduce the time spent reviewing disconnected systems and manually reconciling information across departments. Automated reviews of contractual obligations, revenue performance and operational alerts could also help teams identify issues earlier and direct attention to matters that still require human intervention.

The enSights AI Studio dashboard provides solar and energy storage operators with AI-powered tools for asset management, operational workflows, and financial oversight. Credit: enSights

enSights emphasized that people remain in control of the AI Team. Users determine which workflows the agents handle and where approvals are required. Physical asset controls remain governed by established EMS and site-level systems.

The AI Studio also builds on an expanded financial management offering within the EBM platform. Its Financial Asset Management Module includes revenue assurance, contract, and obligation management, and profit-and-loss reporting.

The module is intended to provide project-level financial visibility, helping companies identify and prevent revenue losses while generating reports for investors.

The platform also includes real-time EMS, weather and tariff forecasting, along with dispatch planning intended to optimize revenue while maintaining grid safety and local compliance.

enSights says its platform is deployed globally across more than 100,000 sites, representing over 5 GW of assets. The company plans to demonstrate the AI Studio and its broader EBM platform at RE+ 2026.

As AI gains traction across the clean energy industry, the next competitive advantage could come from connecting operational intelligence with the financial and contractual decisions that determine how successfully renewable energy portfolios perform.

#5713. Republican wants FBI and CIA answers before questioning Nvidia chief

Rep. Brian Fitzpatrick, R-Pa., speaks at a House Foreign Affairs subcommittee hearing in Washington on Sept. 21, 2022. Samuel Corum/Getty Images hide caption

Jensen Huang is back at the helm of Nvidia with President Trump's backing. But the questions surrounding the AI chipmaker and its ties to China are far from settled.

U.S. intelligence officials told congressional staff there is no evidence that Alice Huang, the woman who had an affair with the Nvidia chief, was a Chinese spy or intelligence asset, according to a person familiar with the matter who was granted anonymity to speak about a closed-door briefing.

There is also no evidence that she initiated the affair to collect intelligence for China's government, the person told NPR's Ryan Lucas on condition of anonymity because of the sensitive nature of the matter. She is, however, believed to have passed along information after the affair began.

Trump has said he helped arrange Huang's return as CEO, and Nvidia's board maintains that his reinstatement presents no national security risk. Some Republican lawmakers, however, want to understand how those conclusions were reached.

Speaking to Morning Edition on Wednesday, before NPR obtained the new information, Rep. Brian Fitzpatrick, R-Pa., said he wants to hear from the FBI and CIA before Huang appears before House lawmakers next week.

For Fitzpatrick, a former FBI special agent who sits on the House Intelligence Committee, the inquiry begins with what federal authorities knew and whether they warned the Nvidia chief of potential espionage threats.

"What briefings did they give? When did they give them?" he asked.

Huang's reinstatement, Fitzpatrick said, should not put those questions to rest.

"The public is rightly concerned, and they deserve answers," he said.

Fitzpatrick also wants to hear from Attorney General Todd Blanche, who on Tuesday described Nvidia as a good corporate citizen, and Commerce Secretary Howard Lutnick, whose department faces questions over Nvidia's chip exports to China.

"Anytime you do an investigation, you follow the facts wherever they lead, and you report those facts with unimpeachable integrity," Fitzpatrick said. "You leave no stone unturned."

The debate over Huang's reinstatement extends beyond Washington.

In Silicon Valley, his return has alarmed women and advocates who have spent years pushing for a better workplace culture, renewing questions about the limits of executive accountability.

#5710. Group of Senate Democrats push to add freshman to leadership

U.S. Senate Majority Leader Charles Schumer (D-NY), center, is seen here meeting with newly elected Democratic Senators, (L-R) Senator-elect Andy Kim (D-NJ), Senator-elect Ruben Gallego (D-AZ), Senator-elect Angela Alsobrooks (D-MD) Senator-elect Adam Schiff (D-CA) Senator-elect Lisa Blunt Rochester (D-DE) and Senator-elect Elissa Slotkin (D-MI) at the U.S. Capitol Building on November 12, 2024. That group of senators is pushing to add an incoming freshman senator to the leadership ranks. Chip Somodevilla/Getty Images/Getty Images North America hide caption

A group of Senate Democrats elected in 2024 sent a letter to colleagues Tuesday proposing to add an incoming freshman to the leadership ranks.

"The Freshman Representative to Leadership would be elected by members of the incoming freshman class and confirmed by the Conference," the letter said. "We intend to request a vote on this proposal."

The letter, first reported by Politico, is signed by California Sen. Adam Schiff, Maryland Sen. Angela Alsobrooks, Michigan Sen. Elissa Slotkin, Arizona Sen. Ruben Gallego, Delaware Sen. Lisa Blunt Rochester, and New Jersey Sen. Andy Kim.

The letter nods to the diverse class of incoming freshmen who helped catapult the party to controlling both chambers of Congress.

"These freshmen have their fingers on the pulse of long-time Democratic voters, those who voted for Democrats for the first time this past Election Day, and everyone in-between," the letter reads. "The deserve a seat at the table."

Senate Democrats have been signaling a desire to uplift new voices in the party.

"On election night, people voted very clearly for a new generation of leadership. That was very, very clear," Slotkin said during remarks at a conference of Democrats after the midterms.

New Jersey Sen. Cory Booker told MS NOW that leadership extends beyond the top post.

"This is a time for new leadership," Booker said, mentioning wanting to see more of incoming Sens. Annie Andrews of South Carolina and James Talarico of Texas. "I want to see them out front, I want to see them taking issues, I want to see them running on the field."

The proposed rule change comes ahead of the leadership election next week. Maryland Sen. Chris Van Hollen is challenging Senate Minority Leader Chuck Schumer, D-N.Y., for the majority leader.

Senate leadership is comprised not only of the top position of Majority leader but of additional conference officers. Both Van Hollen and Schumer support the idea of adding a position for a freshman to that leadership group.

"Leader Schumer was already planning to do this and is incredibly supportive of the idea," a spokesperson told NPR.

On Tuesday, Van Hollen posted to social media: "There should be a freshman senator on the Senate leadership team. As I've said, we need a new way to organize so we can deliver for the American people."

#5707. U.S. Judge rules FCC violated ABC's free speech rights to please Trump

Under Chairman Brendan Carr, left, the Federal Communications Commission launched an early review of Disney's licenses to operate all the local ABC stations it owns. New Disney CEO Josh D'Amaro, right, says the company is fighting back to protect ABC's journalistic integrity and free speech. "We're very principled on this," he said. Heather Diehl/Thibault Camus/Pool/AFP via Getty Images hide caption

A federal judge has blocked President Trump and his administration from taking action to challenge the ABC television network's broadcast licenses, finding it to be an unconstitutional punishment for satire, commentary and news coverage that Trump doesn't like.

In her preliminary injunction filed Wednesday, U.S. District Court Judge Loren AliKhan found that the federal agency's early reviews represented retaliation for speech the Trump administration resented.

Such perceived offenses included late-night host Jimmy Kimmel's jabs at the president, the critical remarks of hosts on the chat show The View, and the moderation of a presidential debate in September 2024.

ABC and its corporate parent, the Walt Disney Co., serve six of the 10 largest markets in the U.S., including New York, Los Angeles, and Houston. They contribute significantly to the network's bottom line.

"Given the extreme, indeed existential, nature of the possible penalty Plaintiffs face -- loss of their broadcast licenses -- the court concludes that early license renewal proceedings create a risk sufficient to chill a broadcaster of ordinary firmness," Judge AliKhan wrote.

She rejected the Trump administration's motion to dismiss the lawsuit from ABC and Disney challenging the review of the local licenses. Usually the licenses last eight years and are up for review on a staggered basis. They are rarely taken away.

In her ruling, AliKhan relied heavily on public statements and social media postings in which Trump attacked ABC and Disney, as well as posts by Trump's pick as the chair of the Federal Communications Commission, Brendan Carr.

"What started in 2020 as questions about how ABC 'can keep getting a license' if it 'report[s] fake news,' ... has crescendoed into repeated calls for the Commission to strip ABC's broadcast licenses for their editorial decisions," she wrote. "The court counts eleven such calls made by President Trump over the past two years -- the most recent being in July 2026."

And the judge cited the administration's "tangible steps toward enforcing President Trump and Commissioner Carr's repeated threats to strip the Plaintiff Stations of their licenses."

"Chairman Carr has taken President Trump's rhetoric as his marching orders," Judge AliKhan wrote.

Carr did not respond to NPR's request for comment Wednesday evening.

ABC hailed the ruling in a statement as "an important victory for the First Amendment."

Among the targets of ire from Trump and Carr was ABC's decision not to broadcast Trump's July 2026 Oval Office address on its television network. ABC's legal team said the network streamed it on its digital news service, but would not have done so absent the political pressure from the FCC.

It is one of several examples of newsroom decisions made under duress, the network's lawyers said in court. Kimmel's interview with then-Democratic U.S. Senate nominee James Talarico of Texas was uploaded to YouTube instead of being aired on ABC's late night television show. (Talarico won election to the U.S. Senate earlier this month.) And The View stopped hosting candidates for political office in 2026 in the months ahead of the fall elections.

Judge AliKhan rejected arguments from the FCC that the court could not take action because it has not yet rejected the license renewals. The very act of premature review for retaliatory reasons is unconstitutional and unlawful – and hard to reverse, she wrote.

"For nearly two years, the agency has weaponized its licensing authority to retaliate against broadcasters for exercising their right to free expression, and now a federal court has stepped in to put a check on that conduct," said Anna M. Gomez, the lone Democratic commissioner on the FCC. "Every broadcaster facing this pressure should take note and choose courage over capitulation because, as I've said from the beginning, the First Amendment is on their side."

This ruling comes six weeks after a parallel ruling in a case brought by CNN, MS NOW and Politico against the Trump administration for restricting access. Officials barred journalists from the three news outlets from the White House after Trump lambasted them for "purposefully negative" news coverage.

In that case, U.S. District Court Judge Timothy Kelly issued a preliminary injunction, writing, "It is not hard to conclude that President Trump likely banned Plaintiffs from the White House because he believes that their reporting is generally inaccurate and biased against him." That, he wrote, represents a form of unlawful retaliation. The administration is appealing. And despite the ruling, the White House continues to limit the three news outlets' access to the president.

In December 2024, just a few weeks before Trump became president for the second time, Disney agreed to settle a lawsuit Trump had brought against ABC News by paying $16 million, including legal fees. Many outside legal experts believed Disney would have had the upper hand if it had stuck it out in court.

Now under new leadership, Disney has drawn the line at what it contends is a threat to its broadcast licenses — and profits — over its editorial independence.

"We're very principled on this," Disney CEO Josh D'Amaro told CNBC in August, just days before the company filed its lawsuit. "We're going to stand up to what we believe is journalistic integrity, and we're not going to be told how to run that side of our business." D'Amaro became chief executive in March.

The FCC had already kicked off an investigation of Disney's diversity practices when it announced the review.

Carr, the agency's chief, has said it initiated the review of Disney's broadcast licenses over concerns that the company engaged in racial discrimination through its diversity, equity and inclusion actions.

"Obviously, the Disney lawsuit is without merit," Carr told NPR in August. "From our perspective, we're going to continue to follow the facts and the law wherever they may lead."

Judge AliKhan wrote in her order that Disney had been responsive to every query that the FCC had made in the diversity investigation. Carr said in a statement to a congresswoman that he found the responses to be "deficient, nonresponsive, and disingenuous."

The FCC generally reviews broadcast licenses once every eight years. The decision to examine Disney's licenses now is remarkable in two ways: Reviews of a company's licenses are usually staggered, rather than all at once. Additionally, some of Disney's licenses are not due for renewal for another several years. It is considered an essentially unprecedented act for the FCC to take.

In a court hearing in October, Disney's legal team argued it was suffering irreparable harm to its First Amendment freedoms as a result of the swift move to review the licenses.

Lead Disney attorney Beth Wilkinson compared the reviews to a "guillotine blade" hanging above the company, ready to fall at any moment. (Judge AliKhan thanked her for not comparing it to the Sword of Damocles of Greek legend.)

U.S. Justice Department attorney Dimitar Georgiev-Remmel shrugged off that claim. But his arguments led to a series of telling and dramatic exchanges with the judge.

Georgiev-Remmel contended that the review was a useful "tool" the FCC was deploying to secure more complete information for the diversity investigation. He said the agency was having trouble eliciting necessary information from Disney.

Disney's Wilkinson seized on the claim, asserting it proved that the license review was unnecessary. And she noted that the FCC only informed Disney that it was dissatisfied with the information the company had provided about its diversity practices 17 days after the reviews had been announced. That gave the company little time to stave off the nearly unprecedented process by fulfilling the requests.

Judge AliKhan asked Georgiev-Remmel what other tools the agency had at its disposal to get that information.

The Justice Department trial lawyers representing the FCC appeared to be caught off guard. Georgiev-Remmel initially said he wasn't sure.

Some minutes later, after conferring with colleagues, Georgiev-Remmel said the FCC could have subpoenaed the information, fined Disney, or taken other measures. But he said the agency could decide to set the reviews aside should Disney provide sufficient information.

The judge appeared immediately skeptical that the instant license reviews were a necessary or even reasonable step to achieve that.

In both Disney's lawsuit against the FCC and CNN, MS NOW and Politico's suit against the White House, the media companies argued that the Trump administration had violated their constitutional rights of free speech and due process. The Justice Department argued other priorities and prerogatives were in play: national security in one, the need to pursue an investigation into diversity practices in the other.

And in both, the judges expressed incredulity or skepticism that the Trump administration had acted in good faith.

As in matters beyond the realm of media, the Trump administration is pressing to expand its authority and powers. The White House access case would effectively require a reversal of decades of precedent on closely related cases.

In the case of ABC, the Trump administration wants the ability to trip up broadcasters who put things on the air that offend the president – simply because that technology falls under government regulation.

Even during all this litigation, the Justice Department initiated an anti-trust investigation into the five networks – including ABC and CNN – that make up the White House video pool sharing footage and duties to other outlets. The networks have been collaborating since their inception.

What caught the attention of the administration? The fact that the networks consulted before deciding not to substitute another channel in for CNN -- on the increasing number of days the president's aides blocked it from serving as the pool representative covering Trump's public appearances in small spaces that cannot accommodate the large contingent of reporters that cover the White House.

#5706. Soneil Spark introduces its Mobile Battery Energy Storage System

This week, Soneil Spark rolls out its Mobile Battery Energy Storage System (Mobile BESS), the portable, modular battery storage built into a towable trailer.

The company, offering bespoke, modular energy solutions, is introducing Mobile BESS at RE+ 2026. It delivers temporary backup and off-grid power, working with what’s available by taking power from the grid, a generator, or solar.

“Wherever your business goes, the power goes with it. Simply tow it where energy demand grows,” said Sonya Jain, CEO of Soneil Spark, in a statement. “Start with one unit and add more as your needs change.”

It’s a simple set up, the company said. Mobile BESS works with no crane, no trenching, and no utility wait. That makes it a natural fit for a job site which may be months away from permanent service, a film set or event that can’t tolerate generator noise and fumes, a utility crew restoring power after a storm, or a remote mine or farm beyond the grid’s reach.

On sites that already run generators, the Mobile BESS syncs with them to cover peaks and start-up gaps, so the generators run fewer hours. Where diesel is restricted, it offers a quiet alternative.

Where containerized storage is built to be placed once, the Mobile BESS is built to be towed and to work in any weather. It includes:

The Mobile BESS is built for generator and equipment rental, construction, emergency and backup power, utilities, electrical contractors, events, film and entertainment, and off-grid sites such as mining and agriculture. A hybrid option adds EV charging.

#5697. OMCO Solar offers solar-powered tracker control system

OMCO Solar now offers a version of its OMCO Star Tracker Control System powered entirely by solar energy, allowing tracker controls to operate without AC power at the site.

The system, first launched last year, is designed for OMCO Origin Factory-Direct Trackers. Its tracker controllers, array controllers and sensor bridges use all-weather battery packs charged by solar modules.

According to OMCO, the technology eliminates the need for AC power to tracker control components and an uninterruptible power supply. It also allows tracker controls to operate and protects modules against wind, snow and hail before AC power is available.

“Our industry has solved so many problems as we have grown – we’re now the leading source for new electric energy capacity in the US. This addition to the OMCO Star product line solves one more – how to protect modules if there’s no AC power to the site even for a short time. We’re excited to offer this solution to our existing customers and to new partners,” said Matt Kesler, vice president of solar technology at OMCO Solar, in a statement.

The OMCO Star system uses LoRa, or long-range, wireless technology with a communication range greater than 1 mile. According to OMCO, this reduces the number of network controllers required and allows direct communication with every tracker without relying on a mesh network.

The company says LoRa is 100 times less susceptible to electromagnetic noise than Zigbee and better able to connect to distant trackers despite obstacles.

OMCO says its all-weather battery packs allow the solar-powered controls to operate reliably in cold climates. The system includes field-replaceable batteries, a phone app that eliminates the need for buttons on controllers and a remote monitoring and control portal.

The portal allows operators to move trackers for activities such as mowing, snow plowing and module cleaning. The system is developed, manufactured and supported by OMCO Solar in the United States.

“We are excited about bringing this innovation to the Utility Scale and DG tracker markets. Over the past few years, OMCO has expanded its capabilities beyond just being a US steel manufacturer to now being a true technology leader in our space. We look forward to supporting our customers/partners with the OMCO Star Tracker Control System, improving reliability, and reducing the labor and materials needed to build their projects,” said Eric Goodwin, vice president of business development at OMCO Solar.

OMCO Solar will display the all-DC tracker control system at RE+ in Las Vegas, November 17–19, in the North Hall, booth N516.

The new state-of-the-art factory in Oklahoma City will create about 300 jobs, strengthen the domestic solar supply chain, and establish a new benchmark for transparency and quality.

Nextnova celebrates the inauguration of its new solar cell manufacturing facility in Oklahoma City. Credit: Nextnova

#5668. SolarEdge unveils U.S.-made optimizer for C&I solar

By Marie DeFreitas, Contributor | November 17, 2026

SolarEdge’s new Duo Power Optimizer promises to simplify commercial and industrial solar installations with 50% fewer optimizer units, independent module-level power tracking, and enhanced safety features.

The commercial and industrial (C&I) solar market is getting a new hardware option aimed at making installations simpler without sacrificing system performance. SolarEdge Technologies has unveiled its new Duo Power Optimizer, a U.S.-manufactured solution designed to deliver module-level energy harvesting and monitoring while cutting the number of power optimizers in half.

Announced November 16 ahead of RE+ 2026 in Las Vegas, the new product introduces a 2:1 architecture that allows one optimizer to connect two solar modules. Notably, each module retains its own independent maximum power point tracker (MPPT), which preserves the module-level performance benefits associated with the traditional one-to-one optimizer configurations.

For developers, EPCs and installers managing large-scale rooftop and distributed generation projects, the value here lies in fewer components to purchase, wire and install, with no trade-off in module-level optimization.

Simplifying installation without sacrificing performance

SolarEdge’s optimization tech addresses the challenge of conventional string inverter systems, which is that an underperforming module can affect the output of an entire string. But by allowing each module to operate independently, the system limits the impact of shading, degradation or performance differences between panels.

The Duo brings demonstrates that capability in a two-module configuration. By reducing the number of optimizers by 50% compared with one-to-one designs, SolarEdge is targeting an opportunity to streamline balance-of-system requirements and reduce the installation labor.

SolarEdge’s U.S.-made Duo Power Optimizer connects two solar modules while maintaining independent module-level power tracking for commercial and industrial solar installations. Credit: SolarEdge

The hardware also incorporates design changes intended to improve jobsite efficiency. Each optimizer features two input pairs: a shorter connection for the nearest module and a pre-extended connection for the adjacent module. The arrangement reduces excess wiring and eliminates the need for field-made jumpers.

The configuration also accommodates modules with different orientations, making it particularly relevant for east-west and dual-tilt racking applications.

Beyond installation efficiency, SolarEdge is emphasizing electrical safety as a central feature of the new product.

The Duo extends the company’s Sense Connect technology to its input and output connectors, monitoring connector temperatures to identify developing issues before they potentially escalate into overheating or electrical arcs.

Also, the optimizer introduces what SolarEdge describes as an industry-first approach to arc fault detection: every optimizer acts as an individual arc sensor. Rather than relying exclusively on detection at the inverter, the system distributes monitoring throughout the array, allowing potential arc faults to be identified closer to their source.

SolarEdge says the technology provides coverage across strings with up to 3,280 feet of total cable length between the inverter and optimizer. It can also help pinpoint problems to the optimizer area, potentially making troubleshooting and maintenance more efficient.

Manufactured at SolarEdge’s Florida facility, the Duo is designed to meet U.S. Domestic Content and Foreign Entity of Concern (FEOC) requirements, according to the company. SolarEdge says its domestic-content eligibility levels are consistent with those of its previous U.S.-manufactured power optimizers, subject to applicable tax guidance and project-specific requirements.

For SolarEdge, the Duo represents an effort to combine three priorities that are becoming more and more important in commercial solar: installation efficiency, system-level safety, and domestic manufacturing.

The technology does not fundamentally change the value proposition of module-level optimization. Instead, it looks to make that architecture more economical and easier to deploy at scale.

SolarEdge will showcase the Duo Power Optimizer at RE+ 2026.

#5664. EV Share Jumps to 68%(!) and Small BEVs Rise to Power — China EV Sales Report for October

High gas prices and a never-ending wave of new BEVs have allowed October to reach 68% EV market share, a new record!

This record achievement is thanks to a significant ICE (internal combustion engine) crash. The overall market dropped 27% year over year (YoY), to around 1.6 million sales. Plugins were down 14% YoY, but pure ICE sales were much worse off, crashing 44% YoY!

Adding 45% BEV share to PHEVs (23% share) meant that in October a record 68% of all cars sold in China had a plug! If this trend continues in the future, the Chinese market will be fully electrified by 2030, and will be 100% BEV before 2035!

This great result pulled the 2026 share up, to 59%, already 5% above the full year result of 2025. BEVs on their own were up to 40% (versus 33% in 2025).

At this pace, I expect the final number for 2026 EV share in China to be above 60%, with BEVs alone north of the 40% mark. And when the largest global automotive market gets this electrified … then the ICE industry is in serious trouble.

The ICE extinction is also visible in the overall ranking. The overall top 10 was fully made by EVs. Again.

Looking at the best sellers in several size categories, the ICE extinction is also quite visible. Although, once again, the C-segment/compact category is the exception, with the only ICE representative showing up there — the Toyota RAV4, in 2nd, behind the leader Nevo Q05 and just ahead of the BYD Dolphin.

Having a quick look at the five size categories, the highlight is the B-segment/subcompact category, which is the hottest segment right now, with three of the top four top sellers in October belonging to this category.

Considering that 56% of the new SUVs launched this year were bigger than 5 meters/197 inches, there is a clear disconnect between what people want (small hatchbacks/crossovers) and what OEMs offer (large SUVs).

Another highlight is the fact that Xiaomi had a gold plus silver win in the full size category, with the BYD Great Tang closing out the podium while the midsize category podium was 100% BYD Group.

Here’s more info and commentary on October’s top selling electric models:

A BYD Dolphin for BYD Seagull money ($10,000). This was Geely’s internal memo to describe the formula for the Geely Xingyuan when developing its star model. With an interesting name — Xingyuan translates as “wishing upon a star” — it seems that Geely had its wish granted. However, the Geely model will have a tough adversary soon, as the new generation Seagull will land with only one goal — to steal the Xingyuan’s best seller status. But while the new Seagull didn’t land, the small Geely scored a record performance in October, with its 50,190 registrations representing a 14% increase YoY. Besides the new BYD Seagull, Geely’s hatchback now has to deal with Leapmotor’s B-segment contenders, the A10 and A05; the new-generation Wuling Bingo; Chery’s new QQ3; and let’s not forget about BYD’s Yuan Up, too.

BYD’s midsize SUV continues to transition into the new generation. Thanks to the ramp-up of the new Ultra body, BYD’s star player was 2nd, scoring 30,772 registrations. Sales were up by 6% compared to the same month last year. The new Ultra generation features lidar and 1,500 kW DC charging, and these two features aren’t even the most impressive aspects of the model! That would be the price. It starts at 152,000 yuan (or $22,000) with the 76 kWh battery, and it goes up to 180,000 yuan (or $26,000) with the 83 kWh battery. For comparison, the Tesla Model Y starts in China at 259,000 yuan ($38,250)…. Expect the Song to experience a second youth from here onwards, and while it is impossible to displace the Tesla Model Y from the runner-up spot, the last place on the podium is doable this year. And maybe higher up in 2027?

BYD’s small crossover continues to surprise, thanks to recently improved specs. BYD’s team player rose to 3rd in October, scoring 28,514 registrations, which was a 44% increase YoY and a new record score. Despite not being the most exciting vehicle to look at, or drive, the truth is that it is surfing the wave of small, affordable EVs better than most, scoring high in value for money with prices starting at $11,000 and higher end versions having BYD’s driver-assistance system. With small vehicles currently having a moment in China, expect the small crossover to show up in the top 5 more regularly.

Things continue to go well for the startup brand, with its new baby A10 now established as the star player in an already strong lineup. Thanks to 27,247 registrations, the small crossover managed another top 5 presence. The model has the usual value-for-money focus of the brand, and a low, low price of 66,000 yuan ($10,000). On top of that, however, the A10 offers something close to a distinct personality, as the design eschews the white product standard design of Leapmotor for something more personal, mostly thanks to the front and back lights and a floating roof effect. With B-segment BEVs now being the hottest category in China, Leapmotor is well prepared to benefit from the subcompact rising tide, thanks to the recent arrival of the A10 small crossover and its sibling, the A05 small hatchback. Talk about perfect timing….

After the SU7 and YU7, Xiaomi has another success on its hands. This big fat SUV — or shall I say SUVs, as the lineup is broken down between the 7-seat N90 and the 5-seat N70 — scored 27,132 registrations in only its second month on the market, pulling it immediately into the overall top 5. Even with the number of competitors growing every passing day, Xiaomi’s new land yachts have been selling like hot cakes, while drying up sales of the competition. Proof of that, is the fact that there were only two full size models in October’s top 20, with the other being … the Xiaomi SU7.

Looking at the rest of the best seller table, there were a number of models hitting record scores, starting with the #8 Nevo Q05 (20,022 units) continuing its success story. The #10 Bestune Xiaoma (17,887 units) benefitted from a recent refresh to score a surprise record score. Could this mean that small city EVs are (finally) seeing the light at the end of the tunnel?

As for the second half of the table, the highlight is the ramp-up of the Xpeng L03, with the attractive looking crossover, which kind of reminds of a mini-Lotus or small Polestar, reaching 13th thanks to 15,653 sales. This EV looks to be the startup’s star model thanks to a competitive price (124,000 RMB) that is half the price of the Tesla Model Y!

Other models with record performances were the #14 MG 4 (15,503 units), with the new generation proving to be much more popular and adaptable to local tastes than the previous one, and the #17 Chery QQ3 EV, which continued its slow climb in the ranking thanks to 14,041 sales, which made it the 5th B-segment model in the top 20.

Still in the top 20, we should also highlight the best selling foreign model, the Tesla Model Y VW ID.Aura T6, the new China-made (and China-developed) German crossover that has allowed Volkswagen to beat the Texans for the first time in years (the Tesla Model Y was down 60% YoY, to 7,807 units, while the Model 3 was down 85%, to 1,010 units). The Aura T6, VW’s take on the Model Y formula, rose to the 20th position in its first full month on the market!

A promising sign, or just hype? Only time will tell, but at least for now, VW seems to be getting some traction on the EV side of the Chinese market, a point also highlighted by the fact that it wasn’t just the Aura T6 getting decent sales. The also new VW ID.Era 5S (think Chinese VW Jetta) got 7,100 registrations in October. Is there hope after all?

Outside the top 20, the main event was the ramp-up of Nevo’s new baby, the Q06, which is looking to replicate the Q05’s success in the category above. With some 10,000 registrations in only its second month, it looks like it could join the top 20 soon. The same can be said about the Geely Galaxy TT, the make’s answer to the Xiaomi SU7, which had around 10,500 deliveries in October. Then there’s also the BYD Yuan Plus, which, after a long cross through the desert, seems to be picking up steam again. It scored 11,658 registrations last month, a new year best.

Looking at the 2026 ranking, the podium remained the same, and unless something unexpected happens, it should stay as it is until the end of the year, with the small Geely repeating its 2025 win. That also means the Tesla Model Y will return to silver after getting bronze in 2025, and the BYD Song will get back onto the podium after a one-year absence.

Below the podium positions, though, there was some news. The BYD Yuan Up and Leapmotor A10 are surfing the B-segment wave and both climbed a position, to 5th and 6th, respectively.

If the BYD Yuan Up continues on its current record streak, then the small crossover could even aim to surpass the #4 FCB Ti7 before the year ends.

Still, with three subcompacts currently in the top 6 (Geely Xingyuan, BYD Yuan Up, and Leapmotor A10), small is hot right now, in China.

Elsewhere, the BYD Sealion 05 was up two positions, to 16th, while the Wuling Mini EV is hoping to bring tiny city cars back into the spotlight, and in October, it jumped two positions, ending the month in 13th….

Looking at the overall manufacturer ranking, it is a sea of red. Everyone is losing sales — big time. The exception is #5 Leapmotor (+31% YoY), while #8 Xiaomi (+3% YoY) and #10 Xpeng (+0%) are just hanging on.

So, now, not only are foreign OEMs suffering (Nissan was down 70% in October, Honda was down 51%), but even domestic ones are being affected by the EV shift.

Some may be asking, “But what about #9 Nevo and its 125% growth rate?” Well, it isn’t so much that they are winning sales, but they are effectively replacing the sales of their mother brand, Changan, which in October was only 19th after a 60% drop in sales YoY.

Looking at the auto brand ranking, BYD is firmly in the leadership position (17.2%), while runner-up Geely held to its 7.1% market share.

Rising 3rd placed Leapmotor (6.7%, up 0.2%) is fast approaching Geely. It wouldn’t be surprising if the startup brand became the new silver medalist in the final days of the year.

One thing is for certain though — Leapmotor will win its first medal this year. The only question is the material that it will be made of….

Speaking of position changes, Tesla had a horrible month (4.4%, down from 4.9% in September), which allowed Wuling (4.6%) to climb one position in October, into 4th. At the end of the year, expect Tesla to keep the #4 position, as its December peak should help it surpass Wuling.

Looking at OEMs/automotive groups/alliances, BYD is leading with 21.9% share of the market, up 0.1% in October.

Meanwhile, Geely remained in second with 11.8% share. The multinational conglomerate has the runner-up spot secured.

#3 SAIC was stable (8%) and remained firm in the last place on the podium, while #4 Leapmotor (6.7%, up 0.2%) remained on the rise. Will we see the startup challenge SAIC in 2027 for the 3rd spot?

Always interested in the auto industry, particularly in electric cars, Jose has pioneered on documenting the plug-in sales evolution through the EV Sales blog, allowing him to gain an expert view on where EVs are right now and where they are headed in the future. Extending that work and expertise, Jose was co-founder of EV-Volumes and currently works with the European Alternative Fuels Observatory on EV sales matters.

#5662. Who Needs Automatic Driving? You Love to Drive! However, for Many Handicapped, Tesla’s Full Self Driving Is the Only Option. It’s Use It, or Don’t Drive!

Editor’s note: In my opinion, a person should not be using Tesla FSD to drive somewhere if they aren’t capable of driving the trip fully themselves. Fritz makes the argument here that it is easier to supervise FSD and that is why someone with a handicap can use it when they don’t feel like they can drive fully on their own — they just have to pay attention and be ready to intervene if needed. However, my experience and opinion is that it can definitely be harder to intervene when needed than to just drive oneself, so if someone isn’t capable of driving fully themself, they shouldn’t be driving somewhere relying on FSD. As he is showing here, though, there are people — himself included — relying on FSD to drive when they feel they can’t drive a trip fully on their own for various physical reasons. Feel free to chime in below with your opinion on this matter. — Z.S.

Warning! Tesla’s Full Self Driving Supervised is an extremely capable automatic driving system. However, to use it you need to be an unimpaired legal driver who can intervene (Supervise) in a fraction of a second if needed. With that caveat, I will discuss in this article how FSD can take over driving for drivers with various kinds of handicaps.

I love to ride my ebike! I ride 15 to 20 miles every day. A few days ago, just after shoulder replacement, I remembered that Vasa Fitness has stationary bicycles. Just two days after surgery with my right arm in a sling, I knew that driving there would be difficult. I thought about asking my wife to drive me. But I got into my Tesla Model Y with Full Self Driving V 14 and said Hey Grok! Navigate me to Vasa Fitness. My car drove me out of my garage and without any intervention by me, touching the brake pedal, steering wheel or accelerator drove me to Vasa Fitness and parked me in the parking lot as you see in the lead photo.

Numerous situations have come to my attention where Tesla FSD can or could drive you safely, where the only other option would be to not drive.

For more on these matters, also read these pieces:

Elon Musk is a despicable person who no longer cares about clean energy. Also, he apparently no longer cares about his stockholders. His choice of supporting Donald Trump and spending almost half a billion dollars supporting Republican candidates in the last election have greatly damaged Tesla and reduced its stock value.

Many of my readers have been very critical of my support for Musk’s company. Unfortunately, in this early stage of EV development, no other company has the hardware, software, and Superchargers that make an electric car a reasonable alternative to a gas car for both city and cross-country driving anywhere in the US.

For those of you who wouldn’t consider buying a car from Musk’s company, I will present the next best alternative: You can buy a Rivian! It is a more traditional non-aerodynamic type of vehicle — a pickup truck or SUV. But it also has its benefits.

Rivian has the best non-Tesla driving automation available for consumer cars in the US at this time. I had planned to drive 40 miles down to the Rivian Demo Center in Salt Lake City to give their automatic driving a test drive. However, my research tells me that Rivian’s automatic driving is not nearly as good as Tesla’s at this point. It won’t drive you point to point in the city and on the highway without intervention. However, Rivian’s sensor suite is more comprehensive than Tesla’s. Tesla has made the bet that since human drivers can drive using only optical sensors, their eyes, it should be possible to drive automatically with only cameras. Rivian’s sensor suite includes 11 cameras and five radar sensors. Human drivers need to pull off the road when the visibility is very bad, but Rivian will eventually be able to continue driving during 0% visibility conditions.

Final comprehensive analysis: you may hate Musk, but if you want the latest technology in automatic driving and the freedom to drive electric on all the major highways in the US without careful planning, you’re going to have to choose a Tesla with Hardware V4. Tesla began delivering cars with HW4 in mid 2023.

According to Musk, 90% of traffic deaths would be prevented by using Tesla’s Full Self Driving. If true, you’d be crazy not to pay for it and activate it.

If you find any of my articles helpful to you, please use my referral link when buying a new Tesla: https://ts.la/arthur73734 (Be sure to use it when you make your order).

Tesla keeps changing the program, but here’s the latest: If you are buying a new Tesla Model 3 or Y and use my link, you will get 2 months of free Full Self Driving (a ~$200 value) on top of the one month that comes free with every new Model 3 or Y right now. This will get you a total of 3 months of free Full Self Driving. It will cost $99/month after that. At this moment, if you trade in a gas car, Tesla is offering you two years of free Supercharging with the purchase of a new Tesla.

#5660. New Jersey utilities have less than a year to implement virtual power plants

Let Utility Dive's free newsletter keep you informed, straight from your inbox.

Data from the two-year interim version of PowerUp NJ will inform the development of a long-term, open-access virtual power plant tariff, state utility regulators said on Nov. 12.

PowerUp NJ is a “first-of-its-kind statewide virtual power plant program that will give ratepayers more control to drive down their own energy costs,” Sherrill said in a statement that also referred to the plan as a “voluntary community energy program” that allows customers to “lend power from their homes” to support the grid.

The BPU’s order this month cements the program features and goals outlined in a July straw proposal from the agency.

Among other things, the order envisions a market-based framework that compensates distributed energy resources for grid services, rather than subsidizing their deployment; has standard program design elements across all four regulated utility territories; is technology neutral, with eligibility extended to net-metered solar-plus-storage systems; and requires utilities to test localized dispatch with an eye towards developing grid services that could defer or avoid infrastructure upgrades, according to a program factsheet.

The order also establishes technical working groups on measurement and verification, cost-benefit analysis, and program marketing; and aims to coordinate existing utility and state programs and proceedings involving distribution system planning, net metering, energy efficiency and electric vehicles, according to the factsheet.

The BPU expects smart thermostats, stationary home batteries, internet-connected electric water heaters and vehicle chargers and electric vehicles themselves to be eligible for PowerUp NJ at launch. Participating utilities and manufacturers will notify customers of specific eligible devices and models in mid-2027.

PowerUp NJ will be an opt-in program, meaning customers must affirmatively choose to participate. Once enrolled, customers will be able to “choose which devices and events to include” to avoid inconvenient or uncomfortable situations, like ceding control of a thermostat on a hot day, the BPU said.

Like other state-level VPPs, such as California’s Demand Side Grid Support program, PowerUp NJ aims to reduce grid stress and mitigate power price spikes by shifting load away from demand peaks. It will target both “distribution-level grid stress events” and PJM capacity and transmission coincident peaks, according to the program factsheet.

A preliminary staff analysis found that a 3% cut to peak demand could avoid about $60 million in annual system capacity costs while cutting local and greenhouse gas emissions from fossil-fueled peaker plants in New Jersey, the BPU said.

In a statement, BPU President Ben Hertz-Shargel said his agency would apply lessons from the two-year interim version of PowerUp NJ to the development of a long-term framework for the program.

“We will develop this program carefully, learn from real-world results, and expand it over time, growing the earnings opportunities for residential and business customers,” Hertz-Shargel said in a statement.

#5658. Mission Solar Energy puts supply chain transparency in the spotlight at RE+ 2026

By Marie DeFreitas, Contributor | November 18, 2026

With new high-efficiency residential and commercial modules and an integrated supply chain spanning polysilicon to power conversion, Mission Solar Energy is making a case for U.S. manufacturing, domestic content and FEOC-compliant sourcing.

As the solar industry continues to navigate shifting trade policies, domestic content incentives, and supply chain scrutiny, Mission Solar Energy is bringing a message of manufacturing strength and transparency to RE+ 2026. The San Antonio-based manufacturer showcased its latest commercial and residential modules while highlighting the advantages of its relationship with fellow OCI Holdings subsidiaries OCI TerraSus and OCI Power.

The announcement puts the spotlight on a growing priority for solar developers, installers and distributors: knowing exactly where components originate and having the documentation to prove it.

The three companies collectively cover significant portions of the solar value chain. OCI TerraSus produces high-purity, solar-grade polysilicon in Sarawak, Malaysia; Mission Solar designs, engineers, and assembles modules in San Antonio, Texas; and OCI Power manufactures inverters and power conversion systems in South Korea.

According to Mission Solar, all three companies are compliant with Foreign Entity of Concern (FEOC) requirements and are not classified as prohibited foreign entities. The integrated structure offers customers access to a traceable supply chain backed by OCI Holdings, a global clean energy group headquartered in Seoul.

For an industry that’s becoming more and more focused on sourcing eligibility and domestic manufacturing credentials, that positioning could prove commercially important.

On the product front, Mission Solar is highlighting two modules aimed at different market segments.

For commercial and industrial (C&I) applications, the company is featuring its 595W N-Type Bifacial 144HC module. The dual-glass design captures sunlight from both sides, potentially increasing energy production without requiring additional project footprint. Designed and assembled in the United States, the module carries 11.5% domestic content, meets FEOC requirements and supports front-side loads of up to 5,400 Pa.

Residential installers will get a look at the 440W N-Type 108HC rooftop module, which Mission Solar says will be the first of its kind on the market with 12% domestic content. Building on an existing 6% domestic-content version, the module combines N-type cell technology and a multi-busbar design to deliver efficiency of up to 23%. It is also approved on residential financing platforms including LightReach and GoodLeap.

Looking further ahead, Mission Solar plans to preview two next-generation G12R modules, with commercial launches anticipated in late 2027. The company will also display a lower-domestic-content U.S.-produced C&I module option expected in 2027.

Although module performance is still critical to the industry, supply chain credibility is increasingly part of the overall purchasing decision.

As developers evaluate project economics, tax credit eligibility and sourcing risk, manufacturers that can offer transparent documentation alongside competitive technology may have an advantage.
