#5657. Ford Begins Producing Its Own LFP Batteries

Ford is now a battery producer. Yes, it’s using battery technology licensed from CATL, but it is now producing EV batteries it is going to be using in its own vehicles. In fact, it is reportedly doing so in a really fast, advanced way. “The BlueOval Battery Park Michigan assembly lines can deliver 40 cells every minute, making it among the fastest LFP prismatic battery assembly lines outside of China.2 That pace helps drive down battery costs, and the facility has capacity for more. But speed must go hand in hand with quality,” Charles Poon, vice president of hardware engineering at Ford, writes.

“The first lithium iron phosphate (LFP) prismatic battery cells are rolling off the line at BlueOval Battery Park Michigan and headed to Louisville Assembly Plant to power Ford Fathom prototypes. With this milestone, Ford is now in the battery business and will be the first to deliver LFP batteries in the U.S. for mainstream consumer automotive use,” Poon writes. He goes on to explain further in a recent article why Ford chose to produce LFP battery cells and a bit on how it is doing so. Here’s a bit more from that article:

“The shipment caps years of work by the team that designed, built, and launched the plant on schedule. Ford now delivers cells at a plant it owns and operates, building expertise to support more affordable electric vehicles (EVs) and Ford Energy’s battery energy storage business in Kentucky.

“Why Start with LFP Prismatic for Fathom?

“When Alan Clarke, vice president, Advanced Development Projects, first joined the development team that created the Universal Electric Vehicle Platform, he issued a clear challenge: find an efficient LFP prismatic battery. The goal was to make EVs significantly more affordable.

“We talked to battery companies around the world. We wanted a technology that was proven in the field, ready to manufacture at scale, and suited to what we were building. That led us to the world’s leading battery manufacturer, CATL, whose batteries power 30 million vehicles across the globe.

“A battery is one of the biggest contributors to an EV’s cost and weight. So, we asked: How do we get more out of the battery without simply adding more cells? Our answer was efficiency over excess.

“LFP chemistry uses no nickel or cobalt, which helps hold down costs. It also holds up over years of charging and use. That durability is valuable as we give customers ways to use their battery beyond driving. For example, AC bidirectional power lets a vehicle receive electricity from a properly equipped home and supply power back to it.1

“This milestone is about more than shipping our first cells. We’re building the expertise to produce high-quality batteries at scale — for more affordable EVs and a new energy storage business.”

It looks like a serious effort and serious progress toward making more affordable, more competitive electric cars at Ford. The aim has been to get as good as making electric cars as Chinese companies are. How effective and successful will this new path be for Ford? We’ll see.

#5655. DOJ closes NextEra-Dominion antitrust probe as state hearings continue

Let Utility Dive's free newsletter keep you informed, straight from your inbox.

The decision followed a promise by the utilities to allow ISO-NE’s internal market monitor to investigate proposed plant retirements.

In a July filing with the U.S. Securities and Exchange Commission, Dominion said the merger needs approval from FERC, the Nuclear Regulatory Commission, the SCC, the North Carolina Utilities Commission and the Public Service Commission of South Carolina.

The SCC has been holding daily evidentiary hearings since Nov. 17, while South Carolina and North Carolina regulators have scheduled theirs to begin in December and February, respectively. Neither FERC nor the NRC have issued a decision.

Dominion and NextEra announced in a Sept. 14 release that they would double a proposed monthly residential bill credit for Virginia customers from two to four years.

In that release, the companies also said they would accelerate solar, storage and other resources in Virginia to “affordably power the Commonwealth’s growth,” advance the Virginia Clean Economy Act and deliver “long-term affordability benefits by leveraging the combined company’s scale to buy, build, finance and operate more efficiently.”

Last week, SCC regulators and attorneys for the state pressed witnesses, including Baine, NextEra CEO John Ketchum and Dominion Energy CEO Bob Blue, on how customer benefits could be calculated and ensured.

Baine said that when Dominion merged with SCANA in 2019, it saw “benefits of scale” where input cost was lowered, adding, “We believe it will be on a much bigger scale with this merger, but just can't do the math for you today.”

John Farmer, a senior assistant attorney general for Virginia, pointed Ketchum to a written response from Dominion and NextEra in which they said they “have not prepared a year-by-year quantification of benefits to Dominion Energy Virginia's existing customers.”

Ketchum said he stands behind “the fact that we've offered up the bill credits” and broadly outlined the potential operational benefits of a combined company.

“Shame on us if we can't realize the scale benefits that I've spoken about,” Ketchum said in Wednesday’s hearing. “That’s what we do.”

SCC Chair Kelsey Bagot asked Blue how he would ensure that “potential issues do not erode the benefits” that are “possible” as a result of the merger.

“Have you started developing a plan?” Bagot said.

Blue said that if the merger isn’t managed well, “it is possible that the longer-term benefits that we're describing won't materialize as quickly.”

“As I mentioned in my answer to you before, I don't believe that would leave our customers worse off,” he continued. “There may be some missed opportunities.”

Some intervenors also criticized the bill credit proposal.

Bloom Advisory Partners partner Teresa Ringenbach testified on behalf of the Retail Energy Advancement League that the bill credit structure “effectively penalizes customers for choosing a competitive supplier,” which she said “is discriminatory and undermines the statutorily mandated retail choice framework.”

“A large general service customer using 72,000 megawatt hours per year would receive approximately $225,000 if it shops, compared to approximately $642,000 if it does not,” Ringenbach said. “That difference is a price signal, and it’s funded by the utility's new parent, and it rewards remaining on the utility's generation service.”

“Shareholder credits should not be viewed as the price of getting a merger approved,” she added. “They should deliver real value to every single wires customer.”

Bagot asked Richard McCann, an energy expert testifying on behalf of Gov. Abigail Spanberger’s office, “Do we have the ability to provide rate credits to a single rate class? For example … only to residential customers and not to any of the other rate classes.”

“I recognize that's not exactly what you're proposing,” Bagot added, “but it does shift potentially what that rate credit would be, maybe closer to $29 per month.”

Raising the credit to $29 a month was a condition McCann proposed in his written testimony, along with a six-year residential rate freeze.

In response to Bagot, McCann said, “I know that at $10, they won't notice if it phases out or not. $30 a month, they might notice that the credit disappears if it's a sudden shock.”

He said that implementing the higher credit for residential ratepayers alongside other methods he proposed — such as raising the debt share of Dominion's capital structure — could lower bills over time and provide a “smooth glide path” for the end of the credits.

SCC Commissioner Jehmal Hudson pushed back on the rate freeze proposal, asking McCann what authority allows the SCC to “freeze future base rate increases irrespective of the future costs” and noting that rate freezes in Virginia “have primarily stemmed from the General Assembly legislation.”

“You are in an extraordinary position right now with this merger,” McCann said. “There are certain things that you have discretion about going forward — you will have to decide if you have that discretion.”

#5653. Memo To Musk: Denial Is Just The First Step On The Long Road To Recovery

Tesla CEO Elon Musk made headlines yet again last week, when he publicly denied any affiliation with US President Donald Trump’s “MAGA” movement. If Musk intends to stage a sort of political recovery, he still has a long way to go. Denial is just a precursor to recovery, which begins in earnest when you stop denying the reality of your situation and start admitting you need help from a higher power or some such. And in fact, a higher power is coming for Musk —  if not in spiritual form, then in the form of Congress, where the incoming Democratic majority plans to exercise its authority, and call Musk to account for manipulating SpaceX satellite services in Ukraine.

During an interview for the New York Times DealBook Summit last week, Elon Musk claimed that he was not affiliated with MAGA, though he did not apologize for supporting MAGA candidates this year, or for supporting Donald Trump in 2024. Musk simply said he “felt foolish” for wasting his money on losers.

In the same interview, Musk insisted — falsely — that Russia was the aggrieved party when Russian President Vladimir Putin invaded the Donbas region of Ukraine back in 2014. Justin sayin’…

For those of you wondering why Elon Musk’s politics should be a matter of concern to CleanTechnica, that’s a good question, and here’s the answer: Elon Musk sailed into clean tech history on the coattails of the EV startup Tesla Motors (soon to become simply Tesla) in the early 2000’s, only to emerge in more recent years as a champion of the far right alongside President Donald Trump’s notoriously racist, fossil fuel-supporting “Make America Great Again” movement, thereby setting back the US decarbonization movement by years if not decades.

Musk has also made his own contribution to the resurgence of fossil fuels in the US. His new xAI data centers are well known for deploying loosely regulated (or illegal) on-site gas generators, and his SpaceX venture has been gobbling up natural gas for rocket fuel while laying plans for future expansion.

So much for Elon Musk’s footprint on the US energy profile. Let’s move over to Ukraine, where rich fossil energy and mineral resources in the nation’s Donbas region prompted Russian President Putin to send unmarked soldiers — aka the “little green men” — to annex that portion of Ukraine in 2014 (see clarification/correction below). A ceasefire temporarily calmed the situation until February of 2022, when Putin sent a fully equipped convoy to the Ukrainian capital city Kyiv, aiming to take over the entire seat of government in one fell swoop — literally, in one day.

The effort failed, both militarily and in terms of Putin’s energy goals. Ukraine promptly cut Russia off from its electricity grid and hooked up with the European grid, a complex effort assisted by the Biden administration. Ukraine also set the wheels in motion to join the European Union as a significant source of clean energy, including green hydrogen gas alongside its copious potential for clean power including biomass, wind, and solar power.

To put that into perspective, consider that Ukraine is almost 90% as large as Texas, which has staked out a leadership position in wind and solar power among the 50 US states.

With Ukraine as a leading renewable energy supplier, the EU would have a fresh opportunity to meets its decarbonization goals on schedule while also reducing imports of fossil energy from Russia… And… Well… That’s where Elon Musk enters the picture.

Musk first emerged as a full blown MAGA supporter in 2020, when he helped Trump stir up conspiracy theories and confusion over the federal response to the COVID-19 crisis, in a failed effort to support the President’s bid for re-election that year. Had Trump won, he would have continued to implement a key part of his foreign policy agenda, namely, withdrawing the US from NATO, the North Atlantic Treaty Alliance. NATO formed in 1949 as an effort to curb Russian expansion in Europe, an effort Trump has upended on account of his longstanding friendship with Putin.

The election of Joe Biden in 2020 did not prevent Musk from providing additional aid and comfort to Trump. Musk used his media-grabbing public profile to undercut the authority of President Biden at every opportunity, including on the world stage as well as here at home. The stream of insults extended into January of 2022, even as the reality of a fresh round of Russian expansion into Europe began to materialize.

Musk had an additional opportunity to challenge Biden’s authority in January of 2022, when the so-called “Freedom Convoy” of truckers barricaded government offices in Canada, ostensibly in protest of a vaccine mandate. Musk gleefully cheered the effort, which disrupted transportation systems and distracted public attention in two key NATO allies — Canada and the US — even as Russia began ratcheting up its plans to take over Kyiv.

However, once it became clear that Putin’s one-day takeover of Ukraine was a failure, Musk about-faced. He took credit for providing “free” communications aid to Ukraine in the form of satellite services through the Starlink branch of SpaceX.

JK! A later assessment revealed that other sources shouldered about 85% of the cost of Starlink terminals initially supplied to Ukraine, including USAID here in the US (not a typo — that’s the US Administration for International Development, the same agency that Musk later destroyed in 2025).

Poland and other groups also contributed initial assistance to purchase  the terminals alongside an estimated 30% of connectivity costs. In addition, by September of 2022 Musk was making the case for US taxpayers to foot the bill.

Don’t just take my word for it. In 2023, the Belfer Center for International Affairs at Harvard recapped SpaceX’s negotiations over payment for its services to Ukraine during the previous year, concluding that “we can assume the U.S. government agreed to pay some, if not all, of the costs for continued operation of the technology.”

“SpaceX, and particularly Musk, was able to play the role of wartime hero, showcasing the power and value of the technology products the company developed, while simultaneously avoiding sustained and hefty bills,” Belfer Center observed.

US Department of Defense contract or not, by 2023 SpaceX was already making unilateral decisions that restricted Ukraine’s ability to deploy the technology, with SpaceX CEO Gwynne Shotwell publicly insisting that the company never intended for Ukraine to use it “for offensive purposes.”

Of course, “offensive” is in the eye of the beholder. Since Ukraine is the invaded party seeking to assert its rights over internationally recognized borders against Russia, one could argue that every action Ukraine takes is a defensive one until Putin agrees to concede defeat and cede back his illegal claims over Ukrainian lands.

Of course, that’s not how Musk portrayed the situation in his DealBook interview last week. Nevertheless, one also could argue that the US government, not the vendor, controls the use of contractually obligated resources. Be that as it may, Musk raised the bar considerably in October, when he — or Trump — or both — once again dialed down satellite service in Ukraine, exposing that country to further attacks.

Musk attempted to mollify bipartisan outrage over the debacle with yet another publicity stunt for SpaceX. However, service interruptions have continued into this week. Meanwhile, Trump has spent the same week cozying up to both Putin and Chinese President Xi Jinping, aided and abetted by an obedient Republican majority in Congress.

That majority will evaporate, though, in January 3, which is just around the corner. The incoming Democratic majority is already gearing up for a fight. In a letter dated November 20, the incoming Chair of the House Intelligence Committee, Jim Himes (D-CT), demanded an in-person briefing from senior SpaceX officers, seeking an explanation for the disruption of military-grade Starshield service in Ukraine. As leverage, Himes warned Musk that a Democratic majority in Congress will do what the Republican majority failed to do, namely, exercise its constitutional authority over federal contracts.

Referring specifically to SpaceX’s military-grade Starshield satellite services in Ukraine, Himes warned Musk that his entire suite of present and future SpaceX security contracts with the US government is at risk. “SpaceX’s potentially unilateral modification of a U.S. Government contract, combined with its lack of responsiveness to congressional oversight, calls into question every contract the U.S. Government has with SpaceX,” Himes explained.

“For that reason, it is difficult for me to continue supporting the authorization of funding for these SpaceX- developed, government-owned systems until I receive satisfactory answers to my questions about the Starshield service in Ukraine,” Himes continued.

Will Musk simply ignore the request for a briefing? After all, as Himes notes, Musk failed to respond to earlier requests. Stay tuned…

Clarification/Correction: Russia initially annexed parts of the Donbas in 2014 through its support for a separatist movement, later acknowledged to be a Russian military operation. The “little green men” refers more specifically to the annexation of Crimea during the same period.

#5651. Off-grid costs will push data centers back to utilities: Mitsubishi Heavy Industries CEO

Let Utility Dive's free newsletter keep you informed, straight from your inbox.

CEO Eisaku Ito expressed confidence in utilities’ long-term expansion plans, arguing that growing demand for electricity does not rely solely on data centers.

Despite the recent attention on new electric demand created by data centers, Mitsubishi Heavy Industries sees greater potential for long-term growth in the utilities sector, MHI President and CEO Eisaku Ito said earlier this month.

Utilities and independent power producers accounted for 94% of Mitsubishi Heavy Industries’ new gas turbine orders in 2024 and 2025, Ito said.

Although many U.S. data center operators say they intend to install their own off-grid generation, Ito argued that these plans rely on smaller gas-fired power plants that cost more to run than the larger plants utilities and IPPs are pursuing.

In the long-term, Ito said, the cost differential will push data centers to connect to the grid, ultimately supporting utilities' expansion plans.

His comments, made during a call with analysts on Nov. 6, according to a translated transcript, come as fallout from the U.S. midterm elections and stock market volatility raise questions about the future of the artificial intelligence buildout in North America. Ito expressed confidence in utilities' long-term expansion plans, arguing that growing demand for electricity does not rely solely on data centers.

“Electricity demand from data centers is expected to grow rapidly in the United States, but data centers still account for only around 10% of the country's total electricity demand,” Ito said. “If some of the anticipated growth in demand does not materialize, there will be some impact, but we believe the effect on our overall GTCC business would be limited.”

MHI booked 8 GW in orders for large frame gas turbines in the first half of its 2026 fiscal year, compared to 16 GW of orders in all of FY 2025. Profit for the company’s Energy Systems segment rose 80% on account of increased orders and shifts in the value of the yen, according to the company's financial statements.

“We are also beginning to see the benefits of improved pricing on new unit projects, lower loss-related costs and greater production efficiency,” MHI CFO Hiroshi Nishio said.

Company executives said they continue to take a conservative approach to forecasting demand and expanding manufacturing capacity. The company expects global demand for gas turbines at all manufacturers and makers to average about 70 GW per year over the next five years — somewhat lower than the 100 GW MHI expects will sell across all manufacturers this year amid what Ito characterized as “exceptionally strong” demand he does not anticipate will continue.

But Ito said he remains confident in MHI’s company backlog, noting that the company vets new orders carefully and hasn’t had any order cancellations in the past two years.

The company plans to double its gas turbine production capacity by 2030, though most of the additional capacity will come from improved processes and automation at existing plants in the U.S. and Japan.

“We are improving productivity and driving other initiatives at a pace that outstrips the growth in orders,” he said.

MHI also signaled that it's exploring opportunities to expand into other infrastructure-related markets.

Ito said MHI will release additional details in May, but suggested the company is interested in expanding its gas turbine service and repair capabilities, as well as investing in unmanned systems that can operate in harsh environments. At the same time, MHI plans to divest some of its aerospace business, he said.

“These examples may seem quite different, but they all share a common theme: resilience,” Ito said. “We aim to identify new business opportunities in infrastructure and industrial machinery through the lens of resilience and turn them into future growth.”

#5649. The Insurance Affordability Crisis: Risks, Resilience, and Solutions

NRDC resources on insurance affordability, market availability, climate risk, resilience investments, and strategies to protect homeowners, businesses, and communities.

For property owners, insurance provides a critical financial safety net that protects homes, businesses, and other assets from unexpected losses, helping owners recover and rebuild when disasters occur. Beyond individual protection, insurance is fundamental to the resilience of communities and local economies, enabling faster recovery, supporting continued investment, and reducing the long-term financial impacts of shocks. As risks become more frequent, severe, and costly, access to affordable, available, and reliable insurance is increasingly essential, not only for safeguarding property owners but also for maintaining overall property affordability, economic stability, and the resilience and prosperity of the communities in which they live and operate.

The frequency and severity of losses are increasing faster than insurers can sustainably price and absorb them, leading to higher premiums, reduced coverage availability, and, in some cases, the withdrawal of insurers from certain markets. This trend is eroding the affordability and accessibility of insurance, increasing the total cost of property ownership, and placing additional pressure on housing and business affordability while leaving individuals, businesses, and communities more physically and financially exposed.

However, the insurance sector is increasingly recognized as a key component of resilience, incentivizing risk reduction through pricing and support for adaptation measures that can help moderate long-term insurance costs. When functioning effectively, insurance enables communities to not only recover from shocks but also to better prepare for future risks while preserving access to coverage.

Linked below are reports, expert blogs, and other resources that examine the insurability crisis and how insurance can play a key role in advancing housing resilience and adaptation.

NRDC’s director of insurance and financial risk, Alfonso Pating, breaks down how homeowner’s insurance works and why climate change is making it unaffordable.

More reports and resources on this topic here.

Article originally published on NRDC website.

NRDC is the nation's most effective environmental action group, combining the grassroots power of 1.3 million members and online activists with the courtroom clout and expertise of more than 350 lawyers, scientists, and other professionals.

#5647. Midterms provide clarity on data center development: Vistra CEO

Let Utility Dive's free newsletter keep you informed, straight from your inbox.

“As we’ve said for some time, load interconnect queues were overstated across the country and especially in ERCOT, driven by speculation from developers,” Vistra CEO James Burke said.

The midterm elections — with data centers a key issue in many races — helped provide clarity around the environment for data center development, James Burke, Vistra president and CEO, said on an earnings call earlier this month.

“Before the elections, in many markets, we were seeing concerns about data centers in the way that the public was reacting to the pace of development, who had control over those decisions, what impact could it have on electric prices, water, all of that,” Burke said on the Nov. 6 call. “Moving past this election is a positive just in terms of being able to understand what's at stake, and now how do we move forward?”

So far, the key messages from Texas Gov.-elect Gina Hinojosa have been “very similar to some of the key messages that Governor Abbott had expressed and … consistent with messages we've been expressing, which is there has to be some guardrails on this development,” Burke said.

Vistra wants tougher requirements for data center interconnection, including higher collateral requirements and take-or-pay agreements for transmission charges, according to Burke.

“As we've said for some time, load interconnect queues were overstated across the country and especially in [the Electric Reliability Council of Texas footprint], driven by speculation from developers,” Burke said.

Even with the midterm elections and regulatory actions around data centers, through 2030 Vistra expects load to grow by 4% to 6% a year in ERCOT and by 2% to 3% annually in the PJM Interconnection footprint, according to Burke.

Despite Vistra’s expectations for load growth in ERCOT, power prices there have been trending lower due to some crypto mining facilities coming offline so they can convert into data centers, and the state’s data center audit and permitting pause, Burke said.

“We do not see this as a long-term impact,” he said, noting that ERCOT hit a 91 GW peak demand record this year.

Vistra’s fleet has grown to about 50 GW with its Oct. 30 Cogentrix acquisition, Burke said.

The fleet includes combined cycle and peaking power plants that are running more often, according to Burke.

“As renewable penetration continues throughout the country, you're seeing these assets get called on more,” Burke said in response to an analyst question about potentially selling off parts of the fleet. “So we've been bullish on dispatchable. We've been bullish on flexibility … So as a long-term owner-operator, we like the fleet.”

#5645. Six new ways to simplify solar installs: Top Rack Technology brings mounting innovation to RE+ 2026

By Marie DeFreitas, Contributor | November 18, 2026

From smarter waterproofing to 360-degree rail adjustment, Top Rack Technology is putting installer efficiency, rooftop flexibility, and cost control at the center of its latest product lineup.

As the solar industry continues to look for ways to reduce installation costs and streamline residential projects, mounting hardware is getting a fresh dose of innovation. Top Rack Technology, Inc. (TRT) is making that case at RE+ 2026, where the solar racking manufacturer is showcasing six new mounting solutions designed to simplify rooftop installations, improve flexibility, and help installers work more efficiently.

Its latest lineup reflects a practical approach to product development: addressing everyday installation challenges while keeping system performance, aesthetics, and affordability in focus.

Six Products, One Common Goal: Better Installation Efficiency

Among the new offerings is TRT’s Full-Coverage Skirt System, which targets an important consideration in residential solar: appearance. By concealing exposed array edges, the system delivers a cleaner, more finished look that can help installations better complement residential properties.

The new TRT-G2 Bracket takes aim at installation simplicity and competitive pricing. Designed around practical field requirements, the bracket can be paired with TRT’s ButyLok waterproofing component, creating an integrated attachment and roof-sealing solution.

Waterproofing is particularly important in rooftop solar, where roof penetrations are still a critical consideration for installers and property owners alike. ButyLok introduces a shoe-like component containing pre-applied, gel-like butyl material. When compressed by the G2 bracket, the material spreads across the intended sealing surface, potentially reducing manual sealant application and simplifying the installation process.

TRT is also introducing SAVRIX, another residential attachment option developed with manufacturing efficiency, installation practicality, and cost competitiveness top of mind.. Meanwhile, the RF-2 Rail Fastener is designed to simplify rail-to-bracket connections.

Top Rack Technology’s FreePivot Bracket features a 360-degree rotatable rail fastener and built-in height adjustment to accommodate varying rooftop conditions and simplify solar panel installations. Credit: Top Rack Technology

But perhaps the most versatile addition is the FreePivot Bracket. Its 360-degree rotatable rail fastener allows installers to accommodate attachment points that aren’t perfectly aligned. Built-in height adjustment helps compensate for uneven roof surfaces, while its all-metal construction supports durability. The one-orientation design and single-socket installation concept are also intended to reduce tool changes and keep crews moving.

Beyond the six new products, TRT is showcasing its established portfolio, including the TRT 01 Rail-less Base System, TRT 02 Rail-Base System, and TRT 03 Tile Hook System.

The company is also highlighting its Online BOM Design Tool, which supports project setup, structural design inputs, module layouts, engineering verification, and automated bill-of-materials generation. Connecting digital planning with physical mounting solutions could offer installers and distributors a more streamlined path from project design to field execution.

Installation hardware doesn’t need to be flashy to make a difference. Improvements in attachment flexibility, waterproofing, assembly time, and aesthetics can translate into meaningful operational advantages.

#5644. RMI’s Hydrogen Research Is Getting Better. Its Investment Advice Still Falls Short.

RMI has become much more realistic about hydrogen demand and competing technologies. That makes the gaps between its findings and its recommendations harder to excuse. Its latest work acknowledges that battery trucks generally have better economics than hydrogen fuel-cell trucks, that Europe needs far less hydrogen in 2030 than it originally targeted, and that low-emissions ammonia still costs substantially more than conventional ammonia. Several recommendations still seek public money to develop the very hydrogen markets the research has found difficult to justify.

I have been challenging this tension since January 2024. RMI matters because governments, investors and industrial companies use its research when deciding where to spend money. Its hydrogen program has made genuine analytical corrections. It has not made all of the accompanying strategic decisions.

Consider RMI’s February 2026 report on hydrogen trucking in India. The baseline comparisons find that fuel-cell trucks do not reach battery-electric truck ownership-cost parity through 2040, including the long-haul use case. The report also explores a much more favourable hydrogen price in an alternative sensitivity, which brings fuel-cell trucks closer to parity. Those are different findings, and the baseline matters to investment decisions today.

The report nevertheless recommends hydrogen vehicle subsidies, support for refuelling stations, production incentives and tax relief. It treats a parallel hydrogen-truck market as something to build. The case for spending public money on that market should start with evidence that a particular freight service cannot be served as well or better by batteries. The reported cost advantage of batteries does not disappear when the policy section begins.

The problem reaches beyond India. RMI’s 2025 US hydrogen report says battery trucks have greater market readiness and lower total cost of ownership, especially in the near term, yet calls for hydrogen distribution and refuelling infrastructure along freight corridors. The independent NACFE Run on Less ownership-cost work, developed within RMI’s freight research ecosystem, projects batteries becoming the lowest-cost option across its modelled duty cycles by 2035 without incentives. Hydrogen remains the most expensive option across nearly all of them. These findings support battery infrastructure as the default and a high evidentiary bar for hydrogen exceptions.

RMI already knows how to make appropriate distinctions elsewhere. Its 2026 steel research separates hydrogen used to reduce iron ore from hydrogen burned merely to supply process heat. In the illustrative plant, electric heat requires about 850 GWh a year, compared with 1,300 GWh of electricity to produce enough additional hydrogen for heating. That is the kind of direct service comparison that ought to govern recommendations throughout the organization.

Its new Green Ammonia Buyers Alliance also has a coherent industrial purpose. Fertiliser production already requires ammonia, commonly made with fossil-derived hydrogen. Purchasing lower-emissions ammonia to replace that supply addresses an existing industrial input. It does not require inventing a new energy market for ammonia combustion.

Europe shows how improved diagnosis can coexist with excessive policy ambition. RMI’s March 2025 recalibration reduced expected mandate-driven renewable hydrogen demand in 2030 to 2.2–2.8 million tons, far below the EU’s original 20-million-ton supply-and-import ambition. Its analysis identifies €13.9 billion in public support under existing mandates, plus €31.7 billion for an accelerated scenario. The combined €45.6 billion deserves a comparison with the other ways industry could obtain the same low-carbon products and services, including direct electrification and imported green materials.

That comparison changes the question for ammonia, steel and refining. A European fertiliser or chemical manufacturer needs ammonia, not necessarily hydrogen manufactured inside Europe. A steelmaker needs iron meeting its grade and emissions requirements. A mill needs process heat at specified temperatures. Governments can pay a premium to preserve domestic capacity for security or resilience, but the benefit must be valued and the public cost made explicit.

My November 2026 European hydrogen assessment applies that approach. In its central 2050 scenario, ammonia produced in Europe with on-site green hydrogen costs approximately €1,222 per ton. Qualifying imported green ammonia costs €745–755 per ton under the model’s assumptions. These are modelled values, not supplier bids. They demonstrate why an analysis that starts with hydrogen production targets can produce the wrong infrastructure plan.

RMI’s 2026 China green ammonia and methanol report acknowledges that green production costs remain two to three times conventional costs and that demand development lags supply ambitions. The report nevertheless discusses expanding ammonia and methanol as energy carriers, including ammonia for electricity. The relevant comparison for electricity is renewable generation with storage and other flexibility, not fossil-derived ammonia alone. RMI has not demonstrated that adding an ammonia combustion pathway wins that comparison.

This is a strategy problem as much as a modelling problem. Using Richard Rumelt’s framework, RMI’s diagnosis has improved, its guiding policy remains too committed to expanding hydrogen markets, and some coherent actions are missing. An economically unfavourable comparison should be able to end an infrastructure recommendation. Instead, it too often generates proposals for subsidies that would make the hydrogen route easier to sell.

RMI has the expertise to correct this. Its hydrogen recommendations should carry explicit alternative-technology comparisons, investment thresholds and exit criteria. Cross-program review should include the researchers measuring battery costs, industrial electrification, freight operations and grid flexibility. Where hydrogen is an essential chemical input or demonstrably the best means of delivering a service, support it. Where competing technologies win on cost and deployment readiness, stop trying to create demand through policy.

The full TFIE Strategy Briefing assessment examines RMI’s US, India, Europe and China reports side by side, including the differences between its findings and recommendations. It also links the original sources and the 2024 critique.

#5642. FERC must take action to protect ratepayers from higher transmission costs in SPP

Let Utility Dive's free newsletter keep you informed, straight from your inbox.

The Southwest Power Pool is misusing an exemption meant for urgent reliability needs to avoid competition in favor of incumbent utilities, writes Paul Cicio.

Paul Cicio is chairman of the Electricity Transmission Competition Coalition and president and CEO of the Industrial Energy Consumers of America.

As electricity prices continue to surge, consumers cannot afford to pay more than necessary for the new transmission lines that deliver their power. Requiring utilities to compete with one another gives regulators a direct way to reduce those costs.

Earlier this year, the Southwest Power Pool decided to exempt four projects expected to cost $3.8 billion from competitive bidding by classifying them as short-term reliability projects under its 2025 Integrated Transmission Planning portfolio.

SPP’s tariff specifies that unless a project is required to be in service in three years or less, it must face competition in building new large transmission lines, consistent with Federal Energy Regulatory Commission Order 1000. But the projects SPP has designated as short-term in its 2025 portfolio are not expected to enter service until 2029 or 2030.

SPP violated its FERC-approved tariff by not requiring the transmission projects to face competition. It is misusing an exemption meant for urgent reliability needs to avoid competition in favor of incumbent utilities, contrary to the vital electricity affordability needs of ratepayers. As a result, ratepayers in the 17-state region could pay an estimated $750 million in higher electricity bills for these projects.

We at the Electricity Transmission Competition Coalition and the Industrial Energy Consumers of America have filed a complaint asking FERC to hold SPP accountable for violating its tariff and require the four projects to be competitively bid.

Ratepayers are concerned because SPP has relied on this exemption before and the projects have grown larger and more expensive over time.

In 2024, SPP directly assigned $3.2 billion in projects to incumbent utilities based on short-term reliability need, without competitive bidding. The Industrial Energy Consumers of America opposed those projects in a January 2025 letter to SPP.

This past January, the ETCC urged the SPP Board of Directors to put its 2025 short-term reliability projects, which included new 765-kV lines, through competitive bidding. But SPP has continued to bypass competition over these and other stakeholder objections without providing sufficient justification or transparency.

If FERC allows SPP to continue, the short-term reliability exemption could become a routine way to avoid competition altogether, and the precedent would reach ratepayers across the country.

When utilities are required to compete with one another, they sharpen their pencils and contractually commit to cost containment provisions. Some include provisions that guarantee delivery of the project on time or pay a financial penalty.

None of the $3.2 billion projects SPP directly assigned in 2024 included a guaranteed in-service deadline or cost containment provisions.

Competition works. ETCC reviewed 19 transmission projects that went through competitive bidding and found an average cost reduction of 34%. ETCC’s review of 15 projects that did not go through competitive bidding found an average cost overrun of 89%.

Also, there is no evidence that assigning projects directly to incumbent utilities gets them built faster. For example, SPP competitively bid the Minco and Wolf Creek projects, which entered into service in 2025 and met their needed or expected in-service dates.

Competition can deliver on both reduced costs and on time start-up of the project. Ratepayers do not have to sacrifice reliability for lower costs.

The nearly $3.8 billion price tag is only the beginning of what consumers could ultimately pay. When financing costs, utility profits and other expenses are factored in, the total cost to ratepayers could more than double over the life of these projects.

SPP's decisions today will shape electricity costs for families and businesses across the region for decades to come. And SPP is poised to develop an even costlier transmission plan for 2027 that includes billions of dollars more in projects that would be exempted from competition.

The Federal Power Act is first and foremost a consumer protection act and FERC has a responsibility to ensure that consumers receive just and reasonable rates. FERC approved SPP’s tariff and has a duty to enforce it.

Electricity consumers will ultimately pay for the billions of dollars that grid operators commit to new transmission. FERC should hold SPP to competitive bidding as the standard for that spending, with exemptions limited to the narrow cases for projects that can be completed and placed into service in three years or less.

The SPP complaint is yet another test for FERC. Will the commission stand with consumers or protect monopoly utility profits? There is a troubling pattern of inaction by FERC to enforce Order 1000. Consumers have filed four complaints at FERC dating back to 2022 without them taking action, despite electricity affordability being top of mind nationwide.

#5639. TVA touts $1.7B data center deal under new large-load tariff

Let Utility Dive's free newsletter keep you informed, straight from your inbox.

Separately, the utility recently received the first small modular reactor construction permit from the NRC for a BWRX-300 reactor at the Clinch River Site in Oak Ridge, Tennessee.

The Tennessee Valley Authority is set to receive $1.7 billion in aggregate payments from an unnamed data center customer under its new tariff for data centers above 5 MW, the federal utility said in a Nov. 12 U.S. Securities and Exchange Commission filing.

“Capacity commitment charges under the agreement are based on a rate of approximately $1.5 million per MW of requested capacity above 5 MW,” the filing said. “This rate reflects TVA's current cohort-based pricing methodology, which may be revised for future customer cohorts.”

The new tariff took effect Oct. 1 and will be phased in through 2029, the filing said. That phase-in period will apply to existing customers to allow them to retain their existing rate structure for a period of time, the Nashville Scene reported in August.

TVA CEO Mike Skaggs said during a Nov. 13 earnings call that the change to data center rates has supported the utility’s investments.

Those investments include extending the lives of the utility’s existing nuclear units and “helping expand the nuclear fuel and supply hub in the East Tennessee area,” he said.

The utility recently received the first small modular reactor construction permit from the U.S. Nuclear Regulatory Commission for a BWRX-300 reactor at the Clinch River Site in Oak Ridge, “although TVA has not yet decided whether to construct a reactor at the site,” its filing said.

Skaggs came out of retirement to serve as interim CEO following the retirement of former CEO Don Moul and was instated as TVA’s permanent CEO following a Nov. 10 board meeting, TVA’s vice president of financial planning and investor relations Clifton Lowry said on the call.

TVA’s filing also noted that in August, the utility’s board approved 1.5 GW of new natural gas generation, “targeted to be online in 2032.”

As of Sept. 30, TVA said it “had spent $463 million on long-lead-time equipment associated with previously approved projects, which will now be utilized in connection with the project. TVA could spend up to an additional $3.1 billion on this project.”

That previously approved project is TVA’s defunct Middle Tennessee Energy Solution project.

In August, TVA said its approval of the new 1.5 GW gas facility “closes out” the previous project, “and all funding has been transferred to the New Gas Facility 2032.”

TVA’s President and CFO Tom Rice said that the utility is in a “strong financial position” to executive its $13 billion capital investment plan over the next three years. That investment comes on top of the more than $23 billion in investments since 2020, Rice said.

However, TVA’s filing notes that it “continues to experience supply chain pressures resulting from inflation, tariffs and other trade restrictions, material constraints, and labor availability.”

“These factors have contributed to project delays, limited availability of critical materials, and increased costs for both materials and labor,” the utility said. “TVA anticipates that inflationary and tariff pressures will persist into 2027. Although these challenges have been managed with limited disruption to business operations thus far, continued or escalating pressures could result in more substantial operational impacts and increased pressure on power rates.”

#5636. FirstEnergy utilities get approval for $2.5B gas plant in West Virginia

Let Utility Dive's free newsletter keep you informed, straight from your inbox.

Consumer advocates and environmental groups opposed the project, citing concerns over load growth projections and shifting of project risk to ratepayers.

The project was prompted by an unnamed data center customer that would require an initial capacity of 100 MW in 2028, increasing incrementally to 1,012 MW by 2033, according to the commission staff.

A commission analysis found that Mon Power had projected a 2,997 MW summer peak in 2026. In 2028, the company could see a 60 MW capacity shortfall.

That grows to 2,194 MW in 2034 with the addition of new large loads, including data centers. That shortfall comes amid surging capacity prices in the PJM Interconnection has seen in recent years, the commission noted.

Groups like the Sierra Club argued that the utility’s $4 billion rate base would increase by 67%as a result of the project. Mon Power should create large load tariffs to ensure recovery from the customers prompting the electric infrastructure upgrades, the group added.

Anna Sommer, a principal at Energy Futures Group, testified on behalf of the West Virginia Citizen Action Group, Solar United Neighbors and Energy Efficient West Virginia, raising questions on the project’s levelized cost of energy.

“The underdevelopment of the Companies’ CC proposal introduces significant risk of cost increases and schedule delays,” Sommers wrote. The approved gas-fired power plant, called the Maidsville Energy Center, is expected to cost $2.48 billion.

The commission approved the construction surcharge, stating the mechanism had been used before – including for the canceled Mid Atlantic Power Pathway by Pepco Holdings, which ultimately was allowed to recover more than $80 million from ratepayers for the uncompleted project.

The surcharge mechanism would prevent any credit downgrading because of a lack of funds from operations flowing in, Mon Power had argued in its case.

In the “unlikely event that construction ceases or experiences extraordinary delays,” the commission will “initiate an appropriate proceeding to determine the applicable accounting and ratemaking treatment, surcharge or base rate recovery and the timing of such recovery,” the order says.

#5633. LDV eDeliver 5 — A Chinese Bargain?

A bargain electric van from China — the LDV eDeliver 5. “The tide is changing; people are more on board with Chinese cars and electric vehicles” in Australia. And it has 50% more cargo space than the Korean competition (6.6 cubic metres). For the same price!

On the same day that we looked at the Kia PV5, we were also able to check out the LDV eDeliver 5. Chatting to the sales rep (Daniel), we discovered they had sold and delivered the one they had on the lot. Nevertheless, he told us some great stories and we caught up with the van at another dealership down the road later in the week.

The second dealership also sold Geely. The lot was full of them, and the rep told us several times how fast the Geely EX2 and EX5 were selling. Thank you, President Trump, and Supreme Leader Khamenei. Sales of Geely in Australia have doubled since the war started!

Anyway, back to the LDV eDeliver 5. For the curious, LDV stands for Leyland DAF Vans. After the collapse of British Leyland, the brand was sold to SAIC (along with MG). Since the eDeliver 5 launch in July, 77 units have sold in Australia. I felt that the number should be higher, agreeing with the comment found under the PV5 article: “High mileage work vehicles are the first frontier for EVs, not the last. That’s where there is the most financial benefit.” Sadly, though, commercial vans which work so hard around the suburbs, especially near Christmas, have been the slowest segment to electrify in Australia.

Another commentator made the valid point: “Business and fleet vehicles have consistent, predictable daily mileage, high daily mileage, thus huge annual battery utilization (therefore huge fuel savings), they don’t burn a ton of expensive fuel, and are purchased by financially rational decision makers.”

So, why aren’t more electric vans being bought in Australia? Running costs are lower, they are more comfortable, and the sticker price is now below price parity. Daniel and I had a fruitful discussion and came to the conclusion that the main thing stopping electric vans from being taken up in large numbers by fleet operators was the issue of the “fuel card.” It is complicated on the road because of multiple apps and providers, and even more if the van driver charges at home. How do we reimburse for home charging?

Surely this hurdle is not that hard to surmount — especially for contractors and owner operators. We’ll see as fuel insecurity in Australia continues. One owner reported back to Daniel that he has spent AU$3,000 to charge his eDeliver 5 over the past 50,000 km, compared with a diesel fuel bill of AU$14,000 for his old diesel van over the same distance. “Even if I sold the LDV van for nothing after 5 years, I would still be ahead.”

Apparently, some couriers are doing 500 km a day around the Brisbane suburbs. With a range of 320 km from its LFP batteries, the eDeliver 5 would have to top up during the day. Near our home in the northern suburbs of Brisbane, some service stations have installed high-speed chargers (BP Pulse is one example). I very rarely see anyone charging there. Perhaps that’s the charging that will be used by electric delivery vans. LDV also makes larger models, the eDeliver 7 and 9.

Daniel usually only sells two vans a year. He tells me that he has sold five eDeliver 5’s in the past 6 weeks! August to November is their peak time for sales. The tide is indeed changing. The diesel Deliver 5 is AU$10,000–12,000 cheaper than the electric, but with the large kilometres they drive it would not be long before this difference was covered by fuel savings. The eDeliver has V2L capability, putting out 6.6 kWh charge for added convenience. One local customer runs a fridge in the back of the van from the V2L capability.

As an example of the effect of the Iran/USA war, Daniel told us of a customer who bought a new diesel ute 3 weeks before the war started and then came back and wanted to trade it in. He even offered his wife’s car as part of the deal. But the numbers just wouldn’t add up.

Service costs roughly $735 every two years or 30,000 km on the eDeliver 5. Compare this with the Toyota HiAce. The diesel needs a service every 10,000 km. Daniel tells me that during EV service, they check the oil in the gearbox and the coolant for the battery. “Couriers like the power on/off button. A lot of people are screen scared.” The eDeliver 5 has a fob, it is a cheaper option than connectivity.

Of course, a Facebook page has been started. I joined it seeking more real-life experiences.

“I have one that I’ve been driving for a month now. I use it for work and have tools in the back, so the weight of the car is 2.4 tonnes. With driving around town (mostly city and some highway). I get around 250 km before I have to charge again from about 10%.

“It’s a great ride and very comfy. I do maybe 100 km per day, so charge every second or third. Works great for me.”

“There has been a seismic shift in consumer behaviour due to recent geo-political tensions, fuel insecurity, and surging diesel costs,” said Dinesh Chinnappa, the general manager of LDV Australia. “With that in mind we brought forward our plans to introduce the LDV eDeliver 5 to the Australian market, which is why stock will initially be limited.”

You can check out the specs here. Of course it has all the safety features you would expect. For those who like to watch, check out the video review of the eDeliver 7.

There are several small cargo vans now available on the Australian market. I will endeavour to check out a few more and report back to CleanTechnica readers. As we hurtle towards Christmas and the online orders increase, I expect to see many more electric Santas delivering parcels to front doors in the suburbs of Brisbane.

David Waterworth is a retired teacher who divides his time between looking after his grandchildren and trying to make sure they have a planet to live on. He is long on Tesla [NASDAQ:TSLA].

#5630. Elon Musk Interview At The DealBook Summit: Starlink Is Revolutionary And Saves Lives

Elon Musk, the chief executive of SpaceX and Tesla, said at the DealBook Summit recently that “the number of lives that have been saved” by Starlink connectivity “may be hundreds.”

Want to get the correct numbers? Musk asked host Andrew Ross Sorkin, editor of DealBook, a daily business and policy report from The New York Times. “It’s all over the internet. Just do a search.”

It was an ill-placed flippant comment, considering that Ukraine has suffered repeated disruptions to their access to Starshield — the military version of SpaceX’s Starlink.

In an editorial following President Donald J. Trump’s meeting this week with Russian President Putin and Chinese President Xi, former Secretary of State Hillary Clinton reminds readers that, according to Ukrainian President Volodymyr Zelensky, Ukrainian access to US early warning intelligence about incoming Russian missile strikes has been fractured, and Ukraine’s access to Starshield’s service remains limited.

Starshield helps Ukraine shoot down Russian attack drones. Limited access to Starshield means increased Ukrainian deaths as the country battles against Russian aggression — the opposite of Musk’s broad brush claim of lives saved by his technology.

Musk did address restrictions on the Ukrainian military’s access to the SpaceX satellite communications network during the Deal book Summit. He said that Starlink was never meant to be used for offensive strikes in war.

Historian and activist Rebecca Solnit refuses to dismiss Musk’s handling of Ukranian airspace as mere commerce. Elon Musk “has made much of his obscene pile of money off the US government with SpaceX and StarLink,” Solnit outlines. “They have given him power that should never belong to individuals – as demonstrated by his return to manipulating StarLink in ways that impair Ukrainian efforts in the war with Russia.”

Representative Jim Himes (D-CT), the top-ranking Democrat on the House Intelligence Committee, has written to Elon Musk with a formal request “that senior SpaceX officials meet with me to explain the Starshield service disruptions in Ukraine in October 2026.”

Himes notes that SpaceX, Musk’s company, is “a US Government purchased service established via a contract between the U.S. Government and SpaceX.” Himes wants to know “why those changes were made, and whether those changes were made unilaterally by SpaceX or were directed by a US Government official.” Since SpaceX has potentially modified a US Government contract, “combined with its lack of responsiveness to congressional oversight,” Himes argues that “every contract the US Government has with Space X” should be questioned.

Reducing the controversy to everyday terms, Musk insisted during the DealBook Summit that the Starlink design is “revolutionary,” in that it can be placed on a roof. “It solves the problem of reaching people who are in a building.” Sure, gaps in service continue, but Musk has a solution. “The missing piece of the puzzle” to making Starlink ubiquitous is an alternative to a metal roof, he suggested. The solution is “terrestrial… we can establish micro-ground stations… a network that are, maybe, bound together with Starlink terminals. Instead of big cell towers all over the place.”

Elon Musk is one of the richest people in the world, with significant political clout. He controls the direction and pace of one of the most important satellite technologies on which the US and the world depends, as former Secretary of Labor Robert Reich explains. Musk is one of the “authoritarians of the new economy,” Reich encapsulates, and Musk has “unparalleled power over every aspect of the lives of Americans and millions of others around the world.”

Reich adds, “The Democrats should investigate why Elon Musk funneled a half-billion dollars into the 2026 midterms. Was it because he wants to keep his satellite monopoly? His defense contracts?”

Musk has a lot on his mind these days. Sorkin reminded Musk that he had once said that his “mind was like a storm.”

Musk responded that, right now, “I feel relatively at peace.”

Carolyn Fortuna, PhD, is a longtime journalist and researcher for CleanTechnica who frequently writes about climate science, renewable energy, and environmental communication. Since joining the publication in 2016, Fortuna has authored nearly 1,900 articles focusing on ecojustice, clean technology adoption, and the intersection of science and public policy. #5629. US to install 36% more wind turbines in 2025 than previous year

The United States is expected to add more than 7 GW of wind installations in 2025, a 36% increase over the previous year, and the five-year outlook remains unchanged quarter-over-quarter from previous forecast, according to the “U.S. Wind Energy Monitor” report released by Wood Mackenzie and the American Clean Power Association (ACP).

Amid a challenging market environment, the report shows that the United States is on track to add 46 GW of new wind capacity from 2025 to 2029, with total projected volumes unchanged quarter-on-quarter from previous forecasts. However, timing has shifted, as 2026 and 2027 will deliver significant gains, at 10.7 GW and 12.7 GW, respectively, as more assets advance through the development pipeline.

While Q3 installations came in 23% below forecast at 932 MW, the market has shown progress with 3.8 GW queued for Q4 2025 — representing 52% of the year’s total expected capacity. This back-loaded installation pattern is consistent with typical project-commissioning timelines.

U.S. turbine order intake has rebounded to pre-One Big Beautiful Bill Act (OBBBA) levels quarter-over-quarter, supported by 2+ GW of firm commitments in Q3, the biggest intake in the region in the last nine months and a 79% quarter-over-quarter increase. However, true visibility remains limited as OEMs increasingly withhold project details and much qualifying “start-of-construction” activity occurs through off-site component manufacturing.

The market will then see a noticeable drop off, as 2029 weakens meaningfully quarter-over-quarter following project cancellations and inactive designations for late-decade capacity, driven by permitting and broader development challenges.

“The U.S. power market is facing mounting strain after a decade of flat demand, with utilities committing to 160 GW of large-load additions. This represents a significant opportunity for wind energy, which benefits from strengthened economic fundamentals and a compelling business case driven by its competitively low LCOE,” said Leila Garcia da Fonseca, director of research at Wood Mackenzie. “However, turbine costs remain elevated due to tariffs and mid-term wind growth will depend on resolving permitting and policy uncertainty.”

Power demand growth through 2029 is expected to average around 3% compared to just 0.7% over the previous decade, with data centers accounting for approximately 59 GW of the 90 GW total peak demand growth. This surge in baseload demand positions wind as a natural fit to meet rising power needs.

The five-year capacity outlook remains unchanged at 39.8 GW of added capacity quarter-over-quarter. The 2025-2027 pipeline is fully committed with all projects having turbine orders in place. More than 60% of the three-year capacity outlook has been commissioned or is under construction.

Activity is led by western states, such as Wyoming, New Mexico and others, that will account for 34% of activity in this time period. Major projects driving the outlook include Pattern’s 3.5-GW SunZia project in New Mexico, which will position the developer as 2026’s top installer, and Invenergy’s 998-MW Towner Energy Center in Colorado, the largest single project expected in 2027.

The market continues expanding geographically, with Arkansas bringing its first utility-scale onshore wind project online through Cordelio’s Crossover Wind.

The repowering market remains strong, as Wood Mackenzie projects that 18 projects will drive 2.5 GW of capacity additions in the next three years.

Wood Mackenzie expects the offshore installation pace to slow in Q4 2025 due to harsh winter weather conditions, pushing remaining capacity into 2026. Despite these near-term adjustments, Vineyard Wind has demonstrated strong execution, connecting 15 turbines in Q3 and delivering 200 GWh through nine months.

“U.S. offshore wind shows diverging momentum,” said Garcia Da Fonseca. “Projects under construction with COD estimated for 2026 continue to hit key milestones, but post-2027 developments face potential delays amid constrained wind turbine installation vessel capacity, driving delays and contract terminations.”

The offshore sector is also experiencing significant financial pressure.

The report highlights that uncertainty around tariffs is a threat to projections. Wood Mackenzie projects that tariffs will drive up turbine costs in 2026, before moderating in subsequent years. In total, U.S. onshore wind capex is projected to increase by 5% through 2029.

“U.S. wind turbine pricing is experiencing unprecedented uncertainty as conflicting market and regulatory forces interact,” said Garcia Da Fonseca. “Domestic manufacturing overcapacity relative to permitted project volumes, particularly after 2028, would normally place downward pressure on prices. Despite this, onshore wind costs are expected to continue rising due to tariff exposure on raw material inputs and subcomponents.”

#5628. Three Green Hydrogen Projects Slip Through The Trump Chopper

When US President Donald Trump swept into office last year, he set his choppers upon the $8 billion Regional Clean Hydrogen Hubs program, snuffing out plans to establish a diversified, domestic hydrogen market with a focus on decarbonization. Nevertheless, some stakeholders have continued to pursue the dream of producing green hydrogen from renewable resources — instead of squeezing it from natural gas. Now all that hard work is beginning to pay off, Trump or no Trump.

If you guessed the startup Plug Power is in the mix, run right out and buy yourself a cigar. Plug Power began life back in 1997 with a focus on electric forklifts powered by zero emission hydrogen fuel cells. In addition to replacing fossil fuels, Plug Power aimed to replace battery-powered forklifts, with fuel cells being a better fit for the fast-paced environment of shipping and logistics.

Plug Power soon pivoted into replacing gas-derived hydrogen fuel with so-named green hydrogen, pushed from water by an electrical current. After a series of ups and downs the company hit the jackpot in January of 2025, when the US Department of Energy tapped it for a $1.66 loan guarantee in support of the Regional Clean Hydrogen Hubs program, which was funded through the 2021 Bipartisan Infrastructure Law. If all went according to plan, Plug Power would have used the loan to finance up to six green hydrogen projects around the US, all affiliated with the Hydrogen Hubs program.

So much for that. The Energy Department announced the loan award at the beginning of the month, when former President Joe Biden was still in office. However, by the end of the month Trump was officially installed in the White House. He soon pulled the plug on the Plug Power loan, so to speak, sending it down the drain alongside many other federally funded clean power projects.

That was not the end of the story for Plug Power. After all, it’s a big world out there, and Plug Power continued to pitch its wares overseas. Last week the company also relaunched itself back into the US market, bigly.

On November 17, Plug Power announced that its new “GenEco™ Scale” green hydrogen electrolyzer system is up for grabs in the US market while concurrently announcing that its first customer is the Texas-based firm Aracdia eFuels. Arcadia will deploy 280 megawatts’ worth of GenEco Scale modules at its Project ARC campus in the Portland/Corpus Christi area in Texas. When fully up and running, Project ARC will deploy green hydrogen from the GenEco Scale system to produce about 23.2 million gallons of fossil-free aviation fuel per year.

While green hydrogen has its fans in the decarbonization field, the stubbornly high cost of electrolysis has raised a formidable obstacle against widespread commercial uptake. Plug Power is among the stakeholders pitching modular, integrated systems to help bring costs down into parity with conventional hydrogen sourced from natural gas or coal, and that’s where the GenEco system comes into play.

“Built around a standardized 20 MW block architecture, GenEco Scale combines Plug’s proven proton exchange membrane (PEM) electrolyzer technology with integrated balance-of-plant systems into a modular, pre-engineered solution for hydrogen production projects of approximately 100 MW and above,” Plug Power explained in a press statement last week. The company also noted that the “outdoor-ready” system avoids much of the cost and time related to the buildings and other infrastructure required of traditional large scale electrolyzer systems.

Arcadia eFuels CEO Amy Hebert also chipped in her two cents in support of the modular system. “Reducing the engineering burden on our EPC [engineering, procurement, and construction] partners and minimizing the complexity of bringing these systems together were important considerations for Project ARC,” Herbert emphasized.

Savings on land is another part of the cost-cutting plan. Unlike conventional electrolyzer system layouts, the GenEco model deploys a vertical configuration. The vertical system requires less than one acre per 100 megawatts of electrolyzer capacity, according to Plug Power.

Another Plug Power project has bubbled up in California, which was on track to host a suite of projects under the Hydrogen Hubs program with a focus on hydrogen derived from biomass as well as water electrolysis. The funding evaporated under Trump, but the state is still getting its hands on green hydrogen.

On November 19, Plug Power announced that its containerized 5-megawatt GenEco electrolyzer is up and running at a green hydrogen plant in Vernon, California, under the umbrella of the New Jersey-based firm Avina Clean Hydrogen.

Green hydrogen from this project will go to fulfill California’s plans for a zero-emission freight corridor, the plan for which includes fuel cell electric trucks as well as battery-powered trucks. “Located approximately 10 miles from the Port of Long Beach, the facility is designed to serve fleets moving freight through the Los Angeles–Long Beach port complex. It combines on-site fueling with tube-trailer deliveries to customers across the Los Angeles region,” Plug Power explains.

The station, also accommodate buses, and it can refuel a heavy-duty vehicle to more than 95% capacity in less than 30 minutes, according to Plug Power. That stacks up well against charging times for battery-powered heavy duty vehicles. On its website, for example, Tesla asserts that its new Semi electric truck can recover 60% of its 500 mile range in up to 30 minutes at a Tesla Megacharger station.

For the record, Avina has not placed all of its cards on hydrogen fuel cell trucks. The company’s roster of clean hydrogen projects also includes SAF (sustainable aviation fuel) and green ammonia, which is a key input for fertilizer among other chemical products.

Rounding out the trio of new projects is the Swedish firm PowerCell Group. The firm is better known for its work in the marine energy field, and now it is tapping into land-based markets as well. Last week PowerCell announced that its US branch is in motion to set up a green hydrogen system run by excess electricity from an existing solar array owned by a to-be-named client in the US. In effect, the hydrogen will serve as an energy storage medium. It will go to run a PowerCell PowerPod 3000 stationary fuel cell, which will kick into action at night to keep the clean kilowatts flowing after the sun goes down.

PowerCell describes the PowerPod 3000 as “a containerized power generation platform integrating our PS190 systems with power conversion equipment, cooling and DMC, PowerCell’s control and Energy Orchestration technology,” with an installed fuel cell capacity of 27 megawatts and 20 megawatts of rated power.

That’s a good question. Rather than let that excess solar electricity go to waste, the client could invest in a BESS (battery energy storage system) for daytime energy storage and night-time electricity. However, a typical BESS only lasts for a handful of hours. In contrast, PowerCell describes its fuel cell as a “long duration” storage system, which generally means eight hours or more.

PowerCell also points out that a fuel cell does something that a BESS cannot. Fuel cells generate electricity by combining hydrogen with oxygen in the presence of a catalyst. Water — H2O — is a byproduct. All else being equal, that opens up the potential for green hydrogen to serve as a water distribution medium, sucking up water in an electrolysis system at one location, then pushing it out in a fuel cell located elsewhere.

PowerCell is not making any specific promises for this particular project, though the company does emphasize that “the ability to generate both electricity and recoverable water adds another dimension of value to hydrogen-based energy storage.”

“The amount of water recovered, and its potential uses will depend on the final system configuration, operating conditions and water treatment requirements,” the company adds.

And, that’s where things get interesting. The client is a to-be-named electric cooperative, which is of interest because electric cooperatives (more specifically, Rural Electric Cooperatives) account for a large portion of the US grid, and their roots in the rural poverty fighting programs of the Great Depression provide for a distinctive public benefit mission that can, in some cases, allow more wiggle room for innovative energy solutions that generate broad benefits for local communities.

I’m reaching out to PowerCell for more details. If they can’t divulge the name of the client yet, perhaps they’ll be able to describe what they mean by long duration energy storage, so stay tuned for an update.

Photo: The startup Plug Power is among the green hydrogen stakeholders carving out more space for renewable energy in the US market, despite last year’s sharp U-turn in federal energy policy (cropped, courtesy of Plug Power).
