#5627. Pondering The Potential Of Plug-In VPP

I recently discussed the potential of the plug-in VPP from Jackery and Every Electric to transform urban energy storage. But the current program could be just the start. The more I discussed it at RE+, the more it started making me thing about where it could go in the future.

Essentially, customers of the plug-in VPP receive a battery, plug it into the wall and their window air conditioner, and connect to Wi-Fi. Then they use AC as they normally would, the VPP shaves off demand peaks on the grid, and the customer gets paid. It is likely the fastest, easiest and cheapest way to implement urban BESS in the US. And regular people living in apartments benefit. You can read more about the plug-in VPP in my previous article for context. Or you can watch the video below.

Both Jackery and Every Electric are collaborating and working to create innovative solutions for customer needs. Plug-in VPP makes the benefits of battery storage accessible to people in apartments. However, the potential could go much farther. Of note, much of what I am discussing here is not available yet. But the plug-in VPP sparked my imagination, and I wanted to expand on what might be possible.

Initially, the system is being deployed in New York City, which has a large number of apartments that can be aggregated to create a meaningful amount of combined storage. Of course, the potential can be realized in areas outside of NYC. Every Electric is already looking to expand into the Boston area. There are several other areas where the existing system based on window air conditioners in urban areas could apply, particularly along the Northeast corridor. However, as the program evolves, there is tremendous potential outside of the region.

In Jackery’s home state of California, 4.5 TWh of solar and wind power was curtailed in the first half of 2026. More electricity wasted in six months in one state than many developing countries consume in an entire year. Residential solar often feeds into the grid when it just leads to curtailments elsewhere. Even with recent ESS installations, the Duck Curve is still deepening. If home batteries can help “decapitate the duck,” customers and the utility both stand to benefit. And all that renewable energy currently being wasted can contribute to reducing emissions.

In addition, every Jackery battery can operate as a solar generator, with a DC input. People can simply plug in a solar panel or two to recharge the battery. No additional inverter needed. As it does not send energy back into the grid, it also avoids the cost and complication of traditional grid-tied systems. Sending solar power directly to the battery would avoid having excess solar generation fed into the grid that contributes to overcapacity, deepening the back of the duck curve. Demand response could then come from a battery charged largely from solar, rather than from the grid. As the US is still trying to figure out balcony solar regulations, this would let people enjoy many of the benefits of small scale, plug-and-play solar now. No electricians needed.

Of course, when balcony solar regulations are figured out, Jackery has solutions like the SolarVault3 that I saw at IFA Berlin. Potentially, this system could integrate with point of use battery storage to form a kind of hybrid plug-in system. In the process, solar power could be utilized when it does the most for the grid and offers the greatest potential payback for the customer.

There is also a lot of potential for this concept to work outside of the US. In many parts of the world, most people live in apartments. As the planet warms, world cooling needs recently overtook heating for the first time. As the VPP is monitored via a Wi-Fi connection, there is no need to upgrade to a smart meter to take advantage of the capability. As the storage is already distributed, there is also reduced need to provide the grid upgrades to distribute the energy stored in a large battery during peak energy demand.

Currently, the plug-in VPP is only working with one model of battery in one application. That makes the deployment and scaling of the plug-in VPP easier. Large urban areas offer the clearest path to aggregate the storage needed for a plug-in VPP to work.

However, Jackery has sold over 8 million units over the past 15 years. This has established a level of customer trust and a reputation for reliability. Many of those portable power stations and solar generators can connect to Wi-Fi. That is potentially GWh of deployable storage.

In addition, portable power stations are very popular now. Many Jackery models are selling out. Preparedness is a major driver. Climate-related disasters amplify the fear and uncertainty. However, the batteries that people are buying to be prepared for emergencies offer far greater potential capability. The more batteries that are out there, the greater the potential.

As the software and capability of the VPP develop, multiple batteries have the potential to connect to amplify value, even if that capability isn’t being offered yet. Those batteries, in coordination with a VPP, could earn people money. Currently, the system is being run with one battery model, but Jackery offers a wide range of batteries with Wi-Fi connectivity that might be able to participate in the future. Once the program has proven itself with the initial application in urban areas, there is potential for expansion.

As people start earning money, even if they purchased the battery out of preparedness concerns, they would also start to see the broader benefit. When they gain both confidence and financial benefit from battery storage and renewable energy, they will tend to rationalize that benefit. That can lead to a broader shift in mindset. When you try to start with a rational argument, especially when that argument involves added costs, people are less likely to be supportive than if you start with the benefit.

Many of the pieces to make this happen have already existed. People have the ability to program Jackery batteries through the app to charge and discharge during certain parts of the day. This can work with TOU utility plans, where available. But it doesn’t take full advantage of the capabilities of a VPP. Customers do not get full credit for the storage capability. The true innovation comes from putting the pieces together, between the Jackery hardware, Every Electric software and the utility. If one of those pieces of the collaboration was missing, a plug-in VPP wouldn’t have the same potential.

One of the things that has impressed me about Jackery since I was first introduced to the company was their focus on solving customer problems. However, to create solutions, they also recognize that collaboration is often necessary. I already saw this with the flexible solar panels of SunBooster at IFA Berlin for balcony solar applications, and then again with Every Electric for plug-in VPP.

I asked Jackery founder and CEO Jack Sun about the value of collaboration in creating an environmentally friendly ecosystem:

This is also very important, because we have an open model, and we are more willing and more innovative…

We will actively collaborate with partners in areas such as power chips and more proactively in co-creation. We aim to accelerate the application of their innovations into our solar generator solutions. Our goal is to achieve faster and more efficient solar energy development through joint innovation. The generator can be installed in every household to help more families achieve energy independence more cost-effectively, while also reducing carbon emissions more quickly.

This focus on collaboration makes meaningful innovation happen. Just as I speculated here on the potential of plug-in VPP, other people will have their own ideas. Some will be different than this VPP concept. Many will be better ideas than the ideas I can come up with. Having innovative companies like Jackery and Every Electric actively seeking to work with partners creates potential for those ideas to come together. I look forward to seeing what new solutions come together next.

Larry lives in Queens, NY, with his wife and cats. While he has spent much of his professional career leading global marketing efforts, his passions focus on clean technology and the automotive industry. He believes in Green Free Trade and the potential for the application of engineering talent to create solutions to the world’s most challenging problems.

#5625. Groups Respond to Army’s Plan to Proceed with Lead Azide Detonation in Iowa

Army says it plans to detonate degraded lead in Iowa town no later than Nov. 24, despite court stay.

Des Moines, IOWA — The Iowa Environmental Council, Sierra Club, and Earthjustice will continue to advocate for accountability from the U.S. Environmental Protection Agency (EPA) and the U.S. Army in the planned detonation of lead azide in Middletown, Iowa. Early Sunday morning, the groups filed declarations with the court that call out the Army’s inadequate public notice and information, and question the Army’s and EPA’s claims that all alternatives were given appropriate consideration.

The U.S. Department of Justice (DOJ) filed a statement with the court late on Saturday, Nov. 21, indicating that the Army intends to invoke emergency provisions of the Resource Conservation and Recovery Act (RCRA) to detonate 9,773.5 pounds of lead azide stored at the Iowa Army Ammunitions Plant, without a permit from the EPA. According to the DOJ’s court filings, the Army intends to do so no later than Nov. 24. On Nov. 20, the Eighth Circuit Court of Appeals granted Earthjustice’s request to put on hold a permit allowing the Army to detonate.

“People across numerous impacted communities speaking up and demanding accountability is the only reason there was an opportunity to pause and think about better ways to approach the lead azide problem,” said Earthjustice Attorney Thien Chau. “Yet despite claiming public safety is its top priority and public communication is essential to trust, the Army seemed more concerned over the weekend with finding a way to sidestep the judicial process than on meeting the public’s demands for greater transparency, notice to affected communities, and far stronger protection of people, property, and the environment from serious harm.”

This emergency is of the Army’s own making. The Army has not publicly provided details about why the lead azide was not disposed of before it became so dangerous. The Army’s own storage and inspection practices allowed a primary explosive to sit until it reportedly degraded to the point that any alternative disposal method is now deemed too dangerous. The lack of transparency raises real questions about the scope and extent of potential impacts.

“The new information from the DOJ indicates that the problem is significantly worse than the Army originally shared. It appears that detonation may be, at this point, the only feasible option due to how long this problem was allowed to persist. The public deserves to know immediately which communities will be impacted and how residents can most effectively protect themselves and mitigate impacts on their health and homes,” said Michael Schmidt, General Counsel for the Iowa Environmental Council.

The Army knew about this stockpile of dangerous munitions waste for years, and it knew since at least March that the material was becoming unstable. Yet, just one week ago, most Iowans — including those directly in the path of a toxic lead plume, the Iowa governor, and key state agencies and officials — were unaware the Army intended to detonate nearly five tons of lead azide.

The environmental groups argue the EPA and Army permitting documents have not demonstrated, nor provided appropriate documentation that all other safer alternatives to mitigate the material were thoroughly explored.

“The Army has clearly mishandled this situation, and Iowans and residents in our neighboring states deserve a full review of all of the missteps that led us to this point. However, if detonation is now the only option, it is imperative that everyone impacted has access to the full range of testing, mitigation, and remediation resources,” said Wally Taylor, Attorney and Legal Chair for the Sierra Club Iowa Chapter.

As of 11 a.m. CST on Sunday, Nov. 22, the Army has failed to provide the public with updated weather modeling and mapping showing the path of the lead dust plume, which means people impacted have little time to take precautions to protect their families and homes. The wind patterns have shifted significantly, which means that potential community exposures have likely changed without visible effort by the Army to provide updated information to the public.

The Sierra Club is America’s largest and most influential grassroots environmental organization, with millions of members and supporters. In addition to protecting every person's right to get outdoors and access the healing power of nature, the Sierra Club works to promote clean energy, safeguard the health of our communities, protect wildlife, and preserve our remaining wild places through grassroots activism, public education, lobbying, and legal action. For more information, visit www.sierraclub.org.

#5624. Bright Horizon utility lands Microsoft data center deals amid Wyoming buildout

Let Utility Dive's free newsletter keep you informed, straight from your inbox.

Cheyenne Light — with a 439-MW peak load record — is working on a 3-GW near-term data center pipeline, with an additional 2.5 GW in emerging opportunities, per company officials.

Cheyenne Light, Fuel and Power Co. has entered into agreements to build distribution and transmission infrastructure to serve three Microsoft data centers planned for Wyoming, the company said Tuesday in a filing at the Federal Energy Regulatory Commission.

The redacted agreements require Microsoft to pay for the infrastructure through “contribution in aid of construction” payments, according to the filing.

Cheyenne Light — a subsidiary of Bright Horizon Energy Corp., a utility holding company created by the Nov. 13 merger of Black Hills Corp. and NorthWestern Energy Group — doesn’t believe FERC has jurisdiction over the retail agreements but filed them for review “out of an abundance of caution” due to the agency’s recent statements related to cost recovery agreements for transmission costs caused by large load customers.

The Microsoft agreements reflect a massive data center buildout that is underway in southeast Wyoming where Cheyenne Light operates.

The utility is executing on a 3-GW data center pipeline, including 600 MW of Microsoft and Meta projects, Black Hills officials said during a Nov. 5 earnings conference call. It is also in initial talks on an additional 2.5 GW of early-stage data center opportunities.

Cheyenne Light has about 46,000 retail electric customers in the Cheyenne area and set a peak load record of 439 MW in July, according to Black Hills’ most recent U.S. Securities and Exchange Commission earnings report.

The utility in September agreed to supply a Google data center project in Cheyenne with 590 MW and manage 2.1 GW of third-party power supply in a microgrid, Black Hills said in its earnings presentation.

The agreements with Google contain cost pass-through mechanisms that protect customers from costs related to the planned data center while protecting utility returns from inflation, interest rate volatility and other cost pressures, Black Hills noted in the SEC filing.

Cheyenne Light intends to serve the data centers through its Large Power Contract Service tariff and a proposed Large Customer Transmission Cost Adjustment Mechanism, which are designed to insulate the utility’s customers from the infrastructure costs related to data centers. Black Hills expects the Wyoming Public Service Commission will make a decision on the transmission tariff proposal by mid-2027.

Cheyenne Light plans to use the transmission tariff to recover the costs of two recently proposed transmission projects totaling $686 million needed to serve large loads, including the Google data center. Under the tariff, large load customers would pay their share of the transmission expansion.

The Google data center is set to begin operating late next year and ramp up to its full load by the end of 2030, according to the SEC filing. To serve it, Cheyenne Light plans to build a $1.8 billion, utility-owned 564-MW gas-fired power plant between 2027 and 2029, through an unregulated affiliate. The company expects the deal with Google will boost its net income by $150 million a year starting in 2030.

In its SEC filing, Black Hills warned that the Google deal increases its risk by making it more reliant on one customer.

“Our agreements with Google represent a significant long-term growth opportunity and are expected to contribute meaningfully to future revenues, earnings, cash flows and capital investment,” the company said. “As a result, we will have increased exposure to a single large customer and to trends affecting the data center, cloud computing and artificial intelligence industries.”

Also, Black Hills said that large load demand forecasts face “significant uncertainty” and could be affected by technological advancements, economic conditions and regulatory developments.

“Although the agreements contain customer credit support, termination payment and other investment recovery provisions, those protections may not fully mitigate the impact of reduced customer demand, contract disputes, non-renewal or customer default,” Black Hills said.

#5623. Petrostates’ Chokehold on COP31 Underscores Need for Rapid Electrification, Says T&E

COP31 climate talks break down shows that fossil fuel interests remain a major obstacle to climate action.

The lack of a final text at the end of COP31 shows how far the fossil fuel industry is willing to go to keep its polluting businesses operating, says T&E. While the final text of COP28, in 2023, called for ‘transitioning away from fossil fuels in the energy system’, fossil-producing countries have blocked similar language in subsequent COPs despite the growing economic and social costs of climate change.

Antony Froggatt, senior director at T&E, said: “In a year marked by record oil prices and extreme temperatures, fossil fuel companies moved to block any agreement calling for a phase-out of oil, gas, and coal. They did so to protect their commercial interests even as solar and wind already power the majority of the grid in countries worldwide, and electric vehicles make up nearly half of new car sales in the world’s largest auto market.”

This is not the end of the international processes, and the Paris Agreement remains in place. The next COP will take place in Ethiopia, and the phase-out of fossil fuels will once again be discussed. In the meantime, the Santa Marta process, which grew out of COP30’s failure to agree on a fossil fuel roadmap, will once again meet, and it is expected that countries representing one-third of the world’s economy will debate and develop practical ways to move away from coal, oil and gas

Analysis by T&E at the beginning of the week shows that EVs displace five times more oil globally than they did five years ago. Battery-electric vehicles already displace 1.2 million barrels of oil a day and avoid more than 200 Mt of CO2-equivalent a year.

“Petrostates’ chokehold on COP31 underscores the need for the progressive majority to continue pushing for international co-operation and global targets on electrification that can accelerate decarbonisation while improving energy security, air quality and economic prosperity,” added Antony Froggatt.

“The same oil shock that delivered record profits for the oil industry has pushed up costs for the world’s most vulnerable countries, most of which import their fuel. Governments should make the polluter pay. Excess profits should be heavily taxed, and the revenue used to fund electrification and climate resilience where it is needed most,” he concludes.

Transport & Environment’s (T&E) vision is a zero-emission mobility system that is affordable and has minimal impacts on our health, climate and environment. Created over 30 years ago, we have shaped some of Europe’s most important environmental laws.

#5622. What is holding back bidirectional EV charging as a grid resource?

Let Utility Dive's free newsletter keep you informed, straight from your inbox.

Costs remain high and tech solutions are incomplete, but experts say careful utility program design and a new generation of vehicle charging solutions will bring value to both consumers and the grid.

Late last month, Ford confirmed that its midsize electric pickup, Fathom, will be able to deliver more than 10 kW of alternating-current power to a home when it hits the market next year.

Though it’s the sort of technical detail that may be lost on drivers more concerned with sticker price, styling or battery range, experts say it’s potentially game-changing for utilities hoping to harness the vast quantities of energy stored in hundreds of thousands of bidirectional-capable EVs on U.S. roads.

Drivers of EVs that can discharge AC power don’t need to install pricey, code-sensitive inverter equipment to back up their homes or send power back into the grid, said David Almeida, director of clean energy transportation at Pacific Gas & Electric Co. A Ford spokesperson told Utility Dive that the automaker’s pre-Fathom vehicle-to-home backup package retails for more than $5,000, not including professional installation. The Fathom’s package will cost up to about 60% less, they said.

“You take that cost from the customer [and reduce the] complexity of installation, and you make it a lot easier for them to take this option and … for the utility to tap into that,” Almeida said.

More accessible equipment is not the only precondition for bidirectional-capable EVs to realize their full potential, however. Utilities, regulators and automakers will also need to coordinate on enabling regulation, utility program design, product development and customer education, experts tell Utility Dive.

Automakers sell more than 20 bidirectional-capable light-duty EV models in the United States. Nearly all are “grid-parallel,” meaning they have the technical capability to both back up a home and export power to the grid, said Zach Woogen, executive director of the Vehicle-Grid Integration Council.

Some of these can be described as mass-market rides. The Chevrolet Equinox EV’s base trim retails for just over $35,000, about $16,000 less than the average cost of a new vehicle in 2026.

But nearly all require external inverters to convert their batteries’ direct-current power to AC flows that homes can use, Almeida said. Before this fall, the slow-selling Tesla Cybertruck — with a sticker price north of $80,000 — was the only AC-capable bidirectional EV on U.S. roads. Tesla began accepting orders in October for AC-capable versions of its more affordable Model 3 and Y vehicles, but drivers will still need a Powerwall home backup battery at an installed cost of around $12,000.

Low AC-capable vehicle stock is one reason why PG&E, which has more EVs than any other U.S. utility in its Northern California territory and aims to support 2 million bidirectional-capable vehicles by 2030, has seen limited uptake so far for its vehicle-to-everything program, Almeida said.

“We are still in the hundreds” of enrolled vehicles, including commercial vans, trucks and buses, he said.

PG&E provides up to $3,000 in upfront incentives for eligible residential customers to install bidirectional charging equipment at home, thousands less than the full cost of a DC-to-AC conversion package with installation.

Another reason is that, cost aside, installing a home DC-to-AC inverter is not as simple as mounting and wiring a new light fixture.

Some inverter models “take up quite a bit of space on the wall” and, at least in PG&E’s territory, must meet building codes requiring minimum setbacks from a home’s gas meter, Almeida said. That gives a homeowner with a tight garage or carport the difficult choice of moving their gas meter — if that’s even possible — or forgoing vehicle-to-home capability, he said.

“It’s not just that it’s costly,” Almeida said. “Any time you add more complexity, it’s going to make it that much less likely that the customer is going to do it.”

While disclaiming inside knowledge of automakers’ plans, Almeida said Tesla’s and Ford’s recent moves suggest other automakers are working on designs for AC-capable vehicles. New car designs can take five years or longer to move from concept to showroom, he said.

Ford and Tesla offer “a good signal of what the market is going to be,” he said. “I’d imagine that … other major automakers have [onboard AC capabilities] in the plan and they’re just not ready to share with us.”

At Ford’s invitation, PG&E in July teamed up with American Electric Power, BC Hydro, Consumers Energy and Exelon to develop what Dave McCreadie, Ford’s director of energy and grid services, called “scalable interconnection processes” ahead of Fathom’s 2027 release.

In an October blog post, McCreadie said the five utilities aligned on three key principles: clarifying when interconnection regulations should apply to bidirectional EVs, and specifically to require interconnection only for customers operating a vehicle in parallel with the grid rather than for charging or “isolated backup power;” updating interconnection procedures and/or regulations to accommodate newer standards, such as AC-capable vehicles; and maintaining collaboration between utilities, regulators and equipment manufacturers to reduce barriers to interconnection.

The working group was “a head start” for electric utilities in places where it expects the Fathom to sell well, Almeida said. After the midsize truck arrives, “it’s totally possible that we will see thousands of vehicles that have this kind of capability” on PG&E’s system, he added.

“We’ve been kind of in the ‘crawl’ stage. In the next year or two we’re going to be in the ‘walk’ stage before we run,” he said.

Bidirectional charging is one component of a portfolio of distributed resources that PG&E, with encouragement from state regulators, can use to get more value from an increasingly constrained grid, Almeida said.

“How can we tap into this technology to mitigate the need for us to build infrastructure to help save all of our customers money,” he said, paraphrasing a question PG&E has kicked around internally and in regulatory filings for years.

Electric vehicles play into other PG&E flexibility initiatives. Most customers participating in Flex Connect, its flexible interconnection program for larger distribution-connected loads, are commercial EV-charging sites, a PG&E manager responsible for the program told Utility Dive earlier this year. PG&E also serves more than 70 bidirectional-capable electric school buses in Oakland, California, which has one of the country’s biggest fleets today. It plans to add infrastructure for more than 230 electric buses over the next two years across three sites in San Francisco, a particularly constrained load pocket.

Utilities, regulators and state energy offices elsewhere see value in private and commercial EVs.

In New England, residential and business customers with enrolled thermostats or stationary batteries have for years taken advantage of the region’s ConnectedSolutions virtual power plant program, which pays battery owners upwards of $200/kW-year to participate in both traditional peak-shaving and more targeted local events. This year, the Massachusetts Clean Energy Center deployed dozens of fully subsidized bidirectional chargers to bring electric vehicles into the fold. One Highland Electric Fleets bus earned nearly $12,000 per summer in a two-year pilot earlier this decade, though “more like $6,000 per year is the norm” moving forward, company CEO Duncan McIntyre told Utility Dive.

Still, even after accounting for equipment installation costs, “that is not hard to make pencil — it is a lot of money if you’re able to show up” for called events, Woogen said.

Regional regulators keep upping the ante. Next month, the Connecticut Public Utilities Regulatory Authority is set to expand the state’s energy storage compensation framework to include commercial EV charging sites. The program would pay $325/kW-year for the first five years of enrollment and $175/kW-year for the next five.

The proposed decision would also clarify bidirectional EV interconnection standards, including for residential customers. But residential EVs aren’t yet eligible for demand-response compensation in Connecticut, highlighting the challenge of bringing regulation in line with technical capability, Woogen said.

In fact, only California and Maryland have adopted vehicle-to-grid interconnection standards for AC-capable EVs, according to Woogen’s group.

“In some cases, technology and customer interest are ahead of the market and regulatory capability to support [them]. That is the long pole in the tent — it’s very fragmented, and that tends to slow things down,” said Greg Guthridge, global power and utilities customer experience transformation leader for EY.

That said, utilities and their partners may also need to work on “consumer interest” through education, incentives and innovative program design, Guthridge said. Recent EY market research found that only 18% of U.S. customers have an “immediate interest” in energy-flexibility services, a broad category that includes bidirectional EV charging, he said.

“We have a bit of a challenge here. … We need to convince consumers that there’s value. Either they don't understand that or they're not buying into [it] just yet,” Guthridge said.

EY’s research suggests customers will be more receptive to “packaged” bidirectional charging programs that meet certain criteria, Guthridge said.

One is financial benefit: generous compensation for participation, ideally with an upfront component so customers feel they’re benefiting early. Control over or at least visibility into a program is another. Customers want to understand what’s happening in their home — and in this case, to their vehicle — and be looped into the process, Guthridge said. They also want participation that isn’t inconvenient or otherwise detrimental to their lifestyle or business activities, he said.

For inspiration, utilities and their automaker partners can look to companies like Rheem, which sells water heaters that play a significant role in some utilities’ distributed resource portfolios and has a reputation for “working with the utility closely to allow a seamless, effortless adoption process,” Guthridge said.

In that light, Ford seems to be making a shrewd move to get key utility partners onboard before Fathom hits the road.

“Automakers have an incentive to make the process as beneficial as possible for customers,” Guthridge said.

Voltus CEO Dana Guernsey compared new “bring your own new capacity” contracts to the power purchase agreements that “revolutionized” utility-scale solar and wind development.

#5621. Trump admin ‘pauses’ leases for five offshore wind projects already under construction

The U.S. Dept. of the Interior has immediately paused the leases for all large-scale offshore wind projects under construction in the United States due to “national security risks identified by the Dept. of War in recently completed classified reports.”

“The prime duty of the United States government is to protect the American people,” said Secretary of the Interior Doug Burgum. “Today’s action addresses emerging national security risks, including the rapid evolution of the relevant adversary technologies, and the vulnerabilities created by large-scale offshore wind projects with proximity near our East Coast population centers. The Trump administration will always prioritize the security of the American people.”

The Dept. of the Interior claims that the blade movement and reflective towers create “radar interference called ‘clutter.'” Allegedly, this is a national security risk.

Dominion Energy released the following statement in response to the pause issued for the Coastal Virginia Offshore Wind project:

“The Coastal Virginia Offshore Wind Project (CVOW) is essential for American national security and meeting Virginia’s dramatically growing energy needs, the fastest growth in America. This growth is driven by the need to provide reliable power to many of America’s most important war fighting installations, the world’s largest warship manufacturer, and the largest concentration of data centers on the planet as well as the leading edge of the AI revolution.

Stopping CVOW for any length of time will threaten grid reliability for some of the nation’s most important war fighting, AI and civilian assets. It will also lead to energy inflation and threaten thousands of jobs.

CVOW is American-owned and benefits all of our Virginia customers. Our customers are paying for the project after a careful review of project costs and benefits by Virginia state regulators in 2022. These same state regulators, along with numerous federal agencies, oversee our cyber and physical security program, which is among the strongest in the energy industry.

The project has been more than 10 years in the works, involved close coordination with the military, and is located 27 to 44 miles offshore, so far offshore it does not raise visual impact concerns. The project’s two pilot turbines have been operating for five years without causing any impacts to national security.

CVOW enjoys bipartisan support and is within months of generating a massive 2,600 MW to support the fastest growing part of America’s energy grid. This growth serves the largest concentration of critical infrastructure in the world.

Virginia’s All-American, All-Of-The-Above-Energy Plan requires a range of power generation assets, including natural gas, advanced nuclear, and renewables. Virginia needs every electron we can get as our demand for electricity doubles. These electrons will power the data centers that will win the AI race, support our war fighters, and build the nuclear warships needed to maintain our maritime supremacy. Virginia’s grid needs addition of electrons, not subtraction.

We stand ready to do what is necessary to get these vital electrons flowing as quickly as possible.”

#5620. Barcelona Is Suffering From The Hydrogen Transport Death Spiral

On November 21, Barcelona had at least 23 of its 46 hydrogen buses out of service because the plant supplying them had stopped producing fuel. Transports Metropolitans de Barcelona (TMB) reported a 2.5% to 3.8% reduction in scheduled bus service, with waits on some routes rising from seven to nine minutes. The buses were available to carry passengers. The hydrogen was not. ARA and La Vanguardia reported the disruption.

The problem runs through a single industrial fuel supply. TMB has a ten-year, €14.5 million hydrogen contract with Iberdrola. Alternative deliveries from Tarragona have fallen short, disrupted by industrial action and restrictions on hydrogen transport. The contract provides for compensation when fuel is not delivered. It cannot make up the missed service.

La Vanguardia connected the Barcelona stoppage with Cummins’ recommendation that customers stop using affected electrolysers following a safety review. Iberdrola cited a valve-system problem at the plant. Cummins’ November quarterly filing confirmed the September HyLYZER stop-use recommendation and said no formal recall had been initiated. TMB now has to assess the price of safely restarting fuel production and the reliability of the system it would be restarting.

Barcelona has not decided to retire the hydrogen buses. But the agency has a more important decision than repairing a plant: whether it should commit public money to keeping this fuel system in service for the remainder of the buses’ lives. Other European cities have faced that choice and walked away.

Aberdeen paid approximately £13.9 million for 25 hydrogen double-deckers that entered service in 2021. Hydrogen supply problems stranded the fleet in July 2024. After prolonged efforts to restore the operation, the city decided in February 2026 to dispose of the buses. A proposed sale would bring in £30,000 each for 23 buses going to First Bus and £63,000 each for two going to GeoPura. The total expected proceeds are £816,000, just 5.9% of the original vehicle expenditure. My September Aberdeen resale assessment examined the proposed terms and the conversion economics. First Bus has also considered battery-electric conversion.

Liverpool chose conversion. Ten of its 20 hydrogen buses had never carried passengers; the other ten had completed only 450 passenger-carrying journeys. In December 2025 the Liverpool City Region Combined Authority decided to convert all 20 to battery-electric operation, with the supplier covering the conversion costs. The buses had years of potential mechanical life, but the hydrogen supply arrangements had not delivered a usable transport service.

A hydrogen bus can become an uneconomic asset long before it wears out. Refuelling plants, fuel deliveries, maintenance specialists, software, fuel-cell modules and replacement parts have to remain available. As hydrogen fleets retire and new orders thin out, each remaining operator has fewer other customers sharing those support costs. Rising costs and uncertain service then encourage another operator to exit. That is the hydrogen transport death spiral. Battery-electric buses draw on a much larger manufacturing, charging and maintenance market.

The pressures already extend through the supply chain. Cummins has stopped seeking new commercial electrolyser business while customers contend with its HyLYZER stop-use recommendation. Quantron’s insolvency disrupted confidence in specialist vehicle support. Alstom acquired Cummins’ relevant rail fuel-cell operations to retain the staff and expertise needed to fulfil long-term contracts. Each case transfers more risk to organizations that have already bought hydrogen equipment.

New Flyer still sells hydrogen buses, but the North American order book is thin. Publicly identified hydrogen-bus orders numbered 288 in 2023, 96 in 2024 and no more than 45 gross in 2025, with about 15 in a stricter reconstruction. Another 38 buses appear in 2026 procurement decisions across differing approval stages. These are small volumes for a specialist vehicle line that needs engineering, spares and continuing product development.

Ballard is still developing bus fuel cells. Its November 5 Solaris announcement describes a new engine entering series production. Its GeoPura acquisition also shifts business attention toward integrated power systems and recurring energy-service revenue. My Ballard–New Flyer framework assessment found that the March agreement covering 500 modules is not an order for 500 buses. For a transit operator, the crucial test is a binding, affordable commitment to parts, diagnostics and maintenance throughout the vehicles’ remaining service lives.

TMB should compare the cost of safely restarting the hydrogen plant and running the buses with the cost of delivering the same passenger service through replacement vehicles. That includes the repair bill, future fuel contracts, station maintenance, spare parts, service interruptions, replacement buses, depot charging and any temporary vehicles required to keep routes operating. TMB already runs battery-electric buses and has local operating data to test a replacement plan.

A modest repair may justify running the existing hydrogen buses while alternatives are prepared. A major refurbishment, fresh fuel commitments and another decade of specialist support are a different investment. Aberdeen chose disposal. Liverpool chose conversion. Barcelona needs to decide what delivers dependable bus service at the lowest credible forward cost, without treating the original hydrogen spending as money it can recover.

My full TFIE Strategy Briefing assessment examines the Barcelona interruption, the Aberdeen and Liverpool exits, and the contracting support market. The paid section sets out how transit boards can compare restarting, bridging and retiring an existing hydrogen fleet.

#5619. Less Intensive Follow-Up of Lung Nodules May Result in Larger Cancers

— Study suggests a return to more intensive recommendations for follow-up of small nodules

A less intensive approach to monitoring newly detected small lung nodules failed to meet noninferiority criteria compared with a more intensive strategy, and may result in larger cancers at diagnosis, a cluster randomized pragmatic trial showed.

Among the patients with lung cancer, 36.3% of those in the less intensive group had cancers larger than 20 mm at diagnosis compared with 27.7% in the more intensive group, reported Michael K. Gould, MD, of the Kaiser Permanente Bernard J. Tyson School of Medicine in Pasadena, California, and colleagues.

After adjustment, size progression to larger than 20 mm was 7 percentage points (95% CI -2.0 to 15.9) more common in the less intensive group than the more intensive group, which exceeded the prespecified noninferiority margin of 5 percentage points, but was not statistically significant.

"Although neither group was clearly superior in the main analysis, we cautiously advise guideline developers to consider returning to the prior, more intensive recommendations for follow-up of small nodules, particularly in younger persons," the authors wrote in the Annals of Internal Medicine.

Indeed, a subgroup analysis showed that tumor progression was significantly more likely among younger patients in the less intensive group.

"We weren't able to validate the less intensive approach reflected in the current guidelines -- we weren't able to demonstrate noninferiority," Gould told MedPage Today.

In addition, "progression in size and stage are more common than one would think, perhaps suggesting that the whole idea of a CT follow-up strategy be reconsidered," he said. "When we see one-third of patients having progression, we need to do better."

Given the frequency of size progression in both groups, "newer approaches to follow-up for patients with small pulmonary nodules, including volumetric measurement, novel biomarkers, and radiomic tools, should be rigorously evaluated," Gould and team wrote.

The "Watch the Spot" trial enrolled 34,686 participants at 14 healthcare systems between March 2017 and July 2019. Mean age was 64.5, and 50.8% were women. All had a newly identified pulmonary nodule measuring 15 mm or less in diameter on a diagnostic or screening chest CT scan, defined as the index scan.

The protocols for less and more intensive CT follow-up were modeled on current and prior recommendations from the Fleischner Society and the Lung CT Screening Reporting and Data System (Lung-RADS).

Lung cancer was diagnosed within 27 months of nodule identification in 1.5% of those in the less intensive group and 1.4% of those in the more intensive group.

The median nodule size on the index CT scan was 9 mm, and 70% of all cancers were detected incidentally. More than 25% of lung cancers were diagnosed in participants with nodules measuring 6 mm or less in diameter at the time of detection on the index CT scan.

Median tumor size at diagnosis was 16 mm in the less intensive group compared with 15 mm in the more intensive group, and stage III or IV cancer was somewhat more common in the less intensive group (31.4%) than in the more intensive group (28.6%).

Adherence to protocol-based follow-up was "poor" in both study groups, the authors observed -- 42% in the less intensive group and 45% in the more intensive group. After adjustment among these patients, diagnosis with progression to a tumor size larger than 20 mm was more common in the less intensive group by 14.9 percentage points.

"Adherence is important," Gould observed. "While we're waiting for newer approaches -- whether it's volumetric measurement or novel biomarkers -- the poor adherence we observed in our study represents a very important opportunity for quality improvement."

#5618. DTE Energy plans up to 150 megawatts of Michigan-based solar generation

DTE Energy recently announced plans to add up to 150 megawatts of new solar energy in Michigan, a move that will create construction jobs, support local communities, and save customers money on fuel costs, the company said.

In addition, because solar generation does not require fuel, the new projects will help reduce the company’s exposure to changing fuel prices over their operating lives. The projects will also support DTE’s work to provide customers with energy that is reliable, increasingly clean, and affordable.

The company stated that these projects are an investment in Michigan and its communities. By producing more Michigan-made energy that doesn’t require the purchase  of fuel, it can help lower energy costs for customers and reduce the reliance on fuel from outside the state. At the same time, these projects will create jobs and bring new investment and tax revenue into local communities, providing more resources for schools, first responders and the services Michigan families count on every day.

DTE is a producer of and investor in renewable energy in Michigan, generating enough clean energy from wind and solar to power nearly one million homes. DTE’s 2026 Integrated Resource Plan builds on that commitment, calling for an additional 15 gigawatts of renewable energy by 2042.

#5617. Adani Cement to Deploy 410 Electric Trucks, Cement & Clinker

Ahmedabad, India — Adani Cement, part of the Adani Portfolio and one of India’s leading building materials solutions providers, flagged off Heavy-Duty Electric Trucks at its Sanghi Plant in Gujarat. This is the first-ever deployment of EV trucks by Adani Cement and a defining milestone in the company’s sustainability and green logistics journey.

Mr. Vinod Bahety, CEO of Cement Business, Adani Group, said, “The deployment at Sanghi is just the beginning. We have identified multiple routes across India for EV-based transport of clinker and cement. Every truck we electrify reduces our carbon footprint and insulates our logistics cost base from diesel price volatility. This is smart sustainability—it benefits the people, the planet, and the balance sheet. This is our commitment in action—to build a greener, more competitive cement company that serves India’s growth while protecting its future.”

The economic case for heavy-duty EV trucks in India’s cement sector has reached a structural tipping point. Over the past decade, diesel prices rose 60%, while Li-ion battery pack costs fell by approximately 70% and solar tariffs declined 47%.

Diesel trucks emit approximately 0.7375 kg CO₂ per km and when powered by renewable energy, EV trucks can eliminate up to 100% of these freight emissions, making logistics electrification a powerful lever for Scope 3 decarbonization. Adani Cement’s 410-truck fleet is projected to reduce over 25,000 tonnes of CO₂ annually, displacing the equivalent of 11 million litres of diesel per year, with plans to expand across additional routes and plants.

India is the world’s second-largest cement producer, and the movement of limestone, clinker, and finished cement across vast supply chains is an essential, but carbon-intensive, part of the sector’s operations. Adani Cement’s EV fleet deployment addresses both the cost and climate dimensions of this challenge directly.

#5616. COP31 Plants A Big Wet Kiss On Failed Carbon Capture Technology

COP31, the annual UN climate conference that took place this month, ended on a sour note after negotiators were unable to reach an agreement to include language calling for a transition away from fossil fuels in the final communiqué  — language that was included two years ago at the conclusion of COP 29 in Dubai.

How is it possible to explain such backsliding at a time when a Kelvin wave — a wall of unusually hot water — is impacting the coast of every country along the eastern edge of the Pacific Ocean? Authorities say unusually high temperatures all across Europe led to the premature death of more than 50,000 people last summer. In addition, huge swaths of the Earth’s land mass are experiencing either severe drought or punishing floods. How can anybody ignore the events happening before their very eyes?

You know the answer — MONEY! According to Kathryn McCallum, the director of strategy at the Climate Action Network Australia, there were 624 delegates from the 14 Pacificc Small Island Developing States at the conference in Turkey this month and 700 paid lobbyists for the carbon capture industry. In an email to CleanTechnica, she said,

“There is no public momentum from parties in the COP31 process asking for carbon capture, utilization and storage, yet it is in the final draft text. As a result, in the coming hours, parties at COP31 will decide whether to lock in a [methane] fired future with a rapid scale-up of carbon dumping. The potential inclusion of a huge increase in global carbon capture, utilization and storage (CCUS) would be one of the biggest loopholes for the fossil fuel industry ever to be included in a COP text. Climate advocates are highly alarmed and are urging parties to come to their senses and make decisions that advance global climate action rather than the interests of the gas industry.”

As it turns out, the knife in the back delivered by fossil fuel interests at COP31 was too much for negotiators from the EU and the UK to swallow. According to reNEWS, the European Union expressed its “profound disappointment” with the draft cover decision following the close of the COP31 conference.

“At a point where the world urgently needs action to avert disastrous level of global warming, the draft cover decision falls short,” a joint EU statement read. “We will not endorse a cover decision that does not respond to the science, that disregards the implementation gap, and risks deepening the chasm between global commitments and the action required to correct the overshoot and bring 1.5°C within reach. We will not endorse a cover decision that does not even mention fossil fuels, the driver of climate change.”

The statement listed a number on other concerns with the draft cover decision, including that the draft extols carbon capture, usage, and storage technologies “disproportionally” as a method to deal with the climate crisis. This is magical thinking, the statement by the EU said.

The UK also refused to be sucked in by the absurd claims that carbon capture was a plausible alternative to reducing the world’s reliance on fossil fuels. The Independent reports that UK energy secretary Miatta Fahnbulleh expressed regret that the talks had failed to produce the “bold ambition” needed, as the negotiations became mired in disagreement over a draft agreement that contained no mention of fossil fuels.

The UK formally objected to a proposed deal that “moves us backwards on the science” of tackling climate change. In a statement, Fahnbulleh said, “This COP sought to confront hard truths — that global warming is set to overshoot 1.5C and that we must act with ever more urgency to address it. The moment required bold ambition and we have been working tirelessly to try to build consensus. It is immensely disappointing we have been unable to do so.”

There are a number of ways to remediate the emissions from burning fossil fuels, which are nothing more than stored sunshine from millions of years ago. We could irradiate them with unicorn farts, incinerate them in nuclear fusion facilities, are store them in the basement at Mar-A-Loco, where large quantities of toxic fumes are already present. There is only one problem — none of those solutions work. In fact, the only thing that does work is burning less fossil fuels, until we are able to phase them out entirely.

In  2025, Mark Jacobson, a professor of civil and environmental engineering at Stanford University, and his colleagues, published a research paper in the journal Environmental Science and Technology.  In it he wrote that if the nations of the world transitioned to renewable energy — solar, wind, and hydroelectric power — they would eliminate energy-related air pollution deaths and CO2-equivalent emissions while reducing their energy needs by 54.4 percent, annual energy costs by 59.6 percent, and annual social costs — energy, plus health, plus climate — by 91.8 percent. Who wouldn’t want that?

On the other hand, Jacobson and his team wrote that every dollar spent on carbon capture instead of wind, water, and solar, “increases carbon dioxide pollution, energy requirements, energy costs, pipelines, and total social costs.” This holds true even if zero-emission energy systems power the technology deployed to extract carbon dioxide. “It’s always an opportunity cost to use clean, renewable energy for direct air capture instead of replacing a fossil fuel CO2 source, just like it’s an opportunity cost to use it for AI or bitcoin mining. You’re preventing renewables from replacing fossil fuel sources because you’re creating more demand for those renewables,” he said.

“Opportunity costs” is the operative concept here. If you are in Peoria, you cannot be in Philadelphia. If you spend $10.00 on pretzels, you cannot spend it on vegetables. If you buy a new car, you cannot use that money to go to Cabo San Lucas. The money the world pisses away on carbon capture cannot be used to build solar, wind, and hydro resources. It’s as simple as that.

The take away here is that fossil fuels will always win the policy debate. So what will drive the transition to renewable energy? Lower prices for renewables and eliminating concerns about energy insecurity from geopolitical disruptions like the closure of the Strait of Hormuz. Low prices always win and people are pretty sick and tired of paying six bucks for diesel while watching the cost of electricity soar higher and higher.

“The first one now shall later be last,” Bob Dylan taught us. That’s a message of hope inside the carbon dioxide cloud the fossil fuel industry has gifted us. The only question is whether the transition will happen soon enough to avoid a climate catastrophe. It’s hard to imagine destroying a planet and decimating your customer base is a good long term business strategy, but that’s what the executives in charge of the world’s oil, methane, and coal industries seem to think makes for good business. There are none so blind as those who will not see.

#5615. Sharper at Age 60: Later-Born Generations Have Slower Memory Decline

— Newer generations also entered old age with better memory scores

Recent generations entered old age with better memory, and their memory declined more slowly than that of earlier generations, longitudinal data from over 219,000 people showed.

Being born 10 years later was associated with a 10% higher memory score at age 60 (d=0.32 per decade), reported Anders Fjell, PhD, of the University of Oslo in Norway, and colleagues.

At every age, later-born cohorts showed a higher probability of having stable memory. Each decade of later birth was associated with 64% higher odds of being in a stable memory period (OR 1.64, 95% CI 1.59-1.68, P<0.001), and 38% lower odds of meeting steep-decline criteria at the same age (OR 0.62, 95% CI 0.55-0.70, P<0.001).

"The combined advantage is large enough to plausibly account for the reported 13% per decade decline in dementia incidence," Fjell and colleagues wrote in Nature Medicine.

"It's known that later-born generations score better on cognitive tests. What this study shows is that the memory changes we observe during aging have also slowed," Fjell told MedPage Today.

"By age 80, about half of the generational advantage comes from each of these sources," he added. "The second half is new, and the total advantage is large enough to account for the reported fall in dementia incidence at a given age in Western countries."

In 2020, an analysis of seven population-based cohort studies in the U.S. and Europe from the Alzheimer Cohorts Consortium showed that the incidence rate of dementia declined by 13% per decade consistently in studies from 1998 to 2015.

In the U.S., the number of people living with dementia is projected to rise sharply as populations age, but dementia incidence among Medicare beneficiaries is falling.

To better understand recent trends, Fjell and colleagues evaluated over 783,000 memory assessments from more than 219,000 individuals in five longitudinal studies in the U.S. and Europe who were followed for up to 30 years. Cohorts came from the U.S. Health and Retirement Study; the English Longitudinal Study of Ageing; the Survey of Health, Ageing and Retirement in Europe; the Irish Longitudinal Study on Ageing; and the Longitudinal Aging Study Amsterdam. Participants were ages 50 and older at baseline.

The researchers focused on verbal episodic memory as assessed by word-list recall, an indicator of Alzheimer's-related cognitive decline. Substudies assessed hippocampal volume change.

The slower decline of later-born generations was mirrored by slower hippocampal atrophy, the researchers observed. The fastest decliners had the most improvements over time.

The Lancet Commission estimated that nearly half of global dementia cases may be prevented or delayed through lifestyle and environmental changes, Fjell and co-authors pointed out. Because cognitive performance in adulthood correlates with childhood function, generational differences in memory decline may reflect early-life conditions that build cognitive capacity, they said.

Midlife factors like hypertension treatment and physical activity also may play a role, Fjell noted. "We cannot pinpoint the causes with certainty, since these are observational data," he said. "Likely drivers are the past century's improvements in education, childhood conditions, and cardiovascular health, which also means the findings support prevention as a life-course project rather than something that begins at 65."

There's no guarantee this positive development will continue, he pointed out. "The gains we see have been created by improvements in living conditions, education, and health throughout the 20th century, and they did not happen by themselves."

The study had several limitations, the researchers acknowledged. Faster decliners were less likely to complete all assessments over time, they said. Findings were from studies in high-income countries in Europe and North America, and may not apply to other parts of the world.

#5614. The European Competitiveness Fund, the Backbone of a Modern European Investment Policy

European businesses call for a robust, well-funded European Competitiveness Fund

Dear Chancellors, Prime Ministers, Presidents,

Europe is losing ground in technological leadership and struggles to scale strategic technologies, while volatile energy costs and external dependencies continue to dampen business confidence.

Europe’s path to enduring technological leadership relies on our ability to scale strategic technologies. By stabilising energy costs, providing a path to clean industrial renewal and cultivating European resilience, we are laying the groundwork for robust business and investors’ confidence. Public investment has a crucial role to play to address these vital challenges. We require a smarter, more strategic investment policy for Europe.

We, more than 75 undersigned businesses, call on European leaders to agree to a Multiannual Financial Framework (MFF) commensurate with Europe’s challenges, to protect its investment capacity and place long-term productivity growth, resilience and decarbonisation at its core. We believe the European Competitiveness Fund (ECF) represents a step-change in the EU’s investment policy and must be strengthened in the remaining negotiations.

The ECF with its toolbox projects the ambition to provide tailored support to the investment and scale-up challenge Europe faces. This is a positive evolution of the EU funding landscape. With a bigger role for the EU’s derisking instrument InvestEU, the ECF can become Europe’s modern investment vehicle with an increased use of guarantees, loans and equity to unlock strategic investments.

At the same time, non-repayable support needs to be dedicated to scaling up sectors that deliver high strategic value but cannot yet secure commercial capital in Europe. Ramp-up support for Europe’s cleantech manufacturing, including support for OpEx, is needed to make Europe’s bid to regain technological leadership credible.

The advisory services for SMEs and the single access portal promises a simpler access to funding for firms across Europe. If successful, the ECF can pool additional national funds through merit-based procedures open to eligible projects across the single market to bring further alignment and synergies into European industrial development.

Europe’s energy import dependency has become a strategic vulnerability for European businesses. To increase our economic resilience, the EU must prioritise investments into energy sovereignty. Incentives need to pivot capital allocation away from fossil fuel infrastructure, and towards clean energy and electrification. From 2028, the next MFF should not support new fossil-fuel infrastructure that prolongs dependency on fossil fuels, while providing narrowly defined safeguards for security of supply. We need steering mechanisms to reduce fossil fuel dependencies. EU funding can help to tackle high upfront costs and derisk the switch to cleaner and more reliable energy. Otherwise, long investment cycles create severe fossil fuel lock-in effects that threaten our operational autonomy with increasingly volatile fossil energy prices.

Europe’s investment gap cannot be solved by a smarter, more strategic funding approach alone. The confidence into European competitiveness needs to translate into a sizable budget for the clean transition to have lasting impact. The Commission proposes an average annual budget of €3.3 billion for a clean transition and industrial decarbonisation window of the ECF. This number is dwarfed by recurring estimates of the annual climate investment deficit of €344 billion. At the same time, according to the OECD, industrial subsidies have reached the highest levels since the financial crisis 2008.

We urge European leaders to respond to the evolving investment environment and adopt the European Parliament’s proposal to increase the size of the ECF by €26.6 billion and to allocate a substantial share of the additional funding for industrial decarbonisation and clean transition. The more efficient use of financial instruments and a strategic prioritisation can improve the impact of EU funding but cannot substitute the investment needs Europe is facing to boost competitiveness and security.

The next long-term budget and the European Competitiveness Fund must become the investment trigger for European businesses to regain competitiveness at the global scale, based on clear incentives and regulatory certainty. We businesses urge leaders to demonstrate a long-term vision and make the ECF the cornerstone of a new EU green industrial strategy.

Download the letter to see the full list of signatories.

#5613. 13 Drugs Get Double-Digit Price Cuts in Medicare

— Savings of up to 84% will take effect in 2028

Medicare beneficiaries can look forward to saving up to 84% on drugs used to treat cancer, arthritis, and diabetes, among other conditions, thanks to the latest round of Medicare drug price negotiations.

"Every year, millions of seniors leave the pharmacy empty-handed because they can't afford their medication," CMS administrator Mehmet Oz, MD, MBA, said Monday in a press release. "The Trump administration is committed to use every tool at our disposal to negotiate fairer, more affordable prices to ease the burden on Medicare beneficiaries and help them get the care they need."

The negotiated prices will result in an estimated net savings for the Medicare program of 45%, or $9.2 billion, on 13 Medicare Part B and D drugs used to treat serious chronic conditions. CMS noted that when the negotiated prices go into effect in 2028, people enrolled in Medicare prescription drug coverage (i.e., Medicare Part D) would save under the defined standard benefit design an estimated $69 million in out-of-pocket costs. People with Medicare Part B coverage would save an estimated $395 million in out-of-pocket costs.

This is the third round of the Medicare Drug Price Negotiation Program, which was established in 2022 under the Biden administration and allows the federal government to negotiate directly with manufacturers to lower prices for some of Medicare's most popular and expensive drugs.

The first round of negotiated prices went into effect in January 2026, covering drugs used by 8.8 million people on Medicare, with list price reductions ranging from 38% to 79%. The second round of negotiated prices will begin in January 2027, covering drugs used by 5.3 million people on Medicare, with list price reductions ranging from 38% to 85%.

This third round covers drugs used by 1.5 million people on Medicare, with list price reductions ranging from 34% to 84%, according to a CMS fact sheet. The listed drugs accounted for $25.6 billion in Medicare drug costs in 2025.

The drugs included in this round of price negotiations were:

Although tofacitinib (Xeljanz, Xeljanz XR) and omalizumab (Xolair) were originally selected for negotiation this cycle, they were not included in final negotiations because "before the end of or during the negotiation period" those drugs had generics or biosimilars approved for them, the agency said. Such drugs are not eligible for negotiation under the Medicare price negotiation program.

Healthcare groups were generally happy with the negotiation outcome. "Voters made clear in this month's midterm elections that families are fed up with the high cost of healthcare, and prescription drug prices are a big part of the problem," Anthony Wright, CEO of Families USA, said in a statement. "Medicare negotiation is showing itself to be a successful solution on prescription drug prices that should be expanded to more discounts for more drugs for more people."

In another statement, Merith Basey, CEO of Patients for Affordable Drugs, noted that "Medicare's third round of negotiated prices demonstrates what patients already know: negotiation works. Across two administrations, Medicare has secured lower prices and billions in savings for patients and taxpayers."

Finally, Nancy LeaMond, AARP executive vice president, said, "For decades, AARP has fought to lower prescription drug prices, helping secure the historic 2022 law that gave Medicare the power to negotiate lower prices. Today's announcement is another victory in that fight."

#5612. Solar Industry Supports BAAJA While Environmental Orgs Oppose It

The Bipartisan American Affordability and Jobs Act (BAAJA) is an interesting piece of legislation. It is getting a ton of support from the solar sector, but it is heavily opposed by environmental organizations.

Following the passage of BAAJA in the US Senate the other day, Tim Pawlenty, CEO of the Solar Energy Industries Association (SEIA), stated the following: “America is now one step closer to lower energy costs, more clean energy and a more reliable electric grid. Senators Lee, Capito, Heinrich, and Whitehouse deserve tremendous credit for advancing this bipartisan bill.”

The organization adds some more detail and why it thinks this is such a good piece of legislation: “SEIA analysis shows that BAAJA could unlock 167 gigawatts of additional solar capacity between 2028 and 2035, bringing solar to over 18% of U.S. electricity generation by 2035. The analysis finds that the bill’s reforms could reduce cumulative power sector CO2 emissions by approximately 20% and avoid $54 billion in public health damages.”

That sounds very appealing, doesn’t it? Why wouldn’t we want that?

Well, the Sierra Club argues the bill goes in quite the opposite direction. The storied organization states the following: “While a rogue president is waging a war on clean energy, the Senate rushed a deeply flawed bill that furthers the buildout of fossil fuels, strips away power from communities and Tribes, threatens our clean water and public lands, and endangers wildlife. The House of Representatives has an equal voice in the legislative process. They must reject the Senate’s fast-tracked bill and demand debate and consideration to deliver for the people, not corporate polluters and Donald Trump. The American people made a clear call merely weeks ago: our elected leaders must address soaring costs and a corrupt system that prioritizes corporate polluter profits over our health, our wallets, and our future.”

It seems the issue is that BAAJA would make it much easier to put up power plants, skipping all kinds of environmental protections. That is why Republicans support it in the first place — they are always keen to plow through environmental protections. The reason SEIA supports it is clear enough — the organization is just thinking about solar power plants and looking at the potential for more growth in the industry, especially considering the fact that solar is now typically the cheapest option for new power capacity and has been dominating new installations.

So, the question is: would BAAJA really disproportionately support solar power, or would it lead to a lot more fossil fuel power plants and an edge to the old, dirty power technologies? Can the legislation be both good and bad, or is someone getting this wrong?

#5610. Smarter Cooling, Stronger Reliability: Advancing Wind Turbine Gearbox Performance

As the wind energy industry matures, operators and service providers are increasingly focused on maximizing turbine availability while controlling long-term maintenance costs. Although major drivetrain components often receive the spotlight, one overlooked system can have an outsized impact on turbine reliability and performance: gearbox oil cooling.

Heat exchangers play a critical role in maintaining proper oil temperature and viscosity within wind turbine gearboxes. When cooling systems become clogged with airborne debris, dust, or organic material, oil temperatures rise, lubrication performance declines, and component stress increases. Over time, these conditions can contribute to reduced gearbox efficiency, elevated maintenance demands, and avoidable downtime.

“Wind operators today are balancing increasing performance expectations with the realities of aging fleets and rising maintenance costs. Technologies that improve cooling efficiency and reduce service exposure can have a meaningful impact on long-term turbine reliability and operational profitability,” according to Mike Erickson, HYDAC’s Market Manager for the Wind Industry.

Traditional “panel cut” heat exchanger designs have long been susceptible to clogging due to their tightly packed fin structures. In many wind environments — particularly agricultural, dusty, or high-pollen regions — debris accumulation can severely restrict airflow. Oil leaks can further compound the issue by trapping contaminants within the exchanger core, making cleaning more difficult and labor intensive.

This operational challenge has driven the need for more resilient cooling technologies designed specifically for real-world wind farm conditions.

HYDAC’s new “Square Wave” heat exchanger design represents a significant advancement in addressing these reliability concerns. Engineered to allow debris to pass more freely through the exchanger fins, the design minimizes clogging while improving cooling efficiency under demanding operating conditions.

The importance of effective cooling cannot be overstated. Wind turbine gearbox oil typically operates within an optimal temperature range of approximately 45°C to 65°C. Even modest temperature increases can significantly impact oil viscosity. According to field data, a 12°C rise in oil temperature can reduce viscosity by nearly 28 percent — affecting lubrication quality and accelerating wear on critical drivetrain components.

Field trials have demonstrated measurable operational benefits from the Square Wave design. During a one-year trial on GE 1.x turbines, the upgraded exchanger consistently reduced clogging and lowered maintenance requirements compared to traditional panel cut cores. Operators reported reduced up-tower cleaning time and fewer temperature-related cooling faults.

Field validation across multiple turbine platforms has further reinforced the operational advantages of the Square Wave design. In one case study involving legacy 1 MW-class turbines operating in the Pacific Northwest, a major renewable energy operator installed pilot sets of Square Wave heat exchangers as part of a cooling performance evaluation. Installation teams reported the upgraded exchangers integrated seamlessly into the existing platform with no retrofit complications.

After initial operating data demonstrated improved cooling consistency, the operator expanded the evaluation program with additional units. Long-term SCADA analysis later showed turbines equipped with Square Wave technology operate approximately 20 to 25 percent cooler than comparable turbines using conventional heat exchanger designs under similar operating conditions. The data also highlighted another important advantage: the Square Wave cores continued to maintain airflow and cooling efficiency in debris-heavy environments where traditional exchanger designs became increasingly restricted over time.

The successful deployment also generated interest from additional wind service organizations seeking retrofit-ready cooling upgrades that could improve turbine reliability while reducing maintenance burdens across aging fleets.

“What operators are increasingly recognizing is that small improvements in thermal management can create meaningful gains in turbine reliability, maintenance efficiency, and long-term asset performance,” said Erickson. “The field data we’re seeing continues to validate the value of designing cooling systems specifically for real-world wind operating environments.”

More recently, long-term SCADA data from Mitsubishi platforms further validated the technology’s performance. After two years of operation, turbines equipped with Square Wave heat exchangers operated approximately 19 to 25 percent cooler than reference turbines using standard exchanger designs under comparable operating conditions.

For wind asset owners and service providers, the implications are substantial: Improved thermal management supports healthier oil viscosity, more stable gearbox operation, and potentially longer component life. At the same time, reduced cleaning frequency and fewer cooling-related service events translate directly into lower operational expenditures and increased turbine availability.

As the industry continues to prioritize lifecycle optimization and energy production efficiency, innovative subsystem upgrades such as advanced heat exchanger technology are becoming increasingly valuable. Rather than waiting for costly failures or performance degradation, operators are seeking practical retrofit solutions that improve reliability without requiring major system redesigns.

“Effective thermal management is critical to protecting gearbox health and maintaining oil performance over time. By addressing one of the most common causes of cooling degradation — debris accumulation — operators can improve uptime while reducing unnecessary maintenance interventions,” said HYDAC’s Erickson.

For wind stakeholders evaluating ways to improve gearbox reliability, reduce maintenance exposure, and enhance turbine performance, cooling system optimization deserves greater attention. Proven retrofit technologies like advanced heat exchanger designs can offer a practical path toward improved uptime and operational efficiency without requiring major system overhauls.

As fleets age and performance expectations continue to rise, collaboration between operators, service providers, and technology partners will be essential to building more resilient wind assets for the future. Now is the time to evaluate whether existing cooling systems are supporting — or limiting — long-term turbine reliability goals.

“HYDAC’s goal is to help wind owners and service providers solve practical operational challenges in ways that create long-term value across the entire asset lifecycle. Innovations like the Square Wave heat exchanger are designed to improve reliability, simplify maintenance, and support greater energy production over time.”

Don’t let cooling system limitations impact turbine performance.

Proactive cooling optimization can help operators reduce maintenance exposure, protect critical drivetrain components, and maximize energy production across the asset lifecycle.
