#5609. The LiTime Lares K1 Expandable Plug & Play Kitchen Backup — CleanTechnica Tested

I spent 35 years in the Tampa suburbs, so I pay attention to backup power when a storm  is coming. Last year I tested the BLUETTI Elite 100 V2 portable power station for CleanTechnica. That unit is meant to be carried around. The LiTime Lares K1 is different: a plug-in kitchen backup battery you leave in place and hook up to a refrigerator and also use to power your small kitchen appliances. First I’ll go through the specs and how it compares with a portable station like the Elite 100 V2, then I’ll give my test results.

LiTime launched the Lares K1 in the US on October 20, 2026. It is a split system: a slim inverter charger paired with a separate 2,304 watt-hour LiFePO4 battery (51.2 volts, 45 amp-hours). You can add up to four batteries, which brings the total to 9,216 watt-hours. The battery is rated for 4,000 cycles, and LiTime backs it with a 5-year warranty.

The inverter puts out 2,000 watts continuously and 4,000 watts of surge power for one second, as a pure sine wave at 120 volts. Adding batteries does not raise that output. You just get more runtime. There are two AC outlets, one on each side of the inverter. I did not see any USB ports listed.

You can charge it at up to 1,500 watts from a wall outlet and up to 800 watts from solar (12 to 60 volts). Combined input is capped at 1,500 watts. LiTime does not put a charge time on the main product page. If you divide 2,304 watt-hours by the 1,500-watt wall input, you get about 90 minutes from empty to full. That is a rough estimate. Charging usually slows down as you get close to 100%, the same way an EV does, and their FAQ later puts the theoretical time at about 1.7 hours if you figure 90% charging efficiency.

The inverter charger weighs about 10.1 pounds. LiTime does not list the battery weight, but I weighted it after I took it out of the packaging and it was about 52 pounds. With the floor stand, the two modules stood up together are 20.47 inches wide, 30.71 inches tall, and 3.94 inches deep. It runs at about 40 decibels (which is very quiet) and switches over to battery in 10 milliseconds or less. It has a touchscreen, Wi-Fi, Bluetooth 5.0, and the LiTime app, plus a time-of-use mode so you can charge off-peak.

The price on LiTime’s site is $1,099.99, shown as $100 off. US preorders opened October 20, 2026, and when I checked, estimated delivery was November 24 to 27.

LiTime calls this “space-based” home backup. Instead of trying to power the whole house, you protect one room, in this case the kitchen. You plug the system into a wall outlet and plug the fridge into the K1. No electrician and no panel work. LiTime estimates about 24 hours of backup on a large side-by-side refrigerator (while I got a bit more than that in my testing) that uses about 2 kilowatt-hours a day, or about 99 hours with four batteries if the fridge is the only load. Depending on the load, it can also run a microwave, an air fryer, a toaster, or a coffee maker for a basic meal. Just don’t try to run them all at once. You can run two smaller loads at once, but a large load like a microwave or air fryer needs to be run by itself (you can keep your refrigerator plugged in too). LiTime says to allow for compressor startup if you also want to run one of those at the same time.

You can wall-mount it, stand it on the floor, or lean it against a wall, with the two modules together or apart. It comes with mounting hardware and two connection cables, 0.25 meters and 1.5 meters (about 10 inches and 5 feet). The battery housing is steel, and LiTime says the metal finish is meant to suit a kitchen. The inverter is rated IP20, so it stays indoors. LiTime says separating the inverter from the battery cuts down on heat transfer, which they claim helps the battery last longer. I have not measured that.

For a plug & play system, this is a new product category. There have been portable generators available for years that could be used for camping or appliance backup, but having a product tailored to a specific room is a new concept. The comparison they offer is this one-room approach versus a whole-house battery system, the kind that usually needs panel work and that will cost you about 10 times as much.

You can use this for camping or travel, but it isn’t built for it. With split modules, wall-mount hardware, only two AC outlets, and no USB ports, this is built for a permanent kitchen install, not something you toss in the car for a weekend trip.

I used my old Kill-A-Watt meter to have an independent measure of the draw on the battery from the refrigerator. During the test, I opened the refrigerator less than I normally would, but still opened it about 10 times.

Next I wanted to test it with my small kitchen appliances. First, one at a time; then, several at once. It is rated at 2000 watts continuous, but it should be able to surge up to 4000 watts for 1 second.

Notice that it pulls 2510 watts from the battery, but the load is only 2290 watts. Where did the other 220 watts go? That must be used by the inverter to convert DC to AC.

After 35 years of Tampa storm seasons, I’ve learned that the fridge is usually the first thing you worry about when the power goes out. The LiTime Lares K1 is built for exactly that. In my testing it kept our large side-by-side GE refrigerator running for almost 26 hours on one battery, a bit longer than LiTime’s own estimate of 24 hours. It then shut off cleanly to protect the appliance. Recharging from the wall took about 3 hours, with 80% back in about 2 hours.

It also handled my small kitchen appliances well. The air fryer, coffee maker, and refrigerator ran together at about 2,200 watts, a little over the rated 2,000. When I added the toaster to push it past its limits, it briefly put out about 3,140 watts and then dropped back to about 2,130 watts without shutting down. The app also showed about 220 watts lost in converting battery power to AC, which is worth keeping in mind when you estimate runtime.

It does have limits. There are only two outlets, no USB ports, and the battery weighs about 52 pounds, so this is a permanent kitchen install, not a camping generator. Adding batteries gives you more runtime, but not more output.

At $1,099.99, with no electrician or panel work needed and a 5-year battery warranty, the Lares K1 is an affordable way to protect your food and still cook a basic meal during an outage. It costs about a tenth of what a whole-house battery would. If kitchen backup is what you care about most, it’s well worth a look.

I have been a software engineer for over 30 years, first developing EDI software, then developing data warehouse systems. Along the way, I've also had the chance to help start a software consulting firm and do portfolio management. In 2010, I took an interest in electric cars because gas was getting expensive. In 2015, I started reading CleanTechnica and took an interest in solar, mainly because it was a threat to my oil and gas investments. Follow me on Twitter @atj721 Tesla investor. Tesla referral code: https://ts.la/paul92237

#5608. Can New Gout Drugs Improve on Existing Treatments?

— Expert weighs promise of two investigational treatments against current options

New data presented at the American College of Rheumatology annual meeting highlighted investigational treatments for gout, including the URAT1 inhibitor pozdeutinurad and the interleukin (IL)-1 beta inhibitor SSGJ-613.

In this exclusive MedPage Today video, Austin Wheeler, MD, of the University of Nebraska Medical Center in Omaha, discusses the potential of these emerging therapies and the unanswered questions that could determine their place in clinical practice.

The following is a transcript of his remarks:

REDUCE-2 is with pozdeutinurad, which is a URAT1 inhibitor, which is kind of a novel mechanism for serum urate reduction. And so there's a lot of interest in this as a potential target. It looked like it did pretty well in the trial that they presented. They showed some nice data of serum urate reduction. They got about 79% of their patients to a goal serum urate, which this was against placebo. So it looks like it works in terms of lowering serum urate levels in gout.

I think it will need to be seen what clinical implication that really has. We know that if we use treat-to-target approaches with allopurinol and febuxostat [Uloric], the STOP Gout study, for example, showed that we can get about 80% of patients to a goal serum urate with those medications. And so I think time will tell, we'll need to see how this stacks up against a treat-to-target approach with our xanthine oxidase inhibitors to really know how this is going to change the landscape of gout treatment.

And then the other study was an IL-1 inhibitor, and so they were looking at how does this perform against placebo, and it looks like it performs as you would expect an IL-1 inhibitor to perform in gout. They did make the statement that it potentially has less of an infection risk than other IL-1 inhibitors. I haven't seen any actual data that shows the frequency of adverse events from an infection standpoint stacked up against our other IL-1 inhibitors.

And so I think that's one where potentially that could be something that makes its way into our treatment options at some point. But we do already have canakinumab [Ilaris] as a long-acting IL-1, we have anakinra [Kineret] as an IL-1. I think until we see more data on is there truly less of an infection risk or not, it's going to be hard to know exactly how that one plays in as well.

I would imagine that at some point they're planning on taking this outside of China. I haven't heard anything personally about plans to do that, but definitely that's something that I think we would need to see. The big things that I'd be looking for would be how does it perform outside of that population and then how does it perform when we have more direct comparisons against the IL-1 inhibitors that we already have available? Is there truly something novel about it that would cause us to reach for that as opposed to canakinumab as a long-acting IL-1?

#5606. Steel solar panel frames hit the mainstream

Steel is considered the backbone of the American economy, critical for both national and economic security. It’s also the backbone of solar power construction, found commonly in the racking and mounting structures tilting solar panels toward the sun. And now, after years of tests and trials, an Ohio factory is roll-forming steel for a solar product that could use a stronger backbone — the solar panel.

A factory worker places a section of steel solar panel frames on a pallet in Welser Profile’s plant in Valley City, Ohio. Credit: Nextpower

Roll-form steel company Welser Profile began manufacturing Nextpower’s NX CoreFrame steel panel frames earlier this year at its facility in Valley City, Ohio, just 30 miles outside Cleveland, and Solar Power World was invited to view production of its first major orders before adding another line fully dedicated to the solar component before the end of the year. Module frame company Origami Solar originally had the idea that swapping aluminum frames with steel would not only save the solar industry money and cut back on carbon emissions, it would also provide more physical support to bigger and heavier solar panels.

Welser, an Austrian company with origins dating back to the 1660s, is a leader in roll-form steel profiles typically only seen in aluminum extrusions and helped produce a steel frame that’s easy for panel manufacturers to assemble and contractors to handle.

“What’s special about Welser Profile is we pre-notch features in the material before it gets formed,” said Bill Johnson, North America CEO of Welser Profile. “Everything about the solar industry is about how do we make it more efficient, cheaper, faster, better? You do that by all these little details to add value farther up the stream that minimizes labor, install cost. Our hole patterns and other features allow operators to snap things together, not bolt or weld things. Everyone is looking at how to make this work in a Lego setup, not a high-intense labor workforce installing all these panels.”

Welser is helping bring the Origami Solar idea to life, but the new owner of the tech, Nextpower, is delivering steel frames to the masses. Nextpower, a steel expert through its experience in producing tracking and mounting systems, acquired Origami in 2025 and will use its existing network of steel partners to convince more PV project developers to consider an alternative to aluminum.

“Nextpower has launched over 30 manufacturing facilities in the U.S. since 2020. Nextpower buys over 700,000 short tons of primary U.S. steel annually, which is more than a small car company,” said Jenya Meydbray, VP and general manager of Nextpower’s steel frames business. “Nextpower has had [the steel frame] for one year, building on the years of Origami’s engineering with Welser’s support. What we’re doing today is launching into high-scale, high-volume production here in Ohio.”

The solar industry defaulted to aluminum panel frames because the material is lightweight, easy to handle and less susceptible to corrosion. But as the solar industry matured, more environmental and practical issues became associated with the metal. Aluminum production on average has a five-times larger carbon footprint than steel production, a difficult point to ignore in the inherently green solar industry. Solar panels have also gotten larger in surface area and use less glass to cut weight. Aluminum might no longer be the ideal material to physically support these “big, floppy modules.”

A steel coil feeds into a roll-forming machine producing solar panel frames. Credit: Nextpower

“Steel provides a far better structural support for the [panel] than aluminum does. That was the initial observation that the Origami founders made,” Meydbray said. “They landed on this specific [roll-form] profile. Roll-forming is a precision process. We’re making millions of sticks of solar panel frames a month, and all of them have to be within sub-millimeter of precision across the full length. Welser is 100 years older than America, so Welser is definitely the most experienced in this art form of steel roll-forming.”

Welser set up North American operations in 2018 after acquiring an automotive roll-form operation in Valley City. The company established a second location in the same industrial park to accommodate its growing client base in the solar, storage and energy industries.

Welser was one of the first roll-form partners to validate Magnelis — a zinc magnesium coating that has “self-healing” properties is capable of lasting 25 years in the field — and found it a great steel coating for solar energy applications. It’s one of the features of NX CoreFrame, Nextpower’s steel solar module frame.

“Coming up with a new coating on steel, you don’t just throw it on the steel and sell it to the market. You have to trial it, validate it, make sure it works,” Johnson said. “Raw zinc — the coating has to be too thick for the longevity that the solar industry needs. Magnelis can go much lighter coating and still produce the same salt spray, same longevity, and it’s cheaper, faster, smarter.”

Wesler is the latest high-volume production facility for NX CoreFrame, and Nextpower plans to reach 6.5 GW of annual production through additional manufacturing partners across the country.

With an established steel frame design, the product just needs to find a place on panel manufacturer’s production lines. Origami Solar had been signing supply agreements with U.S. panel assemblers, an activity that has only increased with the Nextpower transition. Meydbray said the pitch for a domestic supply chain has become more attractive, especially with Nextpower’s established regional steel manufacturing network.

A machine treats steel coil as it travels along a factory line. Credit: Nextpower

“Steel strengthens the supply chain of domestic manufacturing facilities. When frames are bought overseas, they spend five, six weeks on the water before they actually arrive to the factory. [Panel companies] have about 2,000 pallets of frames in inventory when they source from overseas,” he said. “We’re able to provide actual just-in-time delivery to our factory clients. We can make deliveries every 48 hours. [They can save room] where they used to have pallets stacked up. There’s also the working capital of having stuff sit on the water for six weeks. Solar panel manufacturing is not a high-margin business, and working capital management is critical.”

Nextpower has inked supply agreements with many multi-gigawatt panel manufacturers in the United States, including Jinko, T1 and Thornova.

“This integration of Nextpower steel frames allows us to deliver high-performance modules tailored to the evolving U.S. market,” said Andrea Bodenhagen, global marketing director at Thornova Solar, which is establishing domestic solar cell manufacturing in Oklahoma. “By incorporating U.S.-made steel frames, we strengthen supply chain resilience while helping our customers maximize domestic content and improve project economics.”

With bonus incentives from the investment tax credit still in play when solar projects use domestic products, an American-made steel frame can make all the difference.

“When we see projects that have 100% domestic content on the tracker, we know that’s a perfect candidate for steel frames,” Meydbray said. “Steel frames get 6% domestic content credit. It’s the second cheapest percent after the tracker. A domestically made solar cell is a lot more expensive than an imported solar cell. The frame is the second-most cost-effective way to buy percentage points.”

The NX CoreFrame is universal and can be used with any brand’s mounting or tracking system, but there are advantages when the project can be designed more cohesively through one supplier. Nextpower has spent the last year expanding beyond mounting systems, now offering inverter, electrical balance of system and panel products.

“[Nextpower] is ambitious, but it’s not arrogant. It prioritizes the customer relationships it’s built over the years. We spent time with our customers asking where their pain points are and expanded the business accordingly …  the company that makes the thing the solar panel is mounted to is the same company that makes the frame that’s being mounted,” Meydbray said.

Kelly Pickerel was a long-time reporter with Solar Power World before leaving her post as editor in chief in 2026. You may find her on LinkedIn.

#5605. FDA Moves to Boost Drug Quality Testing

— It's important to assess product quality, not just focus on regulatory compliance, expert says

The FDA announced that it's expanding its approach to drug quality testing, combining traditional laboratory methods with newer, faster screening technologies to identify potential problems with medications on the U.S. market. Its Enhanced Approach for Sampling and Testing (EAST) initiative builds on existing surveillance efforts and aims to use testing data to better inform regulatory decisions and inspections, the agency said.

In this exclusive MedPage Today video, David Light, co-founder and CEO of independent drug-testing company Valisure, discusses the potential benefits of expanded testing, emerging technologies being explored through the Advanced Research Projects Agency for Health (ARPA-H), and why evaluating finished drug products should complement traditional manufacturing inspections and regulatory oversight.

The following is a transcript of this video:

We need to trust but verify. We need to test more.

The announcement, I think overall, is exciting. I think it's more underscoring how important it is to not just inspect facilities and review paperwork and review approvals, but also to test, to actually test the physical products themselves, the quality of the pharmaceutical drug products.

Is it new? I mean ... it's a new announcement. This is a program that builds upon the DQST [Drug Quality Sampling and Testing] program within FDA, which has been going on since at least 2013. So it sounds like this is an enhanced version, which also sounds exciting, leveraging some new, more modern techniques.

ARPA-H launched a program called FASTPASS just a few months ago -- that stands for Fast Assessment of Solutions, Therapeutics, Pharmaceuticals, and Supplies -- effectively trying to scan or test the quality, not just compliance, but the quality of about 70 million shipments of pharmaceutical products into the United States in all U.S. ports every year. That certainly, as they say, is an ARPA-Hard task.

The idea of using modern testing techniques is interesting. I mean, their DQST program is having conventional techniques as the foundation. Looking at a product from many different lenses, many different techniques and methods, I think, is actually really important. I think that's part of what independent testing has shown over the years and what perhaps some of the previous testing programs have missed.

One of the things that I saw that was very exciting in the FDA announcement is that they're talking about this kind of data driving regulatory policy and FDA inspection planning. I think a big part of regulatory testing is essentially seeing if something's legal to be on the road, right? Is it legal to be marketed and sold in the U.S.? And that's regulatory compliance. But what we focus on in independent testing is more of a market driver, what procurement actually looks for, which can be not just, is it legal to be on the road or legal to be sold, what are the best quality products or the lowest risk, but both have an influence on the other.

So I think the two can really overlap very well to help compliance and help with driving the market to a race to the top as opposed to the race to the bottom. But driving the market to a race to the top, to be as good as possible, I think is certainly a key driver of independent testing and quality testing. The two can absolutely work hand-in-hand. I think they do and will hopefully work even more in the future.

I don't have much visibility in what the FDA means by modern testing techniques, faster testing techniques, that they're saying is being done with traditional methods. I assume it may leverage some of the technologies that ARPA-H is looking at as well. ARPA-H is looking beyond the conventional traditional destructive testing where you need to essentially dissolve a tablet or a capsule in a solvent, run it on a mass spectrometer or run it on an HPLC [high-performance liquid chromatography].

These are expensive, lengthy types of tests, which are very, very precise. So can you still leverage insights with a core tenet of ARPA-H project's non-destructive testing? So laser-based approaches, other technologies like terahertz or NMR [nuclear magnetic resonance], NQR [nuclear quadrupole resonance], hyperspectral imaging. One of the core areas that actually Valisure has been developing for over a decade is a Raman technology-based approach, which is laser and looking at what reflects back as a chemical fingerprint of the product. You can actually identify some pretty serious quality issues by doing that.

I think there's been a lot of talk in previous years that you can't test your way into compliance. You have to have a regulatory overview, you have to have product inspections, you have to have a GMP [good manufacturing practice] process that's very robust, which is certainly true, but testing at the same time is very complementary to that. And I think we're seeing more and more evidence of it and more growth in that area.

#5604. 90 New Vehicle Models Were Just Introduced in Colombia’s Largest Automotive Exhibition. 55 Were Electric.

The last two weeks, the future was on display in Bogota’s Automobile Hall.

This Hall is Colombia’s largest and most important Automotive Exhibition, and since 2023 (17th edition, held after a hiatus of 5 years), it has heralded the unstoppable tide of EVs progressively taking over the national market. Back then, despite EVs making up less than 3% of the market, EVs were already present and relevant in the Automobile Hall, with the Changan Lumin being a particularly bright star.

EVs have been gaining ground since, with more of them arriving every year, and in the latest iteration of the Automobile Hall, we’ve reached a point of no return: EVs make up a majority of the newly announced models arriving in the country. Or to be specific: out of 90 arrivals, EVs account for 55!

32 brands brought new electric or plug-in hybrid cars to showcase in Bogota’s Automobile Hall. Even though most of them (unsurprisingly) are from China, we still find presences from 7 European and 2 Japanese brands here — all will be listed at the end of this article with their respective models.

Most of these models arrive at very competitive pricing, many times undercutting their direct gasoline and diesel competition, and with ever-improving specs — by reasonable standards, most of these cars should be able to make at least 350 km on Colombian roads, making them quite appealing options even for people who travel regularly.

Colombia’s EV market share has been growing in 2026, reaching 20% — or almost — every month since March. But with this EV avalanche (or EV-lanche), I think it’s inevitable that we will see that number go up in the months to come — though how fast exactly, I leave to you to debate.

And no, you were not the only ones to notice that there are exactly zero (0) arrivals from US brands. Tesla was of course present, but with nothing new (though they did present the Cybercab at a later date than I attended), other than Tesla, US brands are either too small or too focused on gas-guzzlers to bother even trying to compete.

Note: all prices mentioned here include taxes, but registration and insurance may not be included.

I will start with the non-Chinese EVs, because, well, they’re fewer.

The most important model here, by far, is the Renault 4 e-Tech. People outside this country probably don’t know that the Renault 4 was built here for 22 years, becoming the most affordable car for decades and a symbol of the Colombian middle class. Nicknamed the “Colombian Car” and the “Loyal Friend,” the Renault 4 has a place in the hearth of most Colombians, and, as such, the R4 e-Tech was one of the most visited cars in the entire exhibition.

Because of this, it’s certain that the car will have some buyers. But at 100 million COP (€26,000, $28,500), it’s a hard sell, particularly when the hatchback segment has stabilized somewhere around $20,000 for Chinese models. Nostalgia has a price for sure, but not that many people will be willing to pay $8,500 for it.

The BMW iX3 was also present in the hall, and I must admit it’s a very compelling and impressive car, but at 300 million COP ($85,400), there are not a lot of buyers that will be interested.

From the European side, other than Renault and BMW, we have the Cupra Terramar and the Mini Cooper SE Paul Smith Edition, both around 160 million COP (€41,700, $45,500). So, quite expensive for their segments, even though I find the Terramar a very beautiful car. The Porsche Cayenne is of course expensive beyond anything seen prior, and the Volvo EX60 has not announced pricing yet. As for Stellantis, it’s betting on its two vans, somewhat expensive but still viable, and it has the Opel Crossland EV, which has been on the market for a while but is now discounted to 90 million COP (€23,400, $25,600), meaning it’s one of the most competitive non-Chinese EVs available.

Regarding the Japanese, the Toyota BZ4X (already present) and the RAV4 PHEV (just arriving) are of course important players, but at 220 million and 270 million COP respectively ($62,600 and $76,800), they won’t sell more than a trickle of units. The Nissan Frontier Pro has the same price as the BZ4X, and, to be fair, I didn’t even notice it was available until I left the Hall.

Paradoxically, the most competitive non-Chinese EVs all come from a brand with zero arrivals: Kia. With its EV2 starting at 90 million COP and its EV3 at 120 million COP ($23,400 and $34,100), Kia has the closest prices to any Chinese competitor and can at least rely on the brand’s popularity to keep some of the market.

With that out of the way, let’s look at the Chinese arrivals, or at least some of them, as there are too many to mention.

You can see the full list of models at the end of the article. Here I will focus on those I think will make the most impact in the market.

First, one of my favorite EVs: the all-mighty Chery ICaur V23. In a previous article I mentioned how surprising its low range was … but I must admit I judged it rashly. I mean, that thing is huge. I really expected it to be much smaller, and thus I understand why it can barely edge a 360 km range (WLTP) despite a sizeable 81 kWh battery. When you’re built like a small tank, you can expect to get the range of a small tank, no surprises here.

But the great selling point for this car is its price: 85 million COP for the 4×2 version and 105 million COP for the 4×4 version ($24,100 and $29,900). We’re talking a fully off-road capable SUV for the same or less than a Renault 4 or a Corolla. Truly impressive, and an example of EV innovation in segments that many other OEMs have not dared enter, assuming they are lost to the ICEV competition.

BYD also surprised by bringing a new Sealion RWD, armed with a 70 kWh battery and aimed directly against the Model Y. At 115 million COP ($32,700), this car is $1,500 cheaper than the Model Y RWD while having a larger battery and better range. It seems the Chinese giant wants to start getting back some market share a year after the US brand took the market by storm. Better late than never I guess.

BYD is also bringing the EREV Leopard 7 under its own brand (BYD Ti7), but at 225 million COP ($64,000), it will certainly not be a best seller, even though it will probably give the likes of Toyota and Land Rover a run for their money.

Now, my dear readers, I hope you will forgive me here, but even though I actually went to the Hall to take a look to the XPeng Mona L03 specifically, it seems I got distracted by the robot and the drone-licopter, and thus this is the picture you’re getting … because I forgot to take one of the L03. Regardless, the XPeng L03 is also pointing directly at Tesla, arriving at 115 million COP ($32,700), the same price of the entry-level Model 3, except the L03 provides full self-driving capabilities for this price, to the extent that XPeng can provide that of course. This car (available in BEV and PHEV versions) and the BYD Sealion are poised to bring a significant challenge not only to Tesla, but to the entire ICEV ecosystem, which is having evermore competition in its bread-and-butter segments by the month.

There are currently 6 subcompact entry-level SUVs fighting in the 80-to-95 million COP segment: the Chery Omoda E5, the MG S5, the Dongfeng Vigo, the Faw Bestune Joy, the Changan Nevo Q05, and the Ora 5.

I chose the Ora 5 to represent the segment because it offers the largest battery (58kWh, vs 50kWh for all the others) and, as of the Automobile Hall, the lowest price for the entry-level version, at only 73 million COP (USD $20.800). This is a true bargain, making it the cheapest available SUV of any powertrain by quite some distance, and a very compelling option for budget-oriented buyers. It remains to be seen if this price will remain after the Hall.

Going by specs alone, I believe the JMEV GSX to be the best value-for-money vehicle currently available in the Colombian market. This SUV boasts an impressive 70 kWh battery with 500 km WLTP range for a mere 90 million COP ($25,600). The only thing against it is that JMEV (JMC) is quite a small brand and has not gained traction in this market, or in any other that I know of.

In Colombia, this minicar has taken the place that belonged to the Wuling Hongguang in China, and more recently belongs to the Chery QQ Ice Cream — that of a (barely) highway-capable minicar that may seat four, or two plus your groceries, but that will fit in any spot and can be parked wherever you may find a square meter available. Available with a brand new 18.1 kWh battery, the Faw Xiaoma 2.0 remains the most affordable “true” car in Colombia at 40 million COP ($11,400).

It’s worth noting that Faw has failed to gain traction in the Colombian market, and it clearly wanted to change that in this Hall, as it brought an entire armada, with its N09 Sedan available for 100 million COP ($28,300) as its flagship car.

Aside from minicars (like the Xiaoma above), the Dongfeng Box deserves a mention for being the cheapest car in the hatchback segment at 65 million COP ($18,500). With its 42 kWh battery, it can reasonably travel long distance with the current infrastructure. I’ve ridden in both this car and the Geely EX2, and, frankly, the EX2 has a higher quality feel, but this one is slightly cheaper, and no doubt this will bring some buyers.

At a price of only 75 million COP ($21,350), the Foton e-Wonder mini-truck provides full price parity with diesel versions and opens the possibility of electrification for the owners of smaller companies. At 1-ton carrying capacity, it’s a very compelling vehicle, even if its 40 kWh battery remains limited for long-distance operations.

Of course, this list would not be complete without the most affordable “real” car here (so, not minicars included, sorry Xiaoma!). The Jac E10X is a city-car available for 60 million COP ($17,000), and even though it looks good and has decent quality, it has never sold well because JAC is not a very popular brand, and because its 30 kWh battery lacks capacity for making even medium-distance trips without having at least some range anxiety.

Well, this is it. My apologies for the fans of Arcfox and Leapmotor — both are very important brands arriving in the country, but Arcfox lacks recognition and prices are a bit over the competition (they are a luxury brand, but that may not convince people who don’t already know them) and Leapmotor only brought EREVs, and as you may have seen, I focused on BEVs in the very long Chinese list.

My conclusions are probably quite predictable. China is leading and by quite an advantage, the Koreans are not too far behind, the Europeans are trying to make a comeback, the Japanese are making a symbolic (but useless) stand, and the Americans — other than Tesla — didn’t even bother to show up.

It’s hard to believe only three years have passed since Bogota’s Automobile Hall of 2023, when the Changan Lumin was the star and when we were celebrating going over 2% market share. Back then, EVs were still expensive, charging infrastructure was much more limited, and the mainstream was firmly on the pure-combustion side, with hybrids making some inroads.

Now, after the EV-lanche we’re seeing, it seems that EVs are now fully in the mainstream, with lower prices and better specs than ever before. EV sales will keep growing fast, and I expect the market to tilt even more to the side of zero-emissions transportation in the coming months. 30% BEV market share is now well within sight.

For those of you who want to look at the full list of arrivals, here it is below. BEVs are in bold, PHEVs and EREVs are in regular.

Arcfox T5, T1, S5; Avatr 11, 7; BAIC X55; BYD Sealion RWD, Ti7 (Leopard 7), Atto 8; Deepal S09; Denza Z9 GT, B5; Dongfeng 7, 8; Farizon V7E, F3E; Faw Xiaoma 2.0, Joyee N09, Joyee S08; Foton E-Tunland; GAC Aion UT 2027, S7; Geely Monjaro EM-I, Starray EM-R; GWM Tank 700, ICaur V23, V27; IM IM6; Jetour G700, T2; JMEV GSX; Leapmotor B10, C10; Lynk & Co 2, 1, 8, 900; MHero II; Rox Motor 1; Xpeng L03, L03; Zeekr 007GT, 001FR, 9X.

BMW iX3, iX5, i7; Cupra Terramar; Mini Cooper SE Paul Smith; Peugeot E-Partner, Peugeot E-Expert; Porsche Cayenne; Renault R4 E-Tech; Volvo EX60.

Passionate for all things Latin American, I’ve been closely following the energy and mobility transitions since they started to become present south of the equator.

#5602. Toxic Port Pollution Costs Europe €2.5bn A Year In Health & Environmental Damages — Study

New research reveals a single ship emits, on average, as much toxic sulphur at port in a year as 19,300 cars, leaving taxpayers and hospitals to pay the hidden cost of shipping pollution.

Commercial ships docked in European ports inflict €2.5 billion a year in societal and environmental costs, according to new research by T&E. While upcoming EU port rules will cut at-berth damages by 40% by 2030, most vessels remain exempt. Previous research has shown that residents living close to a commercial harbour already face significantly higher risks of cardiovascular and respiratory mortality¹. Tightening port pollution rules would save society billions and protect the health of port communities, says T&E.

Instead of plugging into the electrical grid when they dock, thousands of commercial vessels run diesel generators around the clock to power onboard systems. These idling engines release harmful pollution directly into neighbouring coastal and port communities. Analysis of Europe’s 100 largest ports shows that Rotterdam is the most polluted with costs at €350 million, followed by Antwerp (€220 million), Marseille (€120 million), Hamburg (€120 million), and Genoa (€110 million).

According to the report, removing loopholes in the EU rules would save Europe €1.56bn in health and climate costs. Under current EU rules, outlined in the Alternative Fuels Infrastructure Regulation (AFIR), only large container ships, ferries and cruise ships are required to plug into dockside electricity sockets, meaning oil and gas tankers, bulk carriers, car carriers and other cargo ships are left out entirely. Requirements to connect should be progressively extended to all ship types in the upcoming review to cut emissions and grant ports the necessary long-term predictability.

Eloi Nordé, shipping policy officer at T&E, said: “Port communities are paying the price for ships running their engines 24/7 at port. Fossil fuels only seem cheap because companies offload health and climate costs onto the public. Fortunately, the solution already exists. Plugging ships into shoreside electric power would save society billions and make port cities far healthier to live in.”

On paper, marine fuels appear less costly than drawing electricity from the port grid. However, this is largely because the associated healthcare and environmental burdens are absorbed by the local population rather than ship operators. Once these external costs are factored into energy pricing, generating power from fossil fuels at dock is twice as costly as using shore electricity.

This policy gap creates a lot of planning uncertainty for EU port authorities and electricity grid operators. Without a predictable policy progressively including all vessel types, ports are disincentivised from making long-term investments in green infrastructure, says T&E.

The EU will review the regulation at the end of this year. T&E urges EU policymakers to use the AFIR review to expand shore power to all ships, batteries, and boilers, backed by national port electrification roadmaps before 2032.

Its call is echoed today in a joint letter to the European Commission from the Clean Port Cities (CPC) coalition, uniting industry, civil society, and maritime and energy stakeholders including DFDS and Green Transition Denmark, urging predictable shore power timelines for all vessels, battery charging rules, and faster grid access.

¹ The port-health research cited refers to Mei et al. (2023), “Long-term effects of air pollutants on respiratory and cardiovascular mortality in a port city along the Adriatic sea,” BMC Pulmonary Medicine.

Transport & Environment’s (T&E) vision is a zero-emission mobility system that is affordable and has minimal impacts on our health, climate and environment. Created over 30 years ago, we have shaped some of Europe’s most important environmental laws.

#5601. Department Of Defense Wants Ford And GM To Make Drones

Happy Tuesday! It's November 24, 2026, and this is The Morning Shift — your daily roundup of the top automotive headlines from around the world, in one place. This is where you'll find the most important stories that are shaping the way Americans drive and get around.

In this morning's edition we're looking at the Department of Defense getting GM and Ford into the drone business, and Tesla pushing its driver assistance on the elderly. We'll also look at Volkswagen's battle against its workers, and Ineos moving stateside.

If you'd like a recap of the latest auto news sent to your inbox each weekday, you can sign up for Jalopnik's free Morning Shift newsletter here.

Under the Trump administration, the United States government has shifted from a focus on soft power — international aid, alliances, things of that nature — to focusing entirely on hard military power. Gone are the days of USAID, now is the era of massing drones. Only, there's a problem: U.S. drone makers can't actually make enough to keep up with Department of Defense orders. Thus, a new push from the Pentagon, to get automakers into the drone business. From the Wall Street Journal:

The Pentagon is in talks with General Motors and other manufacturers to help mass produce drones, according to people familiar with the matter, the latest effort by the U.S. military to enlist the car industry's sprawling assembly lines to increase production of critical munitions and equipment.

Under a deal, auto companies would supply parts to drone companies, the people said. Among the scenarios: GM is weighing whether to manufacture drone motors, albeit closer in size to something that would power a riding lawn mower than a car. Defense officials have met with leaders at Ford Motor, Canadian parts giant Magna International and other manufacturing companies, including outside the auto industry.

Responses from automakers around drone building have been "mixed," according to the WSJ, but we live in an era where the President can hand-pick who runs companies in spite of all manner of allegations. Do we really think any U.S.-based company in its right mind would say no to such a man? Maybe an automaker could stall until the new legislative session in January, but that doesn't remove Trump from power.

Tesla doesn't so much care about selling its cars as it does selling its software these days, and the company seems to have its sights set on a new frontier of driver-assistance software: The elderly. If you're wondering what happens when you combine technology that lulls drivers into a false sense of security with drivers whose reflexes and powers of perception are failing them, well, we're about to find out. From Bloomberg:

Tesla warns consumers that FSD requires drivers to pay attention and be prepared to take immediate action at all times. It acknowledges none of its driver-assistance features make Teslas autonomous. And yet the company is increasingly promoting the merits of the system to seniors, further muddling the already complex question of when the moment has come for older drivers to give up their car keys.

Experts say the technology's limitations may actually raise risks for the elderly. The pitfalls of aging, including changes in vision, loss of physical mobility and slower reflexes, can impair an older driver's ability to safely take control when needed.

The core issue here is a thorny one. On the one hand, seniors still need to get around — grocery delivery isn't always an option on a fixed income, and socializing staves off dementia. On the other hand, people whose faculties are decaying probably shouldn't be controlling thousands of pounds of metal that hurtle through our roads, especially with software that only demands occasional-yet-immediate engagement rather than constant attention. The answer is buses, but no one wants to talk about that.

Volkswagen, one of the world's largest carmakers, thinks it's too big. To compete with lower-cost competitors from China, the company is trying to scale back its own costs — by cutting production and people. Those people are, understandably, not super psyched about these decisions. From Reuters:

EMDEN, Germany, Nov 24 (Reuters) – Volkswagen began crunch ‌wage talks with labour representatives on Tuesday amid mounting worker anger over deep cost cuts, with protesters carrying black coffins to symbolise what they said was the death of trust and the company's future prospects.

The first round of talks takes place in the northern German ​town of Emden, home to one of the factories fighting for survival, where around 4,000 workers protested before representatives of the IG Metall ​union ad the automaker's works council met Volkswagen management.

"If necessary, there will also be industrial action of a kind never before seen at Volkswagen," works council head Daniela Cavallo told workers chanting "strike-ready" and carrying black coffins inscribed with the words "future" and "trust".

When your workers start carrying coffins around to show the long-term costs of your short-sighted decision making, it's probably worth listening to them. Volkswagen's idea to cut its staff and production capacity to the bone is the sort of move that works on a spreadsheet, but realistically hampers the company's ability to be agile in a market that's seen constant changes from shifting international regulations and new competitors. Essentially, the company is looking to specialize in doing things in a way that works right now, with the hope that the current circumstances never change.

Ineos, a company I occasionally remember exists and is real, wants to sell in the U.S. without tariffs. To do so, it's looking to start production on our shores, and it's going to make the numbers work by getting rid of one of its European factories — specifically, by selling a French factory to BYD. From Automotive News:

Ineos Automotive, hampered by President Donald Trump's import tariffs, is ready to build in the U.S.

The small British automaker said it has identified a short list of U.S. sites where it plans to produce pickups and SUVs. At the same time the company, in a major pivot, plans to sell its French assembly plant to Chinese automaker BYD.

The two moves would have major repercussions on both sides of the Atlantic Ocean: Ineos would gain a manufacturing footprint in the country where it generates 70 percent of its global sales, and BYD would collect another European plant.

This is the kind of knock-on effect that's hard to predict when you institute tariffs. Sure, production of the Ineos Grenadier expands into the U.S., and that plays well with the UAW folks from whom Trump wants votes, it also enables a further BYD foothold in Europe, though, emboldening a Chinese competitor that Trump would rather see quashed.

This bastion of legalized slavery is for women!

God only knows what the hell is happening with the economy now. Gas prices were skyrocketing, and now nothing's all that different but the prices are plummeting. Stocks were bad when Jensen Huang had affairs, but now he's back in the job that enabled all the affairs and the stocks are good, but later today they're set to be bad again. Anyone who says they can predict anything is a liar.

Demi, it's me! I'm the lead singer of The Decemberists! I'm Colin Meloy! I'm in The Decemberists!

#5600. These Are The Real Cars You Wish Were In 'Grand Theft Auto 6'

"Grand Theft Auto 6" has been out for over a week at this point, so if you play it, you're probably well acquainted with the seemingly endless number of cars you can drive in the game. However, like all GTA games, none of them are real. Sure, they're all pretty close to what we get in the real world, but they're not exactly one-to-one. There are plenty of reasons why that is, but it can still leave ya (read: me) wishing that there were some real cars in the game, and that idea is what led me to the question I asked you all last week.

I wanted to know what real cars you'd want to be able to drive in "Grand Theft Auto 6". For the most part, you folks played along, giving me real answers, and I do appreciate that. Some of you wieners, however, decided that it would be a good time to let everyone know you don't play video games, and I'd like to be the first person to say, you are so, so interesting. Can I kiss you on the mouth? Because no one has ever been cooler than you. They should give you an award.

In any case, why don't ya scroll on down below and check out what real cars your fellow Jalpos wish they could drive in "GTA 6," and if you need to reiterate that you aren't interested in GTA and you don't play video games, please kick rocks with open-toed shoes.

A 1974 Dodge Monaco with a cop motor, a 440 cubic inch plant, cop tires, cop suspension, and cop shocks. It should be a model made before catalytic converters so it'll run good on regular gas.

A Japanese dekotora truck. I wished for one in GTA 5 Online. It's wild, unique, and would fit right in driving down Ocean Drive at night.

It was cool in GTA V when they had an Abarth 500 because I love the RL car so much. But one thing that constantly eluded the car nerds in GTA was the Miata (minus the Mañana from LCS), and at this point I'd love any generation. For a game that honors cars so well, I'm shocked they didn't make even the NA, a car with a cult-like following, nicknamed "The Answer" by so many car enthusiasts, and available in any respectable (and un-respectable) racing title you can think of, and yet more niche stuff has entered the game before the Miata did.

I think a Miata would fit right in driving around Vice City.

An Audi RS6 avant would be nice for all the errands. Come to think f it there are no Audis in the game when they ubiquitous in GTAV

MINE! I've driven down to Miami a couple times with my 968, and what I'm looking forward to most is re-creating some of the photos I've taken down there.

I don't expect to find a 968 clone in game, but they've got to at least have a 928 clone. The Scarface reference is right there.

If I played any video games at all – for GTA I would want to drive a car that I have personally owned, so my choice is between a 1979 Ford LTD 2dr coupe with the 351ci V8 and a 1994 GEO Metro XFi with a 50 hp 1L 3cyl.

Did a little digging to see what vehicle can be easy (but ungodly expensive) to purchase, register, and take care of everything the GTA games are known for. Besides, with this kind of truck, I'll have it paid off pretty quickly after a few quick hits. It's the Alpine Armoring MASTIFF Limited Edition. It's a counter-terrorism, extreme-threat customized Ford pickup, and it can stop up to .50 caliber BMG rounds. It does cost over $400,000 though, but how much is my life worth? Plus it looks durable enough for those high speed chases down the beach, causing beachgoers to flee and umbrellas to burst into confetti. There's plenty of room for massive bags of cash, my associates, and enough weaponry to take over a small European country.

A cargo delivery van, just imagine how many flowers you could buy for her.

There are so many IRL cars that I only came to love after seeing their GTA equivalents. I'd never seen or heard of the R32-R34 Nissan Skylines until I saw the Elegy Retro Custom in GTA Online. I also thought the Sabre Turbo looked stupid until I dechromed the rear bumper and thought, "okay that looks rad" — it's based on the Oldsmobile Cutlass and 442. Also, I loved the Ford Falcon XB from Mad Max before seeing its derivative in GTA Online, so as soon as the Apocalypse Imperator came out I bought it.

I don't think I'd want to actually drive any particular IRL cars in GTA 6 without the ability to substantially customize and mod them though, especially if the "base civilian version" is boring and anemic compared to the cars the game pushes you toward driving. Filling a garage with your "favorite cars" but never being able to really drive them beyond RP purposes kinda sucks.

None, I've almost never been satisfied by driving a hero car of mine on a video game. They always end up being way too fast and uncontrollable or feel stupidly slow because they're old. Especially cars I've driven/owned, every one I've been able to drive in a game has been unsatisfying. I'd much rather develop favorites in-game as I play than pine for certain cars to be added.

#5597. Stellanis Confirms Nearly All Its Racing Programs Are Dead

Peugeot might have major racing victories in three different centuries, but that heritage wasn't enough to save its Le Mans Hypercar program. Stellantis announced on Monday that it's pulling the plug on all its factory motorsports programs, confirming last week's reports. The automotive giant won't fully shutter the motorsport division, hoping to still generate revenue from customer racing. The only projects to avoid the axe were its North American subsidiary's revived NASCAR efforts under the Dodge and Ram brands.

Stellantis stated it intends to "refocus its motorsport activities and resources on customer racing and high-performance vehicle development." Amid the company's dire financial situation, CEO Antonio Filosa set a goal early this year of $6.6 billion in cost savings by 2028. The decision was sudden for the programs. Peugeot planned to compete with an updated 9X8 in next year's FIA World Endurance Championship and had already begun testing the new prototype. With Alpine's previously announced departure, this means there will be no French manufacturer in the top class of the 2027 24 Hours of Le Mans.

There were two silver linings to Stellantis' decision. First, the company will be continuing its customer racing programs. Second, the company's two Formula E teams will be allowed to contest the upcoming season. Citroën and Opel collaborated in developing new cars for the debut Gen4 campaign. These 800-horsepower machines will get a single season in the sun (or rain) before getting sent to the scrap heap. It's a similar situation to what happened to Lancia's WRC2 program, with the Ypsilon Rally2 HF Integrale running its maiden season this year before Stellantis pulled the plug.

As much as some auto executives champion the "win on Sunday, sell on Monday" mentality, the exposure of being in a motorsport series only truly exists if you're competing in the front half of the field. Using Formula 1 as an example, Aston Martin and Cadillac might as well not exist to TV cameras unless their cars crash or get lapped by the leaders. Saving the Peugeot 9X8 program would be a hard sell for anyone because LMH went five years without winning a WEC race or finishing a Le Mans podium.

#5596. Rotterdam’s Methanol Expansion Is Not an Inland Shipping Fuel Strategy

Rotterdam is expanding its methanol and ethanol business for industrial customers and ocean-going ships. Its inland freight fleet needs a different energy strategy. Confusing cargo, marine bunkering and propulsion would commit port authorities to parallel fuel networks when regular freight routes offer a stronger case for direct electrification.

In May 2026, Tankmatch’s inland bunker vessel MTS Experience delivered certified biomethanol and second-generation ethanol to X-Press Feeders’ ocean-going Eco Levant. The fuels were transferred separately and blended aboard the receiving ship into 90% methanol and 10% ethanol. It was Rotterdam’s first ethanol bunkering of a seagoing vessel. The receiving ship burned the alcohol blend. The inland barge delivered it as cargo. Those are two different businesses.

Rotterdam had already announced an expansion of Evos’s Europoort terminal. Five storage tanks totaling 67,500 m³ are planned for methanol and ethanol, along with an inland-shipping berth scheduled for completion in early 2028. The berth includes shore power for vessels alongside. The project expands industrial storage, cargo transshipment and marine bunkering capacity. Developing an electric inland-freight corridor requires a separate charging and battery-service plan.

Low-carbon alcohols have a role in shipping services that still need liquid fuel. Aviation competes for oils and fats suitable for renewable diesel, leaving other sustainable feedstocks for marine alcohol fuels. I reassessed biomethanol’s role in the residual liquid-fuel market and examined alcohol-fuel generators as range extenders for hybrid ships. The investment test is lifecycle emissions, delivered fuel cost and demand from ships that need liquid energy.

The five planned tanks add 67,500 m³ of storage capacity across several markets. Rotterdam’s biomethanol bunkering grew from about 3,900 tons to almost 12,000 tons in 2025, according to the port authority. That is growth from a small base in the wider bunker market. Long-term investment still depends on recurring customers, contracted volumes and infrastructure utilization.

Ocean-going ships arrive with different engines, fuel contracts and routes. International ports will serve several bunker fuels for years. Los Angeles and Long Beach are approaching this market in stages, beginning with the fuels visiting vessels can buy and the commercial commitments needed for expansion. My assessment of those ports makes the same distinction between proving safe delivery and proving repeatable demand.

Inland shipping is a separate energy business. Many freight routes repeat between a limited number of ports and terminals. Charging equipment, onboard storage and battery exchange can follow sailing schedules, cargo cycles and terminal availability. A shared electrical network can serve dry cargo barges, chemical tankers and container vessels with different battery configurations and common interfaces.

My 98-page inland freight electrification report models five complete loaded-and-empty route cycles. A local port-canal shuttle requires 1.8 MWh, regional industrial service 4.3 MWh, irregular service 8.3 MWh, a major-port corridor 13.1 MWh and a long industrial corridor 36.8 MWh. These planning values precede vessel design margins. The twentyfold spread calls for different battery sizes, exchange frequencies and charging arrangements within a common electric propulsion system.

Some predictable services can charge fixed batteries during normal stops. Others benefit from exchangeable 20-foot battery containers, with smaller onboard batteries supplying hotel loads and resilience. Long corridors need reliable intermediate energy points. Hazardous-cargo tankers need cargo-pump integration, electrical segregation, fire protection and appropriate approvals. Those are problems of vessel design, safety approval and electrical infrastructure. Adding hydrogen, LNG and methanol supply chains adds costs of its own.

The Dutch Zero Emission Services model is already operating at modest scale. It lists five charging stations, two vessels in service and twelve conversion subsidies. Battery-container ownership and energy service can be separated from vessel ownership. Its shared interfaces demonstrate how multiple operators can purchase energy service from the same battery and charging network.

The Central Commission for the Navigation of the Rhine records battery, hydrogen and LNG vessels, then argues that inland navigation has no universal propulsion solution. Its selected deployment database includes passenger vessels as well as freight. That inventory tells us which technologies have been tried. Comparing their economics and infrastructure costs is a separate task. Inland fleets can use varied hulls, battery sizes and charging arrangements while converging on electricity as the main propulsion energy carrier.

Port authorities need three distinct investment cases. Industrial alcohol storage needs chemical customers and cargo commitments. Marine bunkering needs ocean-going ship demand, fuel certification and contracts. Inland propulsion needs route-by-route electricity requirements, grid connections, terminal windows and a dependable energy service. Sharing port land and utilities can lower costs. Confusing the three markets can multiply them.

Rotterdam can expand methanol and ethanol handling while building a common electric backbone for inland freight. The barge carrying methanol does not need to burn it. Port investment should follow the service purchased by each customer.

My TFIE Strategy Briefing assessment examines Rotterdam’s three energy businesses, the different inland route requirements and the investment implications. The supporting 98-page report contains the route energy and vessel architecture analysis.

#5595. Maybe Just Stay Off The Roads This Thanksgiving, Folks

The weeks surrounding the Thanksgiving holiday are some of the busiest travel days of the year in the United States. This year, AAA projects that 81.5 million Americans will travel at least 50 miles from home between Tuesday November 24 and Monday, November 30, and it projects that 72.8 million of those Americans will be making that drive by car. That's actually a slight drop from last year's result of 73.1 million travelers, but AAA says travelers in major metropolitan areas could still expect to see more than double the normal traffic congestion. We think that may even be a conservative estimate, given that weather forecasts across the country tell a stormy story.

A coastal flood advisory has been issued for the Pacific Northwest and parts of Northern California, and there's currently a nor'easter storm that looks like it will bring heavy rains, winds, and coastal flooding to the entire East Coast over the course of Wednesday and Thursday. The mayor of San Francisco even posted a video to social media urging people to avoid unnecessary travel, saying, "If you don't need to, please don't travel into the city. We always want visitors, but these are special circumstances." We got all your wet weather driving tips to help folks prepare for the Super El Niño a few months ago, so feel free to revisit that if you can't avoid driving in stormy conditions.

The National Weather Service determined that the nor'easter storm strengthening off the southeastern coast could undergo rapid cyclogenesis and become a bomb cyclone by Thanksgiving day. It's also expected to bring powerful winds with gusts of over 50 mph along the coast and 40 mph inland. The most significant effects are expected to impact the mid-Atlantic and Northeast, so be sure to check the weather forecast in your area before making your elderly grandparents brave treacherous roads.

West Coast travelers should be aware of the impact of potentially record-high king tides as well as a storm that's expected to bring rain, high-elevation snow, and dense fog from San Francisco all the way up the coast to Southern Washington. This king tide is expected to amplify the already elevated sea levels that have been affecting the West Coast thanks to the Super El Niño's higher water temperatures, potentially causing a surge of more than eight feet in San Francisco Bay. Waves are also expected to become dangerously large, reaching sizes of 20 to 30 feet on Wednesday. Federal experts say San Francisco International Airport (SFO) and Oakland San Francisco Bay Airport (OAK), two of the largest airports in California's populous Bay Area, will flood this week from the huge tides. If you've been looking for an excuse to avoid the holiday chaos and stay home to play more GTA, just blame the weather forecast and order takeout.

#5594. Ianalumab Extension Study Confirms Benefit in Sjogren's, Though Still Modest

— Much will hinge on third phase III study now underway

For patients with Sjögren's disease, a second year of treatment with the investigational anti-B cell drug ianalumab boosted response totals, raising the likelihood that it could eventually win approval.

Research presented at the American College of Rheumatology's recent annual meeting showed further improvements in EULAR Sjögren's Syndrome Disease Activity Index (ESSDAI) scores after already improving significantly, relative to placebo, with an initial 48 weeks of treatment.

Ianalumab targets B-cell activating factor (BAFF) and is also lethal to B cells. The expectation is that this double whammy will make the drug more durably effective than other B-cell depleting agents like rituximab (Rituxan). It's also being tested in a range of other conditions marked by B-cell pathology, including lupus and systemic sclerosis.

As previously reported, participants in the so-called NEPTUNUS-1 and NEPTUNUS-2 randomized trials saw an average 6.5-point decrease from baseline in ESSDAI scores after the first 48 weeks of ianalumab treatment at 300 mg monthly. Continuing on that regimen in the open-label extension study for another 60 weeks brought an additional 1.0-point improvement, according to Thomas Dörner, MD, of Charité Universitätsmedizin Berlin, and colleagues.

More impressively, responder totals also increased substantially in the extension. Whereas 58.9% of patients in the main trials had achieved at least 5-point reductions in ESSDAI scores, the proportion rose to 68.4% by week 108. Numbers for the fraction of patients achieving ESSDAI scores less than 5, indicating low disease activity, were identical -- 58.9% at week 48 and 68.4% at week 108.

As well, almost one-quarter of participants initially randomized to monthly ianalumab achieved clinical remission (ESSDAI=0) at week 108, up from 13% at week 48.

Patients randomized to placebo in the main trials were re-randomized in the extension to receive ianalumab, at doses of 300 mg given either monthly or every 3 months. Among those getting the drug monthly, they too saw substantial improvements. Basically, by week 108, they reached the same degree of symptom relief that the original ianalumab groups had achieved at week 48.

All this is good news for ianalumab's sponsor, Novartis, for which the original NEPTUNUS findings were somewhat disappointing. Yes, the primary endpoint of a significant advantage over placebo in change-from-baseline in ESSDAI score was met in both trials, but just barely (P=0.0496 in NEPTUNUS-1). And other outcomes either failed to show a significant difference from placebo or the effect sizes were too small to matter much.

Although safety did not look to be a substantial problem -- and the extension data appeared to confirm that -- the modest-at-best efficacy did not bode well for FDA approval. So Novartis is now sponsoring another placebo-controlled phase III trial focusing on patients with a high symptom burden, the better to see a clear difference between the drug and placebo in effectiveness.

Two ianalumab doses were tested in the NEPTUNUS trials, 300 mg monthly or every 3 months. Those patients continued on these doses, while the placebo groups were re-assigned between them. The latter got their first ianalumab dose at week 60, meaning they then were under active treatment for 48 weeks at data cut-off for the current report.

Of the original 666 patients in the two studies, 610 entered the extension and 551 remained through week 108. In total, the extension is slated to last 5 years beyond the initial 1-year, double-blind period. The current report focused on those patients receiving monthly dosing in the extension, a total of 358, of which about one-third were from the original placebo groups.

A key secondary outcome was change in salivary flow. Participants with a modicum of flow at baseline did better than those with only minimal flow in the primary results, and this remained true in the extension. Dörner and colleagues stratified participants according to whether baseline salivary flow was more or less than 0.4 mL/min. The low-flow group saw little benefit from ianalumab, a 0.1-mL/min increase by week 48, and it remained at about that level through week 108 among those receiving the drug throughout. Not much improvement was seen, either, in the original placebo group after switching to ianalumab.

It was quite different in the group with baseline flow >0.4 mL/min. Those patients saw a 0.2-unit boost in flow by week 48, with an additional jump of 0.2 mL/min at week 72, although the mean value with continuous ianalumab at week 108 fell back to the level seen at week 48. Flow levels actually fell with placebo during the main trials, but recovered back to baseline levels with ianalumab in the extension.

No new side-effect concerns arose in the extension. At the time of data cut-off this past June, some patients had already been on the drug for 4 years (mean 135 weeks); 3% had experienced a serious adverse event considered related to therapy, with 1% stopping the therapy because of such events. Systemic reactions to drug injections occurred in 12%-14% of patients; these appeared to wane as patients continued with treatment.

#5593. 1970 Plymouth Hemi 'Cuda For Sale With 58,000 Miles Shown Is Racing Royalty With A Wild History

Special is subjective — even when it comes to cars. A car that is special to you might just be junk to somebody else. Sorry to burst your bubble, but not every car can count itself among the unicorns and truly special automotive special editions. Then again, other cars are special, full stop. Cars with the sort of presence that makes the little hairs on the back of your neck stand at attention. Understandably, most of us don't often get to see such cars, like the Plymouth Hemi 'Cuda. Big V8 power and an even bigger personality quickly established the short-lived E-Body Hemi 'Cuda as a muscle car mainstay.

Every once in a great while, one of these special Mopar muscle cars comes up for sale. But seldom does one with this sort of record head to the auction block. This is not a Hollywood car, nor was it celebrity-owned. No, this 1970 Plymouth Hemi 'Cuda has a racing rap sheet longer than your arm, teeming with victories and championship wins. Better yet, the 426-cubic-inch Hemi V8-powered racing legend racked up its impressive record as a primary factory-backed race car for Ecurie Chrysler in France, of all places. This is one well-traveled 'Cuda. Now, the esteemed asphalt-kicking American ambassador and its 58,000-mile odometer are back in the U.S. of A. and up for grabs courtesy of Bring a Trailer.

You might not expect a 1970 Plymouth Hemi 'Cuda to rack up race wins in France, but this 'Cuda did just that. Henri Chemin, the Chrysler France competition director at the time, drove the car in many Fédération Internationale de l'Automobile (FIA) and Fédération Française du Sport Automobile (FFSA) events. Chemin managed nine victories in 1970, 16 wins in 1971, and seven victories in 1972. Jean-François Mas took the wheel in 1973, winning 12 times in 1973. This Hemi 'Cuda's last recorded victory was the handiwork of Jean-Claude Géral, who drove the muscle car to victory at the 1974 hillclimb event in Charnizay. All said and done, the 'Cuda claimed 45 Group 1 wins and three FIA championships between 1970 and 1974.

So, what state is the car in today? For starters, the restored-and-retired racer packs a 426-cubic-inch Hemi V8. That Hemi replaced the 'Cuda's original mill during the 1971 season. Should the next owner want to set up a display, the sale includes the original engine block, as well as extensive documentation, photos, a Simca en Compétition racing book, and Chemin's press clippings. A Hurst pistol-grip shifter commands the 'Cuda's four-speed manual transmission, while a Shaker hood and air dam help the old Mopar look the part.

In keeping with its racing DNA, the old Plymouth features a roll cage, Simpson harnesses, and a white-and-blue racing livery. Other features include 15" Rally wheels in a complementary white hue, body-colored bumpers, front disc brakes, a wood-rimmed steering wheel, an ATL fuel cell, and a dual exhaust system with Flowmaster mufflers.

We don't have to tell you that racing is dangerous — for both driver and machine. Duh, right? Well, at the Brides–Méribel hillclimb event in 1970, the Ecurie Chrysler Hemi 'Cuda met with a motorsport disaster of its own. Piloted by Chemin, the 'Cuda's power steering failed, causing the car to crash into a ravine. The crash was as bad as it sounds. Ecurie Chrysler deemed the Plymouth's body shell beyond repair. So, the racing team's engineers and mechanics salvaged everything they could, including the original engine, much of the drivetrain, the gauges, and the seats. They then transplanted it all into a replacement E-body from a 440 Six-Pack 'Cuda that Chemin had brought into France with the original factory-backed car.

Want this one? You're going to pay for it. As of this writing, the high bid for the Ex-Ecurie Chrysler France 1970 Plymouth Hemi 'Cuda is $110,000. Now, stock and well-preserved Hemi 'Cudas routinely net six-figure sums when they go up for sale. Don't believe it? This 1970 Plymouth Hemi 'Cuda with 31,300 miles that we reported on last month sold for $176,000 on Mecum. Add a factory convertible configuration into the mix, and a third-gen Hemi 'Cuda has the potential to cross the block for seven figures. After all, just 12 factory rag-top Hemi 'Cudas rolled off the line as 1971 models, making the classic Plymouth a 'Holy Grail' of muscle cars.

There's plenty of space for this motorsport Mopar to sell for big bucks. We just hope someone buys this tenured racing machine and lets it off its leash from time to time. It would be a shame for this one to spend too much time locked away from view with an odometer reading under 60,000 miles.

#5592. Digital Outages Are Rampant All Across America. Is AI to blame?

Coincidence is not causation, and yet the urge to ascribe causative power to random events is a typically human characteristic. Take the case of the man who snaps his fingers every five minutes. When a neighbor asks why, he replies, “To keep the elephants away.” “But there are no elephants anywhere around here,” the neighbor points out. “See?” the man says. “It’s working!”

Last week, people who rely on Microsoft Outlook to manage their lives suddenly found it was not working properly. The next day, the FAA issued a rare “ground stop” order because pieces of the digital system that air traffic controllers rely on failed.

The following Monday, four of the country’s largest banks found their computer systems were malfunctioning, and United Airlines experienced a computer outage that exacerbated the travails of long suffering passengers still trying to recover from the “ground stop” fiasco. Then on Tuesday, the National Security Agency’s internal communications network went offline, and Apple web services were disrupted.

According to the New York Times, Senator Josh Hawley, a Republican from Missouri, told reporters last week, “I think we ought to be briefed thoroughly on what is actually happening here. If this is all a coincidence, it’s like the greatest coincidence in the history of the world.”

The president of the United States offered this reassuring statement: “It’s life. When you’re big and powerful and have all the money, things like this happen. It’s fine.”

Not surprisingly, with all the talk about AI taking over the world and employees quitting their jobs to warn people of the danger, quite a few people are wondering if AI is responsible for all these digital disruptions.

For some Americans, the timing and scale of the outages made it hard to believe that sheer coincidence was the explanation. In a curious way, AI is involved, but not in the way you might think.

Ever since the digital revolution began more than 50 years ago, the computers we depend on have only been able to do what humans tell them to do. Those instructions are created by coders — people who use the programming language of the day that tell computers what to do and how to do it.

It is well known in the trade that millions of lines of bad code exist. It is a phenomenon known as “garbage in, garbage out” or GIGO. Digital firms employ phalanxes of people to check the code their coders write, but it is a tedious process that often misses even glaring errors. Curiously, the most effective way to find bad code seems to be by using artificial intelligence, which can scan 10,000 times more lines of code in a given amount of time than a human can do.

Tech startup Calif said in a blog post this week that, using AI, it had found a critical flaw in Java4Shell, which leaves much of the internet exposed to cyber attacks if it is not corrected. Java is a popular programming language used by most Fortune 500 companies, according to Politico.

“Banking, aviation, telecommunications, nearly every critical sector runs on Java, including national security systems,” Calif wrote. It calls Java “the beating heart of the modern economy.” Oracle, the developer of Java, released a patch for the vulnerability Tuesday. South Korea’s intelligence agency reportedly sent a separate security advisory earlier Tuesday to public institutions, warning them to fix the issue and prevent hackers from exploiting the Java flaw.

The Java vulnerability comes weeks after a separate major vulnerability was discovered in another popular programming language, PHP, which powers up to 70 percent of the internet. At the time, governments worldwide advised organizations to take their sites offline to patch the vulnerability and prevent breaches.

Director of National Intelligence Jay Clayton said on Fox News that ongoing efforts to patch the PHP vulnerability took an internal National Security Agency communications system offline Tuesday. He added that AI is making it easier to find such vulnerabilities, which can take years to fix without using the power of AI to find them.

Peter Avery is the chief information and security officer for Velocity Security, a cyber security firm in Michigan. He told the New York Times he was skeptical that the events of last week were connected. “I like facts,” he said. “And until now, these are all separate issues.”

Drawing patterns where they do not yet exist, he said, is human nature. It’s part of the need to make sense of things, he said, but can lead down a dark road. “When you start to live in that world where everyone’s a liar and no one is telling the truth, you are starting to live in fear.”

Maybe snapping your fingers every five minutes is our best defense against cyber criminals stealing our data and crashing our online persona. That’s not a particularly comforting thought.

Steve writes about the interface between technology and sustainability from his home in Florida or anywhere else The Force may lead him. He is proud to be "woke" and believes weak leaders push others down while strong leaders lift others up. You can follow him on Substack at https://stevehanley.substack.com/ but not on Fakebook or any social media platforms controlled by narcissistic yahoos.
