#5591. The True Cost of In-Port Emissions

How current shore power rules cost hundreds of millions every year in health and climate damages.

Air pollution and climate emissions from ships at berth impose a heavy and unaddressed burden on European port communities and local economies. While the Alternative Fuels Infrastructure Regulation (AFIR) took an important first step by mandating Onshore Power Supply (OPS) for large container and passenger vessels, several blind spots leave over half of all in-port emissions unregulated.

To quantify the cost of inaction, we evaluated at-berth emissions across the 100 largest EU ports using CE Delft’s Handbook on the External Costs of Transport. Based on 2024 MRV data, in-port emissions cost €2.53 billion every year in health damages and climate abatement costs. Covering only the electricity demand from large unregulated ship types would already save €500 million annually, while tackling boilers would save another €714 million. Shipping companies effectively offload these costs onto public budgets, making fossil fuels appear artificially cheap. Once all impacts are counted, using electricity when at berth is half as expensive as burning fossil fuels.

While AFIR successfully kickstarted early shore power roll-out, its narrow scope leaves significant volumes of emissions unaddressed. Beyond health and climate impacts, this partial coverage creates uncertainty and deprives port authorities, network operators , and investors from the predictability they need to develop and deploy the required infrastructure. Providing a clear time horizon for the provision of shore power to all ships is essential to share infrastructure costs, reduce electricity prices and permanently clean up air in ports.

By Eloi Nordé, Shipping Policy Officer, T&E

Transport & Environment’s (T&E) vision is a zero-emission mobility system that is affordable and has minimal impacts on our health, climate and environment. Created over 30 years ago, we have shaped some of Europe’s most important environmental laws.

#5590. Slate Thinks 205 Miles Is More Than Enough Range

Slate Auto is in a strange spot. Yes, with a starting price of $26,400, including destination, the two-door pickup truck it's selling is cheap compared to the competition, but it also lacks basic features like power windows and a radio at that price. With options that'll make the truck more livable for everyday use, prices will quickly climb. No matter which options they choose, they'll still be saddled with the same 205 miles of range. Even though that's quite meager by today's standards, Slate feels that's more than enough to win over customers.

It makes sense why the Slate has such a shoddy range, even compared to its competition. The battery is the single most expensive part of an electric vehicle, so an automaker looking to make a cheap EV will use a smaller battery. There's a view that Slate's approach could work for folks who are already comfortable with EVs, but it might not translate for those more accustomed to what gas-powered vehicles offer, according to Automotive News. There's also the issue that similarly priced rivals will offer more range on base models, and they usually have the option of extended-range batteries.

Still, Slate feels 205 miles is really all the average American needs from a vehicle like its truck, the cheapest EV and pickup for sale in the U.S. To be fair, it's also the smallest pickup in the U.S. — about two feet shorter than a Ford Maverick. In a marketing email sent earlier this month, Slate said 205 miles is "more than [many people] need each day." That's sort of fair, because according to a 2024 survey done by AAA, the average American drives about 31 miles per day, meaning that from full to empty, you'd only need to charge the Slate about once a week. Of course, no one actually drives like that, and the range will never actually be 205 miles, but we can't really account for that.

Slate isn't the only company offering an EV with less-than-stellar range. The Kia EV3 compact crossover, which starts at $31,385, comes as standard with 221 miles of range. While the starting prices are far different, the EV3 comes with a lot more from the get-go. To get the Slate to a comparable place, you've got to add a bed kit that brings in seats and a roof — giving the Slate "crossover" a $31,400 base price, according to AutoNews. It'll still be missing plenty of other things at that price, like a radio and electric windows.

In any case, Kia doesn't expect this low-range EV3 to be a big seller — capturing just 3 to 5% of its sales. The higher Wind trim, with its 321 miles of range and $36,485 starting price, is expected to be the volume seller with about 60% of sales. That sort of speaks to Slate's problem. It seems like folks would rather pay the extra money to not have to worry about range anxiety or charging up their cars as often.

In reality, the EV3 isn't a direct competitor to the Slate, but Ford's yet-to-be-unveiled Fathom pickup is. It's expected to have a standard battery with around 250 miles of range and an optional battery with over 300 miles, Automotive News reports. Despite the fact it range-mogs the Slate, its starting price will come in at $29,945, including shipping, and it'll be an actual vehicle with the sort of modern luxuries and conveniences you'd expect in today's day and age.

I'm not saying that the Slate is dead on arrival by any means, but I do think the Jeff Bezos-backed company is really, really hoping that there are a lot of Quirked Up Individuals out there who will fall for the proletariat virtual signals of crank windows, no radio, a key you've got to insert and no screen while also paying a price that doesn't really connect with that reality. I'm not sure they're out there.

#5589. Sjogren's Disease Brings a Lot More Misery Beyond Dryness

— Brain fog, fatigue, infections, death explored in new studies

Oral and ocular dryness are the hallmark features of Sjögren's disease, but understanding the full symptom burden is still an active area of investigation, with recent studies reported at the American College of Rheumatology's annual meeting shedding light on some of the condition's lesser-known aspects.

For example, one new study found that oral disease serious enough to cause tooth loss helps drive serious infections such as sepsis in Sjögren's patients, a risk that is worsened further with immunomodulators typically used to control the disease.

Another found that nonphysical symptoms including "brain fog" and fatigue are nearly as common as dryness in Sjögren's disease, and a third confirmed that patients face a significant risk for early mortality.

All in all, this research puts Sjögren's disease alongside conditions more famous for their broadly burdensome symptom spectrum, such as rheumatoid arthritis, systemic lupus erythematosus, and systemic sclerosis.

An analysis of U.S. commercial and Medicaid insurance claims led by Thomas Grader-Beck, MD, of Johns Hopkins University in Baltimore, looked at associations between oral disease indicators and infection rates among some 60,000 people with Sjögren's disease.

Those suffering tooth loss, typically the result of periodontal disease, were significantly more likely to also experience systemic infections -- modestly when five or fewer teeth were lost (OR 1.18, 95% CI 1.13-1.24), or more strongly with more extensive extractions (OR 1.69, 95% CI 1.36-2.11). Absolute risks were substantial too: whereas systemic infections were recorded for 36% of those with no tooth loss (itself an indicator of risk associated with Sjögren's disease), the percentage reached 43% in patients with up to five lost teeth and 54% among those with six or more lost.

Dental visits, too, tracked with systemic infection risk in a "dose-response" relationship. Odds ratios for infections rose with each additional visit in the previous year: 1.11 with one visit, 1.32 with two, 1.41 with three, and 1.60 for four or more (all P<0.001 relative to no dental visits).

Moreover, the researchers reported, use of immunomodulator therapy -- a category that spans virtually every drug used for Sjögren's disease including corticosteroids, antimalarials, biologics, and conventional immunosuppressants -- by itself raised the risk for infections by 49%.

Oral disease and immunosuppression are "independent, additive risk factors for systemic infection," the group concluded.

Lane Destro, PhD, and colleagues at the Sjögren's Foundation in Reston, Virginia, had the Harris Poll organization survey people with the condition last year, with a total of 6,360 responses. It found that so-called brain fog -- a feeling of mental sluggishness and inability to focus -- and fatigue were almost as common as salivary and ocular dryness (prevalences of 77% and 90%, respectively, versus 93% and 95%), and also in the same range as joint pain (81%).

Other nonphysical symptoms were common too, if less so than brain fog and fatigue. Some 73% of respondents complained of insomnia, 64% reported eye fatigue specifically, 56% said they suffered headaches, and 56% indicated feeling dizzy or faint.

Patients also complained of additional burdens. Close to 60% said Sjögren's disease had affected relations with their spouses or partners and two-thirds reported impacts on other family relationships. A large majority agreed that Sjögren's "adds an emotional burden to my life," while half said the disease made them feel lonely. Not surprisingly, most also reported difficulties with work and employment stemming from the diagnosis.

Furthermore, the problems arising from fatigue and brain fog were disproportionately burdensome for these quality-of-life (QoL) issues. Having relatively severe dry eyes and mouth, for example, raised the likelihood of experiencing negative QoL effects by factors of roughly 1.5 to 2; a high fatigue burden, on the other hand, raised the risk by factors of 4 to 10, and the negative impact of brain fog was nearly as great.

French researchers confirmed that patients with Sjögren's disease face increased mortality risk, in the same way as patients with other autoimmune inflammatory conditions such as lupus.

Led by Elodie Portier, MD, of Hôpital Bicêtre in Paris, the group examined vital statistics for a 395-member cohort of Sjögren's disease patients in France and compared them with the general national population. Followed from 2007 to 2025, the cohort saw 77 deaths. The rate was 41% higher than among similar-age French people (95% CI 11-76). The gap was even greater for a Sjögren's subgroup marked by high symptom burden (54%, 95% CI 10-110).

The researchers also identified several risk factors for early mortality in the Sjögren's cohort. In multivariate analysis, each year of older age added 13% to the risk, while being male nearly tripled the likelihood of death (HR 2.66, 95% CI 1.04-6.77).

Having more than mild disease was also a mortality risk. Multivariate analysis comparing patients with EULAR Sjögren's Syndrome Activity Index (ESSDAI) scores of 6 or more to those with ESSDAI 5 or less yielded a hazard ratio of 2.16 (95% CI 1.29-3.60). Each ESSDAI point conferred a 15% extra risk for mortality.

Patient-reported overall symptom burden showed a similar pattern, the researchers noted, but it fell just short of statistical significance.

#5588. Changing of the Guard: Chinese Automakers Overtake Japanese in 6 More Markets

There are various opinions on the Chinese auto market, as shown recently in discussions under this article, but no matter what your opinion is on the Chinese economy and auto market, you have to recognize that Chinese automakers — driven by their electric leaders — are gaining more market share quickly in markets around the world, while legacy automakers from the US, Europe, and Japan are losing market share.

In particular, I just saw a headline highlighting that Chinese automakers have overtaken Japanese automakers in 6 more countries this year. That adds to the 20 markets where Chinese automakers had already surpassed Japanese automakers in market share. One thousand bonus points to anyone who can guess the six new markets before scrolling past the image below.

The new markets where Chinese automakers are now outselling Japanese ones are: Brazil, Ghana, Italy, South Korea, Tunisia, and the United Kingdom. Again, these are spread across the globe about as much as possible. Chinese auto companies, especially due to their insanely competitive electric vehicles, are overtaking the old guard in one corner of the world after another.

This news comes to us from data from Mobility Global, via the South China Morning Post. “Chinese carmakers stand out for their competitive pricing, EV portfolios and market-specific advantages such as localisation, said Tatiana Hristov, the company’s director of global vehicle sales forecasts,” SCMP reports.

Of course, EV demand has also risen due to the ongoing oil wars going on in the world, most notably now the US war on Iran. “Chinese brands benefit in particular because they are normally cheaper, so they are more attractive to consumers who are increasingly price sensitive under the oil shock,” noted Xu Tianchen, an economist at the Economist Intelligence Unit. “Chinese brands benefit in particular because they are normally cheaper, so they are more attractive to consumers who are increasingly price sensitive under the oil shock.” Good point.

I think the question now is not whether Chinese automakers will continue to surpass Japanese automakers (and others) in markets around the globe, but rather which ones will be next. And how many markets will flip in 2027? We shall see….

#5587. New York Just Banned Subscriptions For Features Your Car Already Has

In-vehicle subscriptions are pretty universally maligned among car owners. Most folks don't like the feeling of having to pay every month for features their cars were supposed to come with, and now New York State is doing something about it. Governor Kathy Hochul just signed a bill that bars automakers, dealers and their agents from selling subscriptions for features that are already installed.

The new law, which takes effect on February 27, 2027, is part of a new section of the state's General Business Law, according to Spectrum News. You can read the law for yourself here. It lays out fairly simple ground rules when it comes to subscriptions dealers or manufacturers can offer you: The feature must use hardware already in the car when it was bought or leased, and it must keep working without continual costs or support from the automaker or a third party. Those two things pretty much bring an end to crap like subscription-based heated seats and alike. It also is supposed to cover hands-off driving systems and enhanced navigation. Basically, once the hardware is physically in the car and the manufacturer isn't paying anything for you to use it, they can't then charge you. OEMs will also not be able to charge for software updates that fix safety defects.

Hochul called the practice a "deceptive business model," according to CBS 6 Albany. She also told automakers to keep their hands out of New Yorkers' wallets. It passed through the New York State Senate 57-3.

As with all good things, there are some exceptions. First of all, your lease or payment plan doesn't count as a subscription, so don't even try it, slick.

The law also doesn't apply to navigation updates, infotainment features, satellite radio, in-vehicle WiFi, telematics, roadside assistance or "vehicle-connected services" that rely on cellular data or other networks, according to Motor1. It also exempts software-dependent driver assistance features, like Tesla's Full Self-Driving or GM's Super Cruise.

I know what you're thinking. Didn't I just mention that hands-free driving systems are covered? Well, yes, but subscriptions tied to software and data are likely to survive, while hardware-only features are the real targets. Automakers will almost certainly make the argument that things like FSD and Super Cruise are "connected" or "software-dependent."

One prime example of what this bill aims to get rid of is BMW keeping things like a 360-degree camera behind a paywall. That hardware is already built into the car, and, as we reported, it doesn't require a data connection to operate. It's the same thing with features like high-beam assist and remote start, so I imagine it'll be hard for BMW and other companies with similar schemes to argue that these are "connected." I mean, these are the same people who argue that buyers actually want subscriptions, which is certainly not true.

In any case, violators will face civil penalties of up to $250 per point-of-sale for each violation, though it's not clear exactly how that'll be counted. It's not clear if other states are going to follow suit, but here's hoping.

#5586. 2027 Toyota Sienna Now Gets Up To 37 MPG Combined, And It'll Be Better To Drive Too

If you're still hating on minivans in The Year Of Our Lord 2026, the joke is officially on you. Minivans are exceptionally versatile vehicles that are objectively the best option for most modern SUV buyers, but their lack of a perceived cool factor prevents many buyers from even considering them. We love minivans, and one of the most compelling minivans on the market today is the hybrid-only Toyota Sienna. It's getting even more compelling for 2027, since Toyota has bestowed it with some significant drivetrain improvements including the automaker's sixth-generation hybrid system that uses high efficiency electric motors, a retuned engine, and a more energy efficient lithium-ion battery.

The 2027 Toyota Sienna isn't visibly distinguishable from the 2026 model since no changes were made to its exterior, but minivans have always been about what's beneath the skin, anyway. In addition to the new powertrain, all 2027 Siennas get the larger 12.3-inch infotainment screen and the larger 12.3-inch digital gauge cluster as standard, Toyota's latest suite of active-safety features, an integrated dash cam, a redesigned steering wheel and column, and new LED interior lights.

The Sienna's new hybrid powertrain has been totally recalibrated to operate the 2.5-liter four-cylinder at lower engine speeds when accelerating through the low and mid-range, and all Siennas now have a 1.15-kWh lithium-ion battery and a 166-horsepower electric motor. The powertrain produces a combined 251 horsepower, 6 more horses than last year, and Toyota reckons it's good for a 0.3-second quicker 0-to-60 sprint of 7.5 seconds in front-wheel-drive form. It can still be had with all-wheel drive, which adds a rear-mounted 54-hp electric motor, but its 0-to-60 time is actually a tenth slower than the front-drive model. Toyota now estimates both FWD and AWD Siennas will return 39 mpg in the city, 35 mpg on the highway, and 37 mpg combined, improvements of 3 mpg for FWD and 4 mpg for AWD.

Toyota says the new steering system is more direct and has better on-center feel while reducing steering effort at low speeds for easier parking thanks to a new steering gear box, motor control unit, and torque sensor. The Sienna's brakes have also been reworked with a new electronic control system, offering improved feel and response with less body roll. The latest Toyota Safety Sense 4.0 suite of active safety features has upgraded sensors and hardware for improved obstacle detection capabilities, and in case that all fails, a built-in dash cam comes standard.

Other little changes include the cancellation of the Limited model, now leaving base LE, XLE, Woodland, XSE, and top Platinum trim levels. Toyota has not yet release pricing for the updated 2027 Sienna, but we expect its prices to increase slightly from their current starting point of $42,915.

#5584. How Certain Foods in Adolescence Are Linked to IBD Risk

— Study finds associations with fruit, fish, and salty snacks, but not always in the same direction

Eating certain foods during childhood and adolescence was associated with higher or lower risks of later inflammatory bowel disease (IBD), prospective data suggested.

In pooled analyses of three cohorts in Norway, Denmark, and Sweden, high versus low intake of fruit in childhood was associated with a reduced risk of Crohn's disease (CD; adjusted HR 0.66, 95% CI 0.47-0.94), as was high versus low intake of fish (aHR 0.65, 95% CI 0.44-0.97), reported Annie Guo, PhD, of the University of Gothenburg Institute of Medicine in Sweden, and colleagues in Gut.

On the other hand, high versus low fruit intake in childhood was associated with an increased risk of ulcerative colitis (UC; aHR 1.54, 95% CI 1.07-2.20).

During adolescence, high versus low fruit intake was associated with a reduced risk of CD (aHR 0.57, 95% CI 0.36-0.88), but no significant associations were observed between fruit consumption and UC.

Meanwhile, high versus low intake of salty snacks during adolescence was initially associated with an increased risk of IBD (HR 1.43, 95% CI 1.11-1.86), but after multivariable adjustment, the risk was attenuated (aHR 1.32, 95% CI 0.99-1.77). The adjusted HR for UC with a high versus low intake of salty snacks in adolescence was 1.51 (95% CI 0.97-2.37).

"To our knowledge, this is the first prospective study to examine associations between food-group intake during both childhood and adolescence and the subsequent risk of IBD, CD, and UC," Guo and team wrote. "If our findings are confirmed in other cohorts and by other study designs, promoting healthy dietary habits could be a potential strategy for IBD prevention."

As to what explains the associations between fruit intake and reduced CD risk, the authors noted that the "dietary fiber content of fruit may partly contribute to the observed inverse association between fruit intake and CD risk by promoting a favorable gut microbial environment."

Conversely, they suggested that the association with an increased risk of UC "could potentially be explained by differences in the etiology between the subtypes, since CD has been suggested to be more related to dysbiosis and UC to mucosal immune dysregulation."

As for the link between higher intake of salty snacks and an increased risk of IBD, they pointed out that the salty snack food group across the three cohorts was mostly composed of items high in saturated fats, and saturated fatty acids "have been shown to promote proinflammatory immune responses."

For this study, Guo and colleagues pooled data from three prospective population-based birth cohorts in Norway, Denmark, and Sweden encompassing 111,999 participants born in 1996 through 2009, contributing a total of 1,541,247 person-years of follow-up.

Information on dietary intake in childhood (7-8 years) and adolescence (11-15 years) was obtained from food frequency questionnaires, and included intake of fruit, vegetables, dairy products, meat, fish, sugar-sweetened drinks, and sweet snacks. Questions on dietary intake of salty snacks, such as potato chips and popcorn, were only asked during the teen years.

Diagnoses of any IBD were captured in national patient registries by 2024.

During the observation period, 653 incident cases of IBD were identified, with country-specific incidence rates of 45, 39, and 62 per 100,000 person-years in Norway, Denmark, and Sweden, respectively.

Following dietary assessment in childhood, the median ages at diagnosis were 15.6 years, 18.3 years, and 25.7 years in Norway, Denmark, and Sweden, respectively.

While the authors observed associations between fruit, fish, and salty snack intake and IBD risk, there were no associations with other food groups such as vegetables and meats.

Guo and colleagues acknowledged that their study had limitations, particularly since it was observational, and thus unmeasured and residual confounding could not be excluded.

#5583. Long Before The U.S. Air Force Existed, The Army Used Planes Made By The Wright Brothers

With how ubiquitous commercial air travel is today it's easy to overlook how astonishingly rapid early aircraft development was. There were only 24 years between the maiden flight of the Wright Flyer in 1903 and Charles Lindbergh made the first solo transatlantic flight in 1927. However, military thinkers were even quicker to realize the potential of powered flight on the battlefield. The U.S. Army purchased a Wright Military Flyer in 1909 for use as an observation aircraft. The first heavier-than-air military aircraft served as the spark that eventually led to creation of the U.S. Air Force, but it wasn't an unmitigated success.

The tactical advantage of holding the higher ground had been known for centuries, but aircraft opened up an entirely new dimension. Initially, it was hot air balloons taking to the wild blue yonder. During the Civil War, the Union Army fielded a balloon corps to perform reconnaissance and scout Confederate Army positions. The corps wouldn't last long with then-General Ulysses S. Grant dissolving the unit in August 1863.

The U.S. Army decided to revive this reconnaissance role when opening bidding for a two-seat observation aircraft in 1908. According to the U.S. Department of Defense, the Wright Military Flyer reached an altitude of 400 feet while averaging a speed of 42.5 miles per hour during its final ten-mile qualifying flight in July 1909. The service would purchase a single Wright Military Flyer for $30,000 next month. The sum would be $1.1 million today when adjusted for inflation.

The demonstration campaign that convinced the U.S. Army Signal Corps to open its checkbooks wasn't flawless. Orville Wright would bring a Wright Model A plane to Fort Myer near Arlington National Cemetery in 1908. The younger Wright brother flew dignitaries on brief flights around the Fort's drilling grounds in front of over 2,000 people.

The spectacle was going perfectly until First Lieutenant Thomas Selfridge climbed abroad as a passenger. He had been the first U.S. Army officer to fly a plane just months earlier. According to Air & Space Magazine, Model A suffered a propeller failure while flying at 40 mph at an altitude of at least 100 feet. The twin-propeller wood and canvas biplane tumbled out of control and plummeted to the ground. Wright was seriously injured, but the crash killed Selfridge.

In the crash's aftermath, a distraught Wright believed that he could've landed the plane safely if he had "a few feet more" of altitude. He returned to Fort Myer with an upgraded plane the next year and secured the sale to the Signal Corps. The Wright Military Flyer would never see combat, but it's easy to see how the early aircraft would've been vulnerable to machine gun fire from the ground with such a low maximum altitude. It wouldn't be the U.S. Army's last foray into experimental aircraft. The service would dabble in early drone warfare less than a decade later.

#5582. Tesla Is Not The Only Automaker To Join The Space Race

The Intertubes are practically bursting at the seam with news of another step towards the merger of Tesla and SpaceX, both of which are helmed by on-again, off-again Trump supporter Elon Musk. Buried beneath the avalanche of press is another automotive-aerospace mashup, that being Ford Motor Company’s newly announced investment in the US startup Revolution Space. Evidently the iconic automaker is particularly interested in Revolution’s “unique in-space propulsion technologies.” Hmmm…interesting!

Before you get too excited, Ford is not exactly taking over the space business, nor is it necessarily competing against SpaceX. Revolution specializes in spacecraft, not rocket launches. In addition, Ford is not merging itself with Revolution. On November 23, Ford announced that it is taking up a minority stake in the startup for a yet-to-be-disclosed sum, through its Industrial Platform LLC subsidiary.

Alongside its newly minted minority ownership status, the automaker will apply its manufacturing experience to help kickstart Revolution’s spacecraft platform into volume production.

So, what does Ford have that Revolution wants? Apparently, lots. “With Ford’s manufacturing capabilities, the companies are partnering to industrialize Revolution Space’s next-generation spacecraft at scale, unlocking new dynamic missions and proliferated constellations,” Ford explained in a press statement.

Ford’s Chief Strategy Officer, Marin Gjaja, also chipped in his two cents, emphasizing that “Ford’s strength is building complex products in large volumes, something today’s growing satellite market requires.”

Gjaja also underscored Ford’s immediate interest in the matter, which is to diversify and harden itself against the shifting winds of the global auto market — presumably including the abrupt shift in federal energy policy here in the US, too. As cited by Ford, the “space economy” currently registers about $600 billion globally, which is peanuts compared to the $1.8 trillion anticipated by 2035.

“Increasingly scaled satellite manufacturing is one of the drivers of that growth,” Ford further explains, noting that the firm Future Market Insights estimates the global satellite manufacturing market in the range of $87–$114 billion by 2035, heads and shoulders above the current estimate of $22–$26 billion.

If all goes according to plan, Revolution Space will give the automaker a hand in government and commercial satellite applications. Ford lists communications, Earth observation, navigation, space domain awareness, satellite servicing, and scientific and deep-space missions among the areas of interest. One can  safely assume that military applications are in there somewhere, too.

As for what Ford may expect in return in terms of technology transfer, that remains to be seen. Ford notes that its experience in the aerospace field includes building communications and weather satellites for NASA and the military here in the US, as well as NATO.

However, that work goes back 50-60 years ago. The satellites of today are a different creature. “Revolution Space delivers and operates advanced space systems with superior performance and propulsion technologies designed to achieve the precision control and sustained maneuverability to enhance existing and create new, high-value, dynamic missions,” Ford notes.

In the November 24 announcement, Ford also notes that it has deployed its manufacturing experience to pivot into new fields as called for, citing the bombers it built during World War II and, more recently, the ventilators and protective equipment it supplied during the outset of the COVID-19 pandemic.

Drones could be next on the menu. Coincidentally or not, on November 24, the Wall Street Journal reported that Ford is in the running to help supply drones to the Department of Defense, alongside General Motors among other stakeholders. If realized, the program would call for supplying actuators, engines, sensors, lidar and other elements to drone manufacturers.

“Defense Department leaders have spent months enlisting automakers and their well-established supply chains to help scale up production of everything from missiles to tactical trucks. Now, they want those companies to help ease shortages of unmanned systems, including surveillance and strike drones, that have become crucial in global conflicts,” WSJ elaborated.

Good luck with that. Don’t expect anything of consequence to materialize until Defense Secretary Pete Hesgeth leaves office, either voluntarily or by virtue of impeachment over the hash he has made of the Defense Department, including its equipment as well as its personnel.

Still, the WSJ report did yield an interesting nugget about Ford’s minority stake in Revolution Space. For those without a subscription, the Detroit Free Press kindly offers a summary citing Ford spokesperson David Tovar, who said that Ford has “had discussions with the Pentagon” focusing on its recent investment in Revolution Space, as well as the manufacture of vehicles for the military.

That’s a good question. The Revolution website is rather thin on the technology itself, though it does note that the board is chaired by former NASA administrator Daniel Goldin, with former NASA astronaut Paul W. Richards serving as Chief Engineer and Chief Strategy Officer.

Revolution does drop a few hints about its expectations for the manufacturing hookup with Ford. The company notes that in 2025 it was selected for the DARPA “LASSO” program, with DARPA being short for the Defense Advanced Research Agency.

DARPA describes LASSO as a program aimed at “significantly increasing the United States’ ability to conduct operations in support of space situational awareness by developing both autonomous satellite navigation in stressing orbital conditions and increasing satellite maneuverability through continuous dynamic repositioning based on changing conditions, with the added benefit of creating a proven reserve model for resources relevant to ISRU on Earth’s Moon,” with ISRU being short for in-situ resource utilization.

If you guessed water on the moon is the aim, run right out and buy yourself a cigar. “The program seeks performers to design, test, build, and deliver a spacecraft capable of determining all areas on the Moon that contain greater than 5% water concentration,” DARPA explains.

The LASSO selection comes on the heels of a flurry of activity in 2024, when Revolution reported that it was awarded a slot in the “Advanced Capabilities & Demonstration Missions” program of the Defense Innovation Unit. DIU also deployed its fast-track authority under the “Other Transactions” program to tap Revolution for an award in support of the company’s responsive modular electrospray thrust technology.

In 2024 Revolution Space also reported that it earned an award from the Defense Department’s “Accelerate the Procurement and Fielding of Innovative Technologies” program, and it nailed down an agreement with NASA to collaborate on space technology.

As for the Tesla-SpaceX merger, if you have any thoughts about that drop a note in the comment thread.

#5581. Florida court tosses Tampa General Hospital's suit against Eli Lilly over 340B discounts

Florida court tosses Tampa General Hospital's suit against Eli Lilly over 340B discounts" style="max-width:100%; height:auto; border-radius:8px;" loading="lazy">

A Florida federal court has tossed Tampa General Hospital's lawsuit against Eli Lilly over the 340B drug discount program.

Florida's Middle District court dismissed the case on a motion from the drugmaker on Wednesday. If Tampa General Hospital wants to file an amended complaint, it must do so within 30 days.

The hospital initially sued the pharmaceutical company over a policy it implemented that requires providers to submit claims-level data on pharmacy and medical dispensing if they participate in 340B. Lilly said it would pull back discounts for providers that did not comply with the requirement and has done so for several organizations, drawing ire from the industry.

Tampa General said the decision is "unconscionable and immoral" in the initial complaint, filed in July.

However, the district court argued that Supreme Court precedent holds that organizations like hospitals do not have the private right to sue if they have been charged beyond the threshold, based on a 2011 decision. SCOTUS said that the right to sue was not outlined by Congress, so it is not an avenue available to these organizations.

"The Court made clear that there is simply no private remedy to superintend the pricing and provision of these discounted drugs," per the order.

"To permit this suit would be to encourage piecemeal litigation and defeat the [Supreme] Court’s insistence on maintaining the Congressional plan 'harmoniously and on a uniform, nationwide basis,'" according to the document.

Eli Lilly cheered the decision in a statement.

"Today’s decision is an important step toward a more transparent 340B program," a Lilly spokesperson said. "Lilly’s claims data requirement asks hospitals for basic information—information they already give insurers every day—to prevent unlawful duplicate discounts."

"Decades of HRSA guidance and two federal appeals courts confirm that Lilly can lawfully require this information," the drugmaker said. "More than 3,200 hospitals and clinics already share it with Lilly, and we hope Tampa General Hospital will join them."

Tampa General Hospital has sued Eli Lilly and Company over the drugmaker’s “unconscionable and immoral” rescindment of discounted pricing under the 340B program.

The complaint, filed last week for the U.S. District Court in the Middle District of Florida, is early legal fallout from a policy Lilly implemented earlier this year that requires providers participating in the federal subsidy program to submit claims-level data for all pharmacy and medical dispensations. The company threatened to pull the discounts for those that didn’t comply and made good on its threat for a subset of hospitals last month, sparking outrage among provider groups.

Tampa General, a nearly 1,000-bed disproportionate share hospital, confirmed in the legal complaint that it was among those cut off. It is now spending 25% to 50% more on average for Lilly products, “many of which it must purchase because of Lilly’s status as the market’s only supplier of a given drug product.”

Tampa General’s lawsuit frequently describes Lilly in such terms, framing the drugmaker as holding significant monopoly power and wielding it in violation of the Florida Deceptive and Unfair Trade Practices Act. It also often draws contrast to the 340B program’s intent to support the margins of safety-net hospitals providing care to vulnerable patients, against Lilly’s current status as the largest pharmaceutical company in the world by market cap.

A spokesperson for Lilly, in a statement given to Fierce Healthcare, described the lawsuit as “just another part of the broader hospital campaign to fight transparency and hide the fraud, waste and abuse that plague the 340B program.”

Tampa General asked the court to order an immediate end to Lilly’s policy and to award damages for the higher prices to which the hospital was subjected. It contends Lilly’s alleged failure to comply with 340B pricing requirements and decision to continue marketing its products as 340B-discounted are unfair trade practices under the Florida statute.

“The most valuable pharmaceutical company in the world is engaged in a campaign to transfer dollars earmarked for charitable purposes into its own pocket,” the complaint reads. “The direct and inevitable result of Lilly’s actions is reduced healthcare for poor people. Its actions shock the conscience and are self-evidently unconscionable.”

Tampa General, similar to the hospital associations that have protested Lilly’s policy, contends that the claims data it requires imposes substantial administrative burden on 340B providers. Even “hospitals who have caved to Lilly’s new demands” have effectively had the prices for their covered outpatient drugs increased due to those administrative costs, it wrote in the complaint.

Lilly and other drugmakers who have signaled similar plans have said they need claims data to spot instances in which a drug purchase is improperly granted multiple discounts under overlapping programs. The company has also disputed that the burden of its policy is as substantial as hospitals say.

“The claims data Lilly requires hospitals to share is information they already collect and send to insurers regularly,” the Lilly spokesperson said of the lawsuit’s claims. “Refusing to share that same data with Lilly shows that hospitals are more concerned with hiding rampant abuse in the program than improving its integrity. Lilly will keep working to ensure this program serves vulnerable patients instead of hospitals and their for-profit partners.”

The 340B program has proven to be a magnet for industry and policy conflict, particularly in recent years as its scale has ballooned. The divide is most evident in the ongoing clash over a pilot rebate program that the Trump administration attempted to launch at the top of the year but was forced to restart after the hospital industry’s legal pushback.

340B providers also received a shock this past week when the Centers for Medicare and Medicaid Services floated a significant reduction in its 340B reimbursement to 33.4% below average sales price in its annual proposed rule on hospital outpatient payments.

#5580. CDC Director Draws Doubts From Employees and Ire From Legislators

— "She has a tough job, at a tough time, and a tough boss," one expert says

Three months into her tenure as head of the CDC, Erica Schwartz, MD, MPH, is being investigated by House Democrats, facing a call to resign, and drawing ire from both within and outside the agency.

What's been happening at the nation's top public health agency has alarmed outside health professionals. It also has alienated staff, leaving morale as bad as ever, employees say.

Schwartz, 54, took the helm of the CDC at a very challenging time: the worst U.S. measles epidemic in more than three decades, a large dengue fever outbreak in Florida, and the fastest-growing overseas Ebola outbreak in history.

She reports to Health Secretary Robert F. Kennedy Jr., who is a longtime critic of vaccines and federal public health policy and who played a role in decimating CDC staffing last year.

"She has a tough job, at a tough time, and a tough boss," said Noel Brewer, PhD, a public health professor at the University of North Carolina at Chapel Hill.

In an interview with the Associated Press on Tuesday, Schwartz defended her work, saying she's trying to restore trust in the CDC -- including by changing how measles-associated deaths are being reported. She said some of the criticisms are based on misperceptions and argued she is making progress on her goals.

"I do understand why people have concerns," Schwartz told the AP from her home in Naples, Florida. "Have I made mistakes? Certainly. But I'm here to work on the behalf of the American people."

Some experts say she has made the situation worse. And critics are seizing on the Pan American Health Organization's declaration last week that measles is continuously spreading here, marking the re-establishment of a public health threat that was eliminated in the last century.

"I have no confidence that she will do what it takes to course-correct at the CDC, and she should resign immediately," said Sen. Michael Bennet (D-Colo.).

When President Donald Trump nominated Schwartz to be the next CDC director in April, he called her "a STAR!" on social media.

She holds both medical and law degrees. She was chief medical officer for the U.S. Coast Guard, where she oversaw 41 clinics and 150 sick bays. She served as deputy surgeon general in the U.S. Public Health Service, helping to lead uniformed medical and health professionals posted at the CDC and government health agencies.

Many public health leaders were cautiously optimistic, noting the CDC badly needed steady leadership after enduring more than a year of turmoil and months of being led by interim directors.

Schwartz was the third Trump nominee for CDC director in less than 18 months. The first was a former Florida congressman whose nomination was pulled an hour before a Senate confirmation hearing. The second was Susan Monarez, PhD, who won Senate approval but then was ousted less than a month into the job when Trump administration officials said she was not aligned with the president's agenda.

Under Kennedy, the agency has seen around 30% of its staff leave, mostly through layoffs, resignations, and retirements.

At her nomination hearing, Schwartz pledged "radical transparency" to rebuild public trust in the agency, which she noted declined during the COVID-19 pandemic and which has eroded further under Kennedy.

"I will always stand on science and speak the truth, even in difficult moments," she testified.

Meanwhile, public misperceptions about vaccines have persisted. A recent Annenberg Public Policy Center survey found that about one in six Americans said they believed that "vaccines given to children for diseases like measles, mumps, and rubella cause autism" -- even though dozens of studies have determined there's no causal link. Roughly another one in five said they didn't know whether it was true.

Schwartz started at the Atlanta-based agency in mid-August. A week later, she held her first "all hands" staff meeting, during which she promised to advocate for the agency and said she wouldn't hesitate to disagree with Kennedy -- but only privately.

Some say that's when their optimism for Schwartz began to fade.

"I don't have a clear sense of what is important to her, what are her red lines," one CDC health scientist told the AP. "If we're talking about radical transparency," it is appropriate to know on what issues and what messaging she will not compromise, the scientist added. The employee spoke on condition of anonymity for fear of retribution.

Asked about that assessment, Schwartz said: "I would never do anything unethical or illegal. ... That is a red line."

Despite that transparency pledge, CDC and HHS officials for months would not release Schwartz's salary -- even though it is public information. Schwartz is paid about $375,000 a year, CDC officials finally told the AP this week, above the typical range for previous CDC directors.

So far she also has been less visible than some predecessors. She has not held any news conferences, and has not used her bully pulpit to try to persuade more Americans to get vaccinations -- including for measles, the flu, and COVID-19.

Schwartz told the AP that, concerning measles, she doesn't believe a federal health official on TV will sway most of the unvaccinated.

But with the nation experiencing its worst measles epidemic in decades, many public health and medical leaders say Schwartz should be strongly urging Americans to get vaccinated.

"I hope to see her display independence from the vaccine rhetoric and challenges that have been part of this administration," said Wendy Armstrong, MD, president of the Infectious Diseases Society of America. "I don't think we have seen that yet."

Congressional Democrats Are Launching Investigations

Some Democratic legislators accuse Schwartz and Kennedy of downplaying measles.

Kennedy has challenged health officials' accounts of measles deaths. And the CDC has stopped quickly relaying state reports of measles deaths. It adopted a new policy requiring the review of death certificates, which has resulted in CDC counting only about half the measles-associated deaths that states are reporting.

Schwartz said that was her decision, and stemmed from her desire to have a uniform definition of what counts as a measles death.

Democrats on the House Energy & Commerce Committee have questioned why CDC changed the policy.

"As the crisis worsens, we are concerned that you continue to direct an effort at CDC to downplay the outbreak and artificially reduce the number of deaths that CDC reports," said Reps. Frank Pallone Jr. of New Jersey, Diana DeGette of Colorado, and Yvette Clarke of New York in a statement.

"We are expanding our investigation to examine the threats your actions pose to Americans' health and whether changes to your statutory authority are required," they added.

The statement followed a briefing at which Schwartz was supposed to answer congressional staffers' questions about the measles data. The committee leaders described Schwartz as unresponsive and, at times, misleading.

Schwartz argued that she was "fully transparent. And I am really concerned that they think there is more to the story. There isn't."

CDC Employees See Little Sign of Progress

The AP interviewed eight current CDC workers about Schwartz's tenure. They all spoke on condition of anonymity, saying they feared retribution for speaking frankly about what's going on within the agency.

Some said they never had high expectations. Others said they initially were hopeful but lost confidence, some of them blaming her handling of the measles death count. One said it's still too early to judge her.

Several said they had hoped for more improvements and stability. Announcements about leadership appointees coming, going, and changing titles have continued in a blur, the employees told the AP.

Some mentioned Caroline DeBerry, a former vice president at a conservative think tank and author of independently published novels including one titled "Esther: The Woman Who Didn't Know Her Place."

DeBerry got a job as acting director of communications, starting in August. Employees said she gained notoriety within the agency when all CDC website updates ground to a halt while awaiting her approval.

Asked about it, Schwartz said: "There was a bit of clunkiness that happened one day." She also said DeBerry is on "terminal leave" and her last day will be in December, but did not immediately offer additional detail.

None of the agency veterans interviewed by the AP said they have spoken with Schwartz. But some said they had seen her around the CDC campus accompanied by a group of security guards.

Security has been a sensitive topic at CDC, especially since an August 2025 shooting outside the entrance of CDC's main campus. A Georgia man who had blamed the COVID-19 vaccine for making him depressed and suicidal opened fire, killing a police officer and spraying bullets into 150 windows.

(The slain officer, David Rose, is widely held up as a hero by CDC employees, and some expressed irritation that Schwartz referred to him as "Officer Stone" during a recent health summit appearance.)

Tom Frieden, MD, MPH, who served as CDC director in the Obama administration, said he occasionally had an escort outside the CDC campus after he received death threats. But Schwartz moves with a large security team inside CDC, and agency staffers said they don't recall seeing that before.

"It feels like they're afraid of us," one health scientist said.

Schwartz acknowledged that early in her time at CDC, she walked the halls accompanied by about a half dozen guards at the recommendation of security leadership. The security details have been reduced to one plainclothes guard, she said.

Confusion Over the Publication of Scientific Reports

A directive about research publications shows just how far apart CDC staff feel from the political leadership.

Two CDC scientists told the AP that they were directed in a recent meeting to stop submitting their research to journals run by organizations that have sued Kennedy. The prohibition would cover Pediatrics, one of the most-cited journals for doctors who specialize in children's medical care, and Clinical Infectious Diseases, a highly regarded publication for doctors focused on germs.

The scientists said the directive was clearly stated but not written down in a memo or email.

HHS and CDC officials are adamant that neither Kennedy nor any other HHS or CDC official ever issued such a directive.

At the health summit, Schwartz acknowledged that CDC employees have lost trust in the political appointees who have been put in charge of the agency, "and I'm trying to earn that trust every day."

She talked about work going on to stabilize the agency's leadership and develop a "strategic hiring plan" for 2027.

CDC employees look around and see burned-out colleagues.

"I think everyone is just exhausted," one health scientist said. "There's just a sense that we can't go on like this anymore."

Cross R-Line with 166 kW (226 PS) and 52 kWh battery as a particularly agile flagship model. A luggage compartment volume of 475 litres and a frunk with an additional 25 litres offer plenty of space for travel, leisure and luggage. The 52 kWh battery versions are also available with a ball coupling (optional) for a towing capacity of up to 1,200 kg. With a drawbar load of 75 kilograms, two e-bikes can also be transported, for example.

All equipment specifications and pricing refer to the German market. Details may differ in other countries.

#5578. 5 Vehicles Recommended By Consumer Reports That Also Have Big Black Friday Deals

Let's ground your enthusiasm just a tad here. Consumer Reports (CR) just came out with a list of a dozen 2026 models that currently offer steep Black Friday discounts. The organization is highlighting these deals because every whip on its list also received a strong overall score in Consumer Reports' testing. We have our own take on which vehicles we'd chase hardest, however, so we'll edit CR's edit.

To square up that giant publisher's musts, the vehicles are weighed on factors like how they perform on the site's road tests. And to earn a higher rating, cars that CR recommends need to have performed well in the owner surveys it conducts on hundreds of thousands of cars — the organization wants its readers to consider whether they'd buy the same car again if they had the chance. It also prefers vehicles that get better marks from the Insurance Institute for Highway Safety (IIHS).

Consumer Reports also gives higher ratings for standard active safety tech. This includes automatic emergency braking, blind-spot warning systems, and rear cross-traffic warning systems. And the publication gives props to vehicles that come with camera systems that monitor the driver for attentiveness. The outlet layers on extra credit if the IIHS says a car does well in its front crash protection tests for pedestrians. Consumer Reports' recommended rating also comes down to predicted reliability, again based on reader surveys.

Still, we've got our own take here, and we do a deeper dive on the discount fine print as well as sift their dozen choices down to an odd five cars — just because a car's cheaper doesn't mean you want it in the driveway for the next decade.

The American-made Honda Ridgeline qualifies for some hefty incentives. Consumer Reports found discounts equaling up to $4,953. That's probably due to varied incentives offered by different dealers. For example, you can find $2,250 off via a national incentive directly from Honda, while Carsdirect shows up to $2,750 off and 1.99% financing for 36 months. While a three-year loan means paying more per month than a longer term, 1.99% is far lower than the latest national average rate of 7.3% — meaning you'd be spending far less to pay off your Honda.

Poke around at area dealers, too. We found a deal offering at least $3,045 off of the Ridgeline, which actually exceeds the deal tallied by Consumer Reports, courtesy of Friendly Honda in New York's Hudson Valley. There's another way to save on the Ridgeline after you make the purchase. See, the truck is listed on Consumer Reports' top USA-made cars list, which could unlock some major loan deductions. The way that works is that if you purchase a qualifying American-made vehicle, the IRS gives you a tax credit of up to $10,000 on the interest paid on the loan for that new truck. On the Ridgeline, we arrived at a $5,633 in deductions.

Even without the tax credit, getting several thousand bucks off the Ridgeline makes it extra compelling. We praised its handling in our review of the off-road-focused Honda Ridgeline Trailsport, and we dig its ride quality. Ford's Maverick didn't make Consumer Reports' best cars made in America list, but this Honda wins for that, and we like it for out-towing the Ford, too.

Earlier this year, we put the Hyundai Elantra on our list of the cheapest new cars on the market for 2026. You, dear reader, also voted the Elantra onto your list of the best value cars, too. Consumer Reports found potential deals of up to $2,958 off the Elantra SEL's $23,950 MSRP before destination costs. Our own research shows $2,000 back from Hyundai nationally, while some individual dealers such as Danbury Hyundai in Connecticut have an additional $500 available for recent college graduates and another $500 for first responders.

As for why you like it — you actually prefer the Elantra Hybrid. The bummer there is that there's only a $500 discount off the $26,650 base price of that more fuel-efficient version of the Elantra. We get the thinking: We praised the Hybrid for being quieter and riding better than the non-hybrid, but don't forget that the base SE runs $24,120 with destination, before the $2,000 discount.

Previously, we praised the standard feature set, including an 8.0-inch touchscreen display, the fact that it gets standard wireless Apple CarPlay and Android Auto, and of course, Consumer Reports likes that it also comes baked with a blind-spot warning system, rear cross-traffic warning, and automatic emergency braking with pedestrian detection, as well as lane keeping. Oh, and if you're lanky, the Elantra easily fits taller drivers thanks to 40.6 inches of headroom and 42.3 inches of legroom.

We don't know how Hyundai will price the next-generation Elantra – which feels too nice to be treated like an economy car. That may mean it won't be as affordable come this time next year, so you should probably buy one now, before prices climb.

Consumer Reports found savings of $4,852 off the 2026 Santa Fe. We found that Hyundai is offering a $3,000 national program plus 0% interest loans for up to 60 months, which is a very good way to save a lot of money on the carrying costs of a new car. Look around for other dealer incentives, too. We also found another $2,004 off offered by Ontario Hyundai in the Los Angeles area, knocking nearly $5,000 off the Santa Fe SEL's $40,075 sticker.

We dig the Santa Fe for lots of reasons. Back in 2024, we called it boxy and beautiful from every angle and praised Hyundai for moving from its frumpy curves to a more chiseled bod. Then there's the combination of utility — since it seats up to seven humans — and the fact that the interior is well designed and comfortable. We crowned it the crossover to end all crossovers, too, in part because we really love the dual-clutch transmission and the Santa Fe's superb ride.

If you're instead chasing fuel economy, Hyundai's $3,000 off the 2026 Santa Fe Hybrid is a better price cut than the carmaker's offering for the Elantra Hybrid. At $40,380, the 2026 Santa Fe Hybrid still pulls a big premium over the non-hybrid, but you can qualify for the same 0% interest loan for 60 months.

Audi's A3 and S3 are cars we can't help but drool over. That's partly because they're sports sedans, which is a dying breed. Audi is currently offering up to $3,500 off the sticker price of the A4 and $2,500 off the S3. That nudges the $41,495 A3's sticker (after destination) just beneath $40,000. However, the $53,395 S3 only gets a minor haircut.

We noted back in 2025, when the Audi A3 and S3 underwent their most recent refresh, that that the cars are really petite — about three inches shorter than the Honda Civic. And yet we claimed that the Audi S3 is all the car you'll ever need. Our review of the S3 dovetails with why Consumer Reports likes the lower-tier A3, saying it outperforms peers like the BMW 2 Series Gran Coupe and Mercedes-Benz CLA. A base, 201-horsepower inline-four provides reasonable power in the A3, and while Consumer Reports doesn't love the car's dual-clutch transmission, we found this seven-speed gearbox delightful.

Note that this is a driver's car, first. We praised the driver's seat for its support, and so did Consumer Reports — but they handed a demerit to the squished nature of the rear perches. Did we mention it's smaller than a Honda Civic?

We found $3,000 off a Rogue SV with a national incentive, plus another $950 from Poughkeepsie Nissan in New York. That knocked the total sticker down to $31,235. Then again, at a different dealer in Los Angeles, prices are sliding $3,000 across the table alongside $3,500 from Nissan, which drops the sticker of a Rogue SV from $32,500 down to $26,122. Even if you don't live in L.A., maybe it's worth flying out to buy that Nissan.

As for the Rogue's attributes, they're good, if not revolutionary. The new-for-2027 Rogue Hybrid, however, is a total game-changer thanks to an entirely reinvented hybrid system, where we said its silence speaks volumes, because it operates way more like a pure EV. That said, the 1.5-liter turbocharged inline-three beneath the hood of the 2026 Rogue is still plenty punchy, with 201 hp. Key to that sensation is low-end torque of 225 pound-feet hitting at 2,800 rpm and sticking around through 4,000 rpm.

We noted that the Nissan's inline-3 delivers power more like what you'd expect from a larger V6, but the bonus is you're not pushing all those extra pistons. That means the FWD Rogue gets a combined 32 mpg while the AWD model gets 31 mpg. That's not the 38 mpg Nissan expects from its latest Rogue Hybrid, but if you can nab a 2026 Rogue for $26,000 versus the $37,065 tab on that latest-and-greatest Nissan, we're guessing you'll be just fine with that trade-off.

#5577. Cigna Group Foundation making $31.5M in grants available to community organizations in 10 states

Cigna Group Foundation making $31.5M in grants available to community organizations in 10 states" style="max-width:100%; height:auto; border-radius:8px;" loading="lazy">

Cigna is making $31.5 million in grant funding available over the next three years to nonprofits in 10 states that are aiming to tackle several key community health priorities.

The company said that funds will be made available through its philanthropic arm, the Cigna Group Foundation, with applications open now through Dec. 18. Cigna said in an announcement that it's looking for organizations tackling youth mental health, veteran wellness and the impact of complex health needs on patients and their caregivers.

Cigna said the program seeks to deepen the company's community health strategy to address critical challenges.

The company first launched a new approach to community health and engagement in 2024, and since then has deployed more than $30 million in funding to 190 nonprofit organizations. Projects backed by these grants have reached at least 321,000 people, Cigna said.

"Over the past several years, we’ve seen the tangible impact we can make when we invest in strong nonprofit partners and come together to help communities achieve better health and vitality," said Melissa Skottegaard, board chair of The Cigna Group Foundation, in the announcement. "We’re building on what’s working, bringing together funding, collaboration and the skills of our employee volunteers to address some of the most pressing health challenges facing communities across the street and around the world."

Cigna will allot $10.5 million to each of the three focus areas under the initiative, per the announcement. Youth mental health, for example, has been a leading topic at the company for some time, and the company said it will also continue to build on its partnership with the Boys and Girls Clubs of America in this area.

In that partnership, Cigna has helped build out trauma-informed care services to 90% of clubs, reaching 11,000 employees. The company said that these efforts have supported 1,600 youths, and that its workforce has contributed more than 2,000 hours to local clubs.

Eligible organizations can apply for two-year, $250,000 grants to support programs in the three categories. The funds will be available to groups in Arizona, Connecticut, Florida, Georgia, Illinois, Missouri, New Jersey, Pennsylvania, Tennessee and Texas, per the announcement.

Cigna said its foundation will also continue to support community health through its Health Equity Impact Fund.

"Community organizations can be a powerful source of support for people navigating complex health conditions,” said Matt Perlberg, president of Evernorth Pharmacy & Care Delivery and board member of the Cigna Group Foundation, in the announcement. “By helping patients and caregivers connect with resources and overcome barriers to care, these organizations can provide important support alongside essential medical treatment, helping people navigate their health and care journey.”

#5576. Europe’s Inland Shipping Models Underprice Green Fuels. Market Prices Favor Battery Swapping.

Europe’s inland-shipping models underprice renewable diesel. For a new 110-meter dry-cargo vessel, PLATINA4Action calculates HVO (hydrotreated vegetable oil) propulsion at €1,798/day against €1,964/day for battery swapping. Apply the observed October 2026 Dutch HVO market price, including delivery, and the HVO cost is €2,515/day. Battery swapping is €551/day cheaper. The model’s claim of an HVO cost advantage fails a market-price check.

The gap between the modeled HVO price and observed market prices matters for the investment choices these studies inform. It also exposes a wider pattern. European studies give renewable liquid fuels favorable future prices while expecting marine batteries to remain expensive for much longer than the broader battery market suggests.

The Central Commission for the Navigation of the Rhine (CCNR) commissioned the original transition study behind its 2021 roadmap. Its scenarios influenced subsequent work, including the EU-linked PLATINA4Action and SYNERGETICS projects. Those projects do not all use identical models, but their assumptions and recommendations contribute to a shared policy picture in which several combustion pathways are expected to remain competitive with direct electrification.

In the full TFIE assessment, I converted the October 2026 Dutch Class II HVO wholesale benchmark, added a delivery allowance and expressed the result in 2025 euros. The market-based HVO cost is €2.59/kg. Battery swapping becomes cheaper than HVO in the PLATINA4Action 110-meter vessel comparison at approximately €1.93/kg. At that observed market price, modeled HVO propulsion costs €2,515/day over the model’s 2028–2047 ownership period, against €1,964/day for swapping. The model puts fossil diesel at €1,844/day, leaving swapping about 7% above diesel but 22% below market-priced HVO.

The price difference matters to freight operators. CCNR’s 2026 cost indices allocate fuel 35% of dry-bulk costs, 30% of tanker costs and 28% of container shipping costs. Operators compete for cargo against rivals still burning diesel. Expensive alternative fuels cannot be passed through to customers merely because a transition scenario requires them.

An April 2025 statement from European inland shipping and port organizations called for enough HVO at diesel-competitive prices to supply 1.6 million tons annually by around 2030. That quantity roughly matches the sector’s entire conventional diesel consumption in 2015. It exceeds the EU’s 2025 sustainable aviation fuel supply of 1.1 million tons by about 45%.

HVO and conventional biodiesel compete for oils and fats also used to produce aviation fuel. Aviation has fewer practical routes to electrification, and its European mandates create demand for renewable kerosene. An HVO-centered inland strategy puts barges in direct competition with aircraft for constrained feedstocks, even where barges can draw electricity from a corridor network.

Synthetic fuels present an even sharper problem. SYNERGETICS gives green methanol from biological and synthetic routes a combined low wholesale price of €350/t in its stated-policy case from 2035. That same scenario uses a low green-hydrogen price of €3.50/kg. Producing a ton of synthetic methanol takes about 190 kilograms of hydrogen, costing €665 before carbon dioxide, synthesis, financing and delivery. The hydrogen input alone costs almost twice the assumed low finished-fuel price.

Biomethanol is a different production route. It can be made from agricultural and forestry residues, and I expect it to play a useful role in deep-sea shipping. Grouping biological and synthetic methanol together conceals the economics of each. For inland waterways, the relevant competitor is often electricity delivered directly to the propeller, not fossil methanol replaced with greener methanol.

The battery forecasts lean in the opposite direction. SYNERGETICS places 2030 marine battery storage at €210–€520/kWh, a €365 midpoint. My inland freight electrification report models standardized marine battery containers at €100/kWh in 2030, including the marine safety and container systems, with vessel integration costed separately. A €265/kWh difference adds €1.06 million to a 4 MWh battery installation before operating the vessel. The full fleet-level analysis also prices spare batteries, financing, charging infrastructure and equipment held in reserve.

The studies’ fleet assumptions compound the fuel-price choices. One PLATINA4Action case requires a large Rhine or Danube pusher to go seven days without replenishing energy, excluding intermediate charging or exchange from the test. The SYNERGETICS SYNFEED model starts every new vessel with a Stage V combustion engine, leaving electrification to later retrofits. Those are consequential design choices for a transition modeled through 2050.

Freight demand should be modeled before fuel demand. Mineral oil products, coal and iron ore accounted for 35.7% of Rhine cargo mass in 2025. CCNR’s own underlying economic projections point toward large declines in coal, refining and primary metals, even as construction and trade grow. The amount and composition of freight needing propulsion in 2050 will differ from today’s cargo mix.

The Netherlands has already reduced its 2040 hydrogen demand expectations and identified electricity as the main inland-shipping pathway toward 2050. CCNR is revisiting its transition work. The new analysis should start with the freight that will actually move, calculate route energy and charging or exchange needs, and then test the remaining fuel demand against prices reflecting aviation, deep-sea shipping and industrial competition.

Technology neutrality is a method of comparing alternatives, not an obligation to finance every fuel pathway. With HVO priced against market evidence and batteries costed realistically, the investment case for parallel fuel infrastructure weakens.

The full TFIE Strategy Briefing assessment walks through the CCNR, PLATINA4Action and SYNERGETICS cost assumptions, the HVO crossover and the synthetic-fuel arithmetic. The open inland freight electrification report contains the route, fleet and infrastructure model.

#5575. From Key Cloning To Car Trackers: How 'GTA 6' Is Making Car Theft A Bigger Hassle

Like missions and side quests, stealing cars has always been an integral part of the "Grand Theft Auto" franchise. For "GTA 6," not only have the developers leaned into advanced physics for more realistic car crashes, but car theft itself has evolved into multiple tiers.

While you can still do things the old-fashioned way, like pulling the driver out and taking control of the vehicle, or smashing a window, hotwiring the car, and driving away, those methods won't necessarily work on every car across the fictional state of Leonida. For example, keyless cars can't be hotwired. Even if someone left the doors of their expensive sports car open, you can't simply waltz in and drive away without a key cloner, which you'll only get access to later in the game. Standard and advanced vehicle trackers are also something to keep in mind. (Minor progression spoilers ahead.)

How can you tell if a car is keyless or has a tracker installed? Enter the WAiNK app. Your friend Cal installs it on your phone during a story mission in Chapter 1. The app lets you scan a car to see its make and model and whether it's locked, alarmed, keyless, or fitted with a tracker. Additionally, the app tells you the car's condition, how much it's worth, and what it costs to register should you wish to own it. Keep in mind that although registering stolen cars is how you take ownership of them in "GTA 6," that feature won't be unlocked until Chapter 2, when you meet Moses, who gifts you a slim jim.

To steal cars without breaking the window or setting off the alarm, you'll need to use a slim jim. It works through a lock-picking minigame when you interact with a locked, unoccupied vehicle. While the method is unlikely to attract unwanted attention, it still won't let you start cars with keyless ignition. For that, you'll need to wait until you're about halfway through Chapter 3, at which point you'll get a key cloner. (It's a phone app you download during a story mission.)

The process itself is similar to using the slim jim, and once it's done, the door unlocks, and you drive away — no need to hotwire the car. As mentioned earlier, you can use the WAiNK app before you try to steal a car to see which sort of tracker it has, among other details. Standard trackers are not that big of a deal — the catch is that you can't take the car to a mod shop for any kind of modifications unless you've registered it as your own.

Cars with advanced trackers, on the other hand, alert the police and pinpoint your location — remember, "GTA 6" has flock-like cameras to track your movements. You'll have to drive really fast to stop the tracker from sending the location signal to the cops. If you slow down, the tracker starts beeping, and you only have a few seconds to speed up before it gives away your location. And, unlike in older "GTA" games, cars in "GTA 6" don't have infinite gas, so keep that in mind as well.

#5574. As GLP-1 Use Grows, Patients and Doctors Rethink Thanksgiving Dinner

— Navigating the holiday while taking weight-loss drugs

For many people, Thanksgiving can feel like a competitive eating event. But for Kim Dillard Davis, one of millions of American adults who take prescribed GLP-1 receptor agonists, it is a day like any other.

The 54-year-old Raleigh, North Carolina-based digital creator and wellness coach has lost around 80 pounds since 2023 using GLP-1 drugs. Thanksgiving on a GLP-1 means she doesn't participate in the part of the holiday associated with indulgence.

"I can't sit down and eat the amount of calories that I once could. I'll still be able to sample everything, but I just can't overdo it," said Dillard Davis, who said she plans to be on the drug for life so long as her insurance is covering it.

More and more Americans are using GLP-1 agonists, and as the medications become more popular, they could be changing some Americans' approach to food-centric holidays like Thanksgiving, altering how they eat, what they enjoy about food, and scrambling some social interactions surrounding food.

The jump in popularity comes as President Donald Trump's second administration has made expanding access to GLP-1 drugs part of its health policy, arguing that the drugs, if made more affordable, could help combat rising obesity rates and other related chronic illnesses, eventually lowering broader healthcare costs.

While GLP-1 drugs will likely cause major shifts in ways that are hard to predict, Ashley Gearhardt, PhD, a psychology professor at the University of Michigan in Ann Arbor, suggests the overindulgence associated with Thanksgiving could diminish over time.

"I anticipate that flavors and tastes that have been culturally linked with Thanksgiving will continue to be important. There just may be fewer turkey comas at the end of the day," she said.

Navigating the Holiday While Taking GLP-1 Agonists

Some people who use GLP-1 drugs feel full faster and stay full longer, meaning they tend to eat smaller portions. The drugs have been reported to change how people think about food, reducing cravings and emotional eating but also diminishing the sensory pleasure people get from food. Some users also avoid eating rich or heavy foods, because the drugs can cause nausea or bloating.

Doctors say people who are just starting their weight-loss journey this year with the drugs should be particularly mindful of potential side effects.

"Thanksgiving doesn't have to be off-limits," said John Horlander, MD, a gastroenterologist in Louisville, Kentucky. "The key is moderation, smaller portions, avoiding excessive fatty foods, staying hydrated, and recognizing warning signs."

"A little prevention can go a long way toward keeping someone out of the emergency room over the holiday," he added.

The Trump administration has framed its steps toward increasing access to and bringing down costs for GLP-1 drugs as "a historic opportunity to reverse the obesity epidemic." More than 100 million American adults have obesity, according to the latest CDC data.

In 2025, Trump, a Republican, signed a deal with drugmakers Eli Lilly and Novo Nordisk to expand coverage and reduce prices for the popular weight-loss drugs tirzepatide (Zepbound) and semaglutide (Wegovy).

And coverage of the drugs for obesity was expanded to Medicare patients this year when the federal government began offering a selection of the brand-name medications to certain Medicare and Medicare Advantage beneficiaries for $50 a month through a new trial called Medicare GLP-1 Bridge. The program is scheduled to sunset after Dec. 31, 2027.

A June analysis by healthcare research nonprofit KFF found that 3.8 million Medicare beneficiaries could be eligible for the Medicare GLP-1 Bridge, based on claims data from 2023. The Centers for Medicare & Medicaid Services does not provide projections of how many people will start taking GLP-1 agonists as a result of the Trump administration's actions. The agency wants to expand access to these treatments, said a spokesperson, who was not authorized to speak publicly and talked to the Associated Press on the condition of anonymity.

Those policy changes come as the share of people on GLP-1 medications for weight loss has risen in the past few years -- 11% of U.S. adults said they currently take the drugs for weight-loss purposes, according to a Gallup poll conducted in May and June. The poll found that 15% of Americans report having used the medicine for weight loss, an increase of 9 percentage points from 2 years ago.

That means Americans, even if they aren't taking the drugs, could find more people on the medication who are sitting across from them at the Thanksgiving table. That could change how people talk about eating.

Gearhardt said that dinner guests should be mindful about not pushing loved ones to eat or commenting on appearances.

"We all have a tendency to kind of monitor and police each other's eating behavior," said Gearhardt, who studies food marketing and how addictive processes shape eating behaviors and mental health.

Commenting on how little or how much someone is eating "usually isn't a great relationship strengthener," she said. GLP-1 users might be especially sensitive, she said, because Thanksgiving can evoke feelings of grief over losing the intense reward sensation of highly processed foods.

Dillard Davis advised family members not to ask, "Is that all you're going to eat?"

"Leave that phrase off the table. We've spent our whole lives overeating. Let us eat small portions and be happy," she said.

Babak Orandi, MD, PhD, an obesity medicine specialist at NYU Langone Health in New York City, said, "There's a social aspect of eating, especially around the holidays. We don't want to lose that."

Patients, Orandi said, should focus "a little bit less on weight loss and a little bit more on weight loss maintenance" during the holidays, so as to not lose the enjoyment of eating.

For Dillard Davis, Thanksgiving has changed in other ways. Because of the reduced appetite brought on by the medication and to avoid large quantities of leftovers at home, her family has spent the last 4 years celebrating Thanksgiving at restaurants.

But as someone who has been on a weight-loss journey since she was 8 years old, she has learned to accept the changes.

"We still have to eat," she said. "Just don't worry about it so much."
