#5573. HLTH 2026: Elsevier upgrades ClinicalKey AI with full patient EHR context

HLTH 2026: Elsevier upgrades ClinicalKey AI with full patient EHR context" style="max-width:100%; height:auto; border-radius:8px;" loading="lazy">

Elsevier unveiled this week a major upgrade to ClinicalKey AI, enabling the AI-powered clinical decision support tool to access a patient’s full electronic health record and evaluate treatment plans against the company’s evidence-based medical library.

As AI advances, Elsevier has strategically shifted from a traditional academic publisher—it’s one of the world's largest publishers of scientific, technical and medical content—to an AI-driven analytics information company, incorporating generative and agentic AI into its healthcare and academic research.

ClinicalKey AI is a HIPAA-ready point-of-care decision support tool that draws answers from articles sourced from approximately 1,000 world-leading journals. Elsevier’s upgraded ClinicalKey AI is already deployed at more than 1,200 hospitals, and the company says the solution integrates with all EHR providers. Elsevier recently struck a content agreement with the JAMA Network that brings full-text content and visuals from the JAMA Network Family of Journals to ClinicalKey AI.

ClinicalKey AI was originally co-developed through a strategic partnership between Elsevier and OpenEvidence. An Elsevier spokesperson said the company's collaboration with OpenEvidence on ClinicalKey AI has concluded.

The company has rapidly expanded its AI capabilities and features, including ClinicalKey Nursing AI, an AI-powered tool built specifically for nurses and nursing workflows. Elsevier has also developed specialized AI assistants, such as LeapSpace, an AI-assisted workspace for researchers that’s built on 15+ million peer-reviewed articles and book content and 100 million Scopus records, according to the company.

Three months ago, the company rolled out its "Ask AI" feature across more than 1,500 U.S. hospital sites using its flagship ClinicalKey platform. With the generative AI feature, clinicians can ask conversational clinical questions and receive direct, cited answers drawn from evidence-based sources, including premium journals and content from leading medical organizations, the company said. Unlike general-purpose chatbots, Ask AI does not pull from the open web and does not store, process or retain patient-identifiable information, according to the company.

In a move to develop context-aware AI, the company acquired Wellsheet in June. The health technology company aggregates and synthesizes patient chart data—including notes, labs, imaging and medication orders—from EHRs to provide relevant clinical intelligence at the bedside.

“The problem we’re trying to solve here is that physicians are challenged by so many things when they’re taking care of patients; top among them is patient complexity. Patients have more and more conditions, and they’re taking more and more medications. It can be hard to really understand the full picture and determine how to proceed,” Dr. Claudine Lott, physician executive for clinical solutions at Elsevier, told Fierce Healthcare during the HLTH conference this week.

“Our original ClinicalKey AI tool has been a place where the clinician can go, and they can ask a question, and it searches this curated database of evidence-based content we’ve given it and gives them an answer. But there was a bit of a missing piece in that it wasn’t capturing that patient context. If you wanted to get an answer, that’s really tailored to a patient, it was limited to what you can type in,” she said.

She added, “Wellsheet has 10 years of experience with looking at the entirety of the patient chart and helping to surface that to the clinician.”

There is a growing market for AI-powered clinical decision support products, but Elsevier contends its advantage lies in its depth of patient context and its large, curated content base, with a wholly owned, large proprietary library of peer-reviewed STM literature, including content from millions of articles, journals and books.

San Juan Regional Medical Center in Farmington, New Mexico, is using ClinicalKey AI’s new patient context functionality to provide quality care with fewer resources. With smaller clinical and quality teams than large academic health systems, and in a state where nearly 40% of residents rely on Medicaid, the medical center, which serves the Four Corners area, has found this support especially valuable, according to Carlo Hallak, M.D., physician executive, information services, at San Juan Regional Medical Center.

A non-profit, community-owned acute care hospital and Level III trauma center, San Juan Regional Medical Center operates outpatient and specialty clinics alongside its main hospital campus, San Juan Regional Rehabilitation Hospital and San Juan Health Partners clinics.

Hallak emphasized that Elsevier is a primary source of medical knowledge rather than a “wrapper” for other AI platforms, and that was a key distinction for the organization, he said. And ClinicalKey AI is designed for the entire care team. “The hospital is run by nurses, mid-level providers, other caregiver teams,” he told Fierce Healthcare at the HLTH conference.

The medical center aims to move clinicians to enterprise LLMs integrated with the electronic health record that can provide the full patient context, Hallak said.

"We're already seeing how ClinicalKey AI can be instrumental at the bedside and help improve care for our patients. As a rural healthcare center, we are proud to say that by using ClinicalKey AI to check each patient’s care against the same evidence base that major health systems rely on, we’re able to deliver the standard of care patients expect in any major city or leading academic center," Hallak said.

He added, "ClinicalKey AI reads the whole chart, checks it against the evidence and shows exactly where each finding comes from. That support is invaluable for a hospital like ours, and we’re already seeing strong adoption of ClinicalKey AI among our clinicians."

Hallak cited an example of a physician who used ClinicalKey AI to correctly identify viral meningitis, preventing an aggressive and unnecessary treatment course for bacterial meningitis.

ClinicalKey AI supports the physician’s judgment rather than replacing it, helping the clinician get to relevant evidence faster, he said, noting that, in the case of the viral meningitis patient, the physician’s clinical judgment was critical in recognizing that something was unusual and then consulting colleagues. The AI tool then helped accelerate the next step by quickly surfacing information the clinician otherwise would have found by searching the medical literature.

Physicians also use the tool during weekly complex case discussions to quickly review trending medications, images and test results in one summarized sheet, he noted.

“It’s going to expedite a lot of the work they do when it comes to these complex cases,” he said.

The AI solution can quickly summarize a complex patient history at the point of care, and this can help care teams in the emergency department understand a frequent ED patient, Hallak said.

One patient has visited their emergency department 95 times in less than a year, in addition to making numerous visits to neighboring health systems, Hallak said. When that patient arrives, clinicians who aren't familiar with him would traditionally have to dig through the chart to understand his history. With Elsevier's ClinicalKey AI tool, they can ask for a summary of the patient's recent visits and care and get that information within seconds, he noted.

Because patients who frequently visit hospital EDs typically receive care at multiple organizations, San Juan Regional Medical Center is also working with other local health systems on interoperability efforts so clinicians can get a more complete picture of what happened during the patient's visits across different facilities.

As a rural hospital with staffing constraints, technology is essential to filling workforce gaps and supporting care delivery, Hallak contends.

"We don't have enough physicians, enough nurses, enough transporters, enough caregivers. We will never be able to have enough of those," he said.

Hallak envisions ClinicalKey AI will be particularly valuable for discharging patients from the hospital faster.

Generative AI tools can help speed up patient discharges by identifying barriers, assessing patients’ care needs and providing relevant information from both structured and unstructured data. Because the platform is designed for the broader care team, case managers and physicians can access the same information, while push notifications can alert clinicians when test results are available so they can act quickly, Hallak said.

"From an operational perspective, it allows for this care team coordination of care, and also from a physician perspective, it provides the context whenever there's something hanging and they're trying to understand more what's happening with the patient['s] discharge. You could be a hospitalist, and you've been off shift for like seven days, and then you come to the hospital and now the patient is on their tenth day, but you inherit that discharge. Going through, reading every progress note for every single day, reading every consult for every subspecialty that looked at this patient usually would take two to three days to actually get the patient out of the building safely," he said.

Rather than manually compiling information for a discharge summary, clinicians can use ClinicalKey AI to aggregate relevant information from across the patient’s record, trace it back to its source and review the AI-generated summary before signing off on the discharge, according to Hallak.

"It is a game changer, in my opinion. One of our hospitals said it used to take me three days to discharge a patient; now it takes me less than three hours," he said.

Going forward, the system expects to measure operational outcomes such as length of stay, safe discharge and multidisciplinary-rounds use, according to Hallak. The medical center also plans to evaluate clinicians' use of the tool and how it changes care decisions.

The medical center has also deployed other AI tools such as Oracle Health's clinical AI agent for ambient documentation.

"These things are crucial. We're hoping to bring in a clinical AI agent for nurses as well to help the whole care team be able to eliminate the noise, the friction that typically EHRs or IT would bring to the conversation," Hallak said.

The medical center is taking a risk-based approach to AI governance, using source verification and peer review for AI-generated summaries while applying greater scrutiny to tools that are predictive or suggestive, Hallak noted. That oversight involves clinical experts as well as compliance, legal and cybersecurity teams along with traditional vendor security and product-vetting requirements.

AI governance should become stricter as the technology moves from summarizing information to recommending or taking actions, he said.

Elsevier designed ClinicalKey AI with security and trust in mind, including HIPAA compliance, citations that show where information comes from and safeguards that keep the tool from prescribing what clinicians should do. Instead, it surfaces relevant information to help clinicians make their own decisions, Lott said.

"Health systems tell us two things stand in the way of adopting AI: tools that don’t work with their existing EHR partners, and concerns about security, compliance and responsible use," Omry Bigger, president of Clinical Solutions at Elsevier, said. "ClinicalKey AI was designed to address both. As an EHR-supported and HIPAA-compliant ready solution, ClinicalKey AI integrates seamlessly across health systems to provide clinicians with trusted evidence and patient context that gives them tailored clinical insights for each patient at the point of care."

#5571. Tesla Wants You To Watch Its 'Reality View' Instead Of Paying Attention To Reality

There's little Tesla loves more than a dumb little party trick, and it often includes new gimmick functionality within major updates to its software. The recent release of V15, software for Tesla's latest in-car computers, adds another trick: A new camera angle that shows the car from a third-person view, like in a video game. Tesla calls it Reality View, and out of everything a Tesla can do, it sure is one of the things.

The Tesla faithful call the software update V15, after the FSD version included, but the full package is known as Update 2026.38.6.11 — not exactly a name that rolls off the tongue. The update did include functional changes to FSD, mostly relating to the computer vision wall Tesla keeps beating its head against, but it seems the engineers had a thought while working on all those cameras: Why not use them to simulate a third-person view, like a video game?

FSD 15 can see chain link fence and power lines! 🤯🙌🏽 @Tesla @TeslaSI @elonmusk pic.twitter.com/m3fn7c4obA

Initially, after looking at both Tesla's demo footage and footage from real owners, I figured the car was using SpaceXAI's models to generate a third-person view based on the input from the cameras — a method that would explain some of the odd glitches and scale issues present in some of the clips as AI hallucinations. According to Elon Musk's tweets, that's exactly what's going on — rather than an actual display of what the car's cameras and FSD software see, Tesla owners are now treated to an AI-generated picture of what's going on around them.

The ability to mesh multiple cameras into a single point of view like this is neat, and the underlying tech is impressive, though it's unclear how much compute is needed to generate these images, and what kind of load that puts on the car. What these images aren't, though, are replacements for the actual data visualization that FSD used to supply. Even Tesla owners are admitting confusion between objects the car does and doesn't see with the new display. Most Tesla party tricks are some ratio of neat to useless, but this one genuinely appears to make the user experience of FSD worse.

#5570. Consumers believe AI will be key in making healthcare simpler: survey

Consumers believe AI will be key in making healthcare simpler: survey" style="max-width:100%; height:auto; border-radius:8px;" loading="lazy">

Americans want a healthcare experience that's simpler and easier to understand, and artificial intelligence may be the key to getting there, a new survey shows.

The poll, conducted by the Harris Poll and commissioned by Elevance Health, found that 82% of American patients view at least some part of healthcare information as a "foreign language," and close to half (45%) said they made a healthcare decision they later regretted due to a lack of understanding.

The survey was conducted among 2,0001 adults in mid-July, and 74% said they have encountered a key barrier, such as understanding their health insurance or a medical bill. And only a quarter (27%) said they completely understand their own health coverage.

That said, most (79%) of those surveyed said they believe AI can help to improve the healthcare experience overall.

Ratnakar Lavu, chief digital information officer at Elevance Health, told Fierce in an email that because the tech has the ability to quickly surface and parse key data about a member or health need, it can serve as a central piece of the puzzle in making a more personalized experience.

"AI-enabled tools give us the ability to create a unique member context," he said. "Because of this digital technology, we can now truly 'know the person' and proactively work with them to help them manage their healthcare needs."

For example, 82% of those surveyed said they would like to receive answers about healthcare as quickly as they can use an app to order a food delivery, and 83% would like to easily access recommendations that pull from their health coverage, individual health needs and financial situation.

Seventy-two percent said they would trust AI for accuracy on at least one type of information, such as an explanation of benefits, cost estimates or details on medication.

However, while many Americans believe AI can play a leading role in driving a better health experience, just 20% said they currently use the tech regularly as a source of information. And while 72% said they trusted the tech to surface key health information, 33% said they would trust an AI platform to translate their insurance benefits, and 30% said they trust the tech to estimate care costs.

Just 13% said they would trust AI to make diagnoses.

Lavu said that healthcare organizations looking to roll out new tools need to avoid simply embedding AI into programs for the sake of it, and be transparent about both the use case as well as the role of the human in the loop.

The study also found that women were less likely than men to trust AI, and younger generations were more likely to use it as a source of healthcare information than older groups.

"We have to implement AI at the speed of trust, which means being thoughtful about where AI adds value, not just delivering technology for technology’s sake," he said. "Trust is also earned when AI-enabled programs truly make things easier for the consumer. "

For example, Elevance Health has deployed an AI-powered program to support utilization management reviews and help drive faster approvals where possible, though AI is not used to make claims denials. It is also leaning on AI for more proactive member engagement initiatives through its digital channels, identifying individuals that would benefit from extra support or who may face gaps in care.

"We start by listening to members and identifying where healthcare is creating the most confusion or friction," Lavu said. "From there, we determine the right solution, which may or may not involve AI. When AI is the right tool, it should fit naturally into familiar interactions and make it easier for members to get an answer or take the next step."

#5569. Antibiotic Paste Approved to Prevent Surgical Site Infections

— Bacfenssi becomes the first approved product for local application following colorectal surgery

The FDA approved doxycycline hyclate (Bacfenssi) to reduce the risk of surgical site infections (SSIs) in adults and kids undergoing colorectal surgery, the agency announced on Tuesday.

Sold as a powder to be reconstituted into an extended-release paste, the tetracycline product is indicated specifically for surgeries involving incisions larger than 20 cm, as efficacy for smaller incisions was not established in trials. Pediatric patients must weigh at least 45 kg, according to the agency.

It marks the first approved option for preventing SSIs that is applied locally, the FDA said. Before closing the incision, the paste is applied directly to the fascia and tissues of the abdominal wall along the incision.

Support for the approval came primarily from SHIELD II, a multinational double-blind phase III trial, which showed that adding the doxycycline hyclate paste to standard of care reduced the risk for SSIs, mortality, or surgical reintervention by 40% over the month after surgery. In the investigational arm, that composite outcome occurred in 10.9% of patients compared with 18.1% in the control arm (P=0.004).

When looking at SSIs specifically, a key secondary endpoint, the product reduced the risk by 60%, with infections recorded in 3.8% of the doxycycline hyclate arm and 9.5% of controls (P=0.001).

Commonly occurring following colorectal surgeries, SSIs "can lead to prolonged hospital stays, reoperation, and significant patient morbidity, including wound breakdown and systemic infection," the FDA noted. "Despite the administration of systemic antibacterial drugs prior to colorectal surgery to reduce SSIs, the infection rate remains substantial, underscoring the need for additional preventive strategies."

Adverse events observed in trials of doxycycline hyclate were mostly mild and included incision site reactions, procedural pain, anemia, and wound dehiscence.

Labeling for the antibiotic paste includes potential risks for tooth discoloration and inhibition of bone growth if used in pregnancy and in children, Clostridioides difficile infection, potential for microbial overgrowth, along with known class effects of tetracyclines -- photosensitivity, fixed drug eruption, pseudotumor cerebri, and anti-anabolic actions. Drug interactions are possible with concomitant use of anticoagulants.

Doxycycline hyclate can cause fetal harm, the FDA noted, and "breastfeeding is not recommended for 40 days after administration."

Launch of the product is expected in early 2027.

#5568. At $7,999, Is This 2017 Alfa Romeo Giulia A Beautiful Bargain?

The 1970s band Nazareth warned us that Love Hurts. Today's Nice Price or No Dice Alfa Romeo has looks that could make you fall in love, but also a brand reputation for tempestuousness that might cause pain — financial and otherwise. Let's find out if this one is priced to make that pain hurt so good.

Legend has it that, though the execs at Land Rover tasked company stylist David Bache with designing the bodywork of the in-development Range Rover, they were so taken with the styling of the engineering mules that, with minor tweaks, that became the truck's first and final design.

The 1987 Range Rover Classic we looked at on Tuesday differed from that original design with a modified grille and, more importantly, four doors, as the earlier two-door model was never offered for sale in the U.S. Our Range Rover was claimed to have been restored, and while there remained some boogers under its desk, it did look to be in serviceable overall shape. That wasn't enough to get the vast majority of you on board with the idea of spending the $28,850 the seller asked for its sale. Ultimately, that price fell in a 93% 'No Dice' loss.

I'd like to get one thing out of the way up front: I find today's 2017 Alfa Romeo Giulia incredibly good-looking, with no bad angles from which to view it. I mention that because my prose describing the car might be a bit biased by the siren's song of the car's Helen-of-Troy-build-a-big-horse-beat-up-the-Trojans level of good looks.

Of course, like Helen's legendary beauty and the battles fought by suitors for the opportunity to awkwardly sit at her knee and stare stupidly into her limpid pool eyes, owning an Alfa Romeo of any ilk could lead to devastation.

Alfa had to get the Giulia's looks just right, a necessity that went so far as to delay the car's launch. Fiat boss Sergio Marchionne kept rejecting designs Alfa's internal Centro Stile Alfa Romeo designers offered during the car's development, claiming they weren't good-looking enough. Why Alfa didn't go the route of having a design house like Pininfarina pen the car is a mystery, although traditionally Alfa handled saloon styling in-house while farming out coupes to such esteemed external designers, so there's that.

Regardless, Alfa got the Giulia's looks right, and didn't screw up the car's underpinnings either. Rolling on a then-new RWD platform developed with oversight by Ferrari engineer Philippe Krief, the Giulia has the goods to compete with the likes of BMW's 3 Series and Audi A4 when the roads get bendy. It's a testament to how good the basic chassis is that it can perform competently with this base four-cylinder model and, with notable tweaks, also with the monster 500-horsepower V6 Quadrifoglio.

Our car has the 1,995cc DOHC MultiAir four under the hood. With variable valve timing and an intercooled turbocharger, that makes an astounding 280 hp and 306 pound-feet of torque. Matched to that engine is a ZF 8HP50 8-speed automatic transmission that powers the rear wheels. While AWD was offered on this model, this car's RWD layout is simpler to maintain and lighter, offering better performance and fuel economy.

According to the ad, this Giulia has 75,000 miles on the clock and, remarkable for any Alfa, especially a first-year model that may still be suffering teething pains, it is claimed to suffer no issues whatsoever. Did I mention it's also really good-looking?

Having established that this is a very attractive car, it's also in very good shape. The pictures in the ad support the seller's claim that it's "well kept," as the Silverstone Gray Metallic paint appears to have no dents, and the darker gray factory alloys show no evidence of curb rash.

This being an entry-level model and with the ad's limited description, the seating surfaces are likely vinyl rather than leather. Some wood on the console warms up the space in an otherwise Germanic, all-black (even the Alfa badge) interior.

That's not to say that the car is parsimoniously equipped, as it has all the basic comfort and convenience features one might expect on a reasonably modern car. Those include power windows, locks, seats and mirrors, dual-zone climate control with eyeball vents for the back seats, and a center stack screen for infotainment and the backup camera display. There's also cruise control, auto headlamps... You name it, it's likely got it.

Per the seller, the car presents in excellent condition, has a clean title, and drives perfectly. What more could one want?

Well, I'm sure one thing we could all ask for is a car that's free and can navigate to a leprechaun's pot of gold when asked. There are certain things we can't have, no matter how much we want them.

This car isn't free, but its $7,999 asking price is a recent reduction from $8,500, and it makes one wonder: just how scared are people about buying a decade-old, out-of-warranty Alfa that even that slightly higher price seemingly couldn't get a nibble?

Naturally, you'll suss it out in the comments and, ultimately, judge it in the vote. With that in mind, what's your take on this base-but-far-from-basic Giulia and that new, lower $7,999 asking price? Is that a handsome deal on this beauty of a car? Or, even at that modest amount, could buying this Alfa get ugly real fast?

#5567. Joint Letter on the Revision of the CO₂ Emission Standards for Cars & Vans

Counting combustion vehicles as clean would blur the market signal and bring back the very uncertainty that carmakers, suppliers, and investors cannot afford. Manufacturers could meet their targets without selling a single electric car.

Dear Climate and Environment Ministers of the European Union,

We, a broad coalition representing non-governmental organizations, think tanks, energy providers, renewable and cleantech industries, and the e-mobility sector, are writing to express our concern over the current direction of EU discussions on the revision of the cars and vans CO₂ emission standards regulation.

Road transport remains the primary driver of Europe’s oil dependency. This structural vulnerability costs the EU hundreds of billions of euros annually, weakens our economy, and leaves citizens exposed to geopolitical shocks. The second fossil fuel crisis in just four years has already added more than €50 billion to Europe’s energy bill, hitting households and businesses hard.

Our continued reliance on fossil fuels also accelerates the climate crisis. This summer, Europeans paid the price as successive heatwaves brought record temperatures, devastating wildfires, and severe droughts across the continent. Transport remains the EU’s biggest emitter, with cars and vans alone accounting for 16% of total CO₂ emissions.

Yet while the market is moving rapidly, political discussions are falling out of touch with reality.

Europe already has a mature, increasingly affordable and readily available structural solution in electric vehicles – which are also the most effective way to shield consumers from volatile fuel prices. Electric vehicles are scaling fast, reaching a record 27% market share in the third quarter of 2026 in the EU.

Instead of building on this momentum, discussions in the Council on the car CO₂ regulation threaten to slow the EV trajectory and inject uncertainty for companies and consumers. Most concerning is the proposal to create a new category for combustion cars running on e-fuels and biofuels, so-called “vehicles running exclusively on eligible fuels” (VEEFs), and count them as zero-emission vehicles.

Too much attention is being paid to combustion-based technologies, distracting from the electric future. Cars running on these fuels still burn fuel in a combustion engine; treating them on par with battery electric vehicles would create a false and unfair equivalence. Under this proposal, manufacturers could meet their targets without selling a single electric car.

Counting combustion vehicles as clean would blur the market signal and bring back the very uncertainty that carmakers, suppliers, and investors cannot afford. It would penalize businesses that are scaling up today and jeopardize the €200 billion already invested in the battery-electric transition. Europe cannot compete globally on electric cars if targets are weakened and undue credit is given to e-fuels, biofuels and hybrids.

We must reset this narrative on vehicle electrification. Europe’s clean industry needs investment certainty and regulatory stability. We therefore call on Climate and Environment Ministers to safeguard the car and van CO₂ targets adopted just three years ago, reject any weakening of the 2030 and 2035 ambition, and remove the “vehicles running exclusively on eligible fuels” (VEEFs) category.

Now is the time for political leadership. Member States have the chance to ensure this fossil fuel crisis is Europe’s last, and to act decisively to build a clean, sovereign, and affordable mobility system supported by a strong EU clean industry.

Download the letter to see the full list of signatories.

Transport & Environment’s (T&E) vision is a zero-emission mobility system that is affordable and has minimal impacts on our health, climate and environment. Created over 30 years ago, we have shaped some of Europe’s most important environmental laws.
